Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for biomedical question answering, but plausible incorrect answers remain a safety risk in clinical settings. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can ground responses in medical literature, while confidence-based adaptive RAG retrieves only when the model appears uncertain. We evaluate this routing strategy on three biomedical QA benchmarks of increasing difficulty and compare models across a broad range of parameter scales. The results reveal a paradox: confidence is well calibrated (AUROC 0.78--0.89) on standard medical exam questions where retrieval adds little value (+2.4\% to -1.4\%), yet becomes \emph{near-random} (AUROC 0.506) on specialist-level questions where retrieval helps most (+11.2\%). Five uncertainty signals---first-token probability, margin, entropy, consistency-based sampling, and verbalized confidence---show the same failure pattern. Five post-hoc calibration correction strategies, including density-ratio reweighting and rank-preserving transformation, do not recover a useful routing signal on hard questions (best AUROC: 0.507). Cross-model comparison shows that larger parameter scale and higher base accuracy do not guarantee reliable confidence-based routing under specialist-domain difficulty shift. These findings challenge the premise of confidence-based adaptive RAG: in high-stakes biomedical settings, defaulting to always-retrieve is safer and more accurate than confidence-based routing when calibration on the target domain is unknown.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Face Verification (FV) systems have exhibited remarkable performance in verification tasks and have consequently garnered extensive adoption across various applications, from identity duplication to authentication in mobile payments. However, the surge in popularity of face verification has raised concerns about potential vulnerabilities in the face of adversarial attacks. These concerns originate from the fact that advanced FV systems, which rely on deep neural networks, have recently demonstrated susceptibility to crafted input samples known as adversarial examples. Although imperceptible to human observers, adversarial examples can deceive deep neural networks during the testing and deployment phases. These vulnerabilities raised significant concerns about the deployment of deep neural networks in safety-critical contexts, prompting extensive investigations into adversarial attacks and corresponding defense strategies. This comprehensive survey provides a comprehensive overview of recent advances in deep face verification, encompassing a broad spectrum of topics such as algorithmic designs, database utilization, protocols, and application scenarios. Furthermore, we conduct an in-depth examination of state-of-the-art algorithms to generate adversarial examples and the defense mechanisms devised to mitigate such adversarial threats.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) on a reward that can be checked automatically has become a common last step in the training of text-to-image models. A simpler way to get more correct images is best-of-N: draw N images from the base model and keep the one that a picker prefers. We ask how many base model samples one RL sample is worth, and call this number the exchange rate. We measure it for Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium and its public Flow-GRPO checkpoint on 398 new prompts in the style of GenEval, the benchmark behind the RL reward, with human labels on every prompt and two automatic judges other than the reward. The answer depends on the prompt. Four fifths of the prompts do not ask for a position (for example, one object left of another). On these, the base model catches up: best-of-16 with an off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM) as the picker is about as good as one RL image. A person who looks through up to 3 base images finds a correct one as often as one RL image is correct. On the prompts that ask for a position, RL is far ahead: a person finds a correct image among 8 base images for only 52% of the prompts, against 92% for one RL image. Over all prompts, our primary test, which we fixed in advance, finds best-of-16 with the VLM picker 9.3 points behind one RL image when both automatic judges must pass an image. People see a smaller gap, 5.5 and 6.5 points in two checks. FLUX.1-dev shows a similar gap in the same test. RL also solves 20 prompts that the base model misses in 128 tries, and 1,024 tries recover half of them. The automatic judges are wrong in opposite directions: a detector-based judge like the reward, as used in our primary test, reports a larger gap than people see, and VLM judges report little or none. Comparisons of RL with best-of-N should therefore check the selected images by hand.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Alongside superposition, polysemanticity is one of the primary obstacles to achieving interpretable deep neural networks. While these phenomena are typically intrinsically linked, the Quadratic Programming Enhanced Model (QPM) naturally decouples them. By representing classes with a sparse binary assignment of very few features, QPM prevents superposition by design on its final features but still exhibits polysemanticity. However, measuring polysemanticity is an open problem. This work proposes a utility-focused approach to measuring polysemanticity by quantifying the decrease in interference-induced activation errors, which yields the practical utility of tighter prediction sets using Conformal Prediction (CP). We explicitly disentangle the polysemantic features of QPM into monosemantic concepts to create the Monosemantic QPM (MonoQPM). Because its features are disentangled, MonoQPM acts as a significantly more efficient Conformal Predictor. Additionally, we introduce CUBCars, a dataset providing ground truth information about shared concepts. Using this and other datasets, we demonstrate that polysemanticity emerges in QPM across all tested architectures, but is effectively alleviated by MonoQPM. For instance, MonoQPM guarantees 88% coverage using Adaptive Prediction Sets on ImageNet with just 66% of the frozen QPM's set size.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Safety-critical interactions are rare in recorded driving data, leaving end-to-end driving planners with limited exposure to evasive behaviour during training. This lack of critical supervision can lead to unsafe behaviour and, in rare instances, collisions. To address this gap, we introduce CriticalScenes, a reconstruction-based framework that transforms ordinary recorded driving scenes into photorealistic safety-critical interactions with corresponding collision-avoidance supervision. Across 53 reconstructed source scenes, 979 of 1,060 attempted interactions yield feasible threat trajectories (92.4\%), while the inserted threat actors remain detectable in more than 95\% of non-occluded observations within 5--35\,m. Experiments with two structurally different end-to-end planners show that the generated supervision improves critical-scenario planning, while closed-loop transfer varies across planner architectures. SparseDrive achieves a 46.5\% relative reduction in collision rate on an independent photorealistic closed-loop benchmark, whereas UniAD shows only a modest closed-loop improvement despite substantial open-loop gains. The results further highlight the importance of closed-loop evaluation, as improvements in open-loop trajectory prediction do not necessarily translate into equivalent safety gains.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Discrete visual tokens encode images for reconstruction and serve as training targets for autoregressive image generators. Vector-quantized tokenizers typically assign each encoder feature its nearest code independently. The decoder uses these codes jointly, so a sequence of individually nearest codes can reconstruct an image less faithfully than an alternative assignment. A local model of the reconstruction loss yields a closest-vector problem with a decoder-dependent target and metric. Reconstruction-only search improves PSNR across the frozen tokenizers we test. Different code sequences can achieve similar reconstruction quality, leaving room to favour more predictable ones.
  We introduce RecaP (Reconstruction and Predictability), which selects codes for a frozen tokenizer by balancing how faithfully they reconstruct the image with how likely their sequence is under a pretrained autoregressive prior. On ImageNet, LlamaGen GPT-B trained on RecaP tokens achieves lower early-checkpoint FID against training images than GPT-B trained on nearest-neighbour tokens. The difference is smaller against held-out images. On an ImageNet subset, the prior improves generation at comparable reconstruction FID. Our code is available at: https://github.com/gmum/recap

</details>

Abstract (英文): Attention over long sequences is a major computational bottleneck in video generation, which can be alleviated through sparsity and low-bit quantization. We propose JetDiffusion, a video generation system built on trainable Video Sparse Quantized Attention (VSQA), with custom inference kernels that combine native NVFP4 computation and kernel fusion. At 90% sparsity, VSQA accelerates attention by 8-29x compared with FlashAttention on different hardwares. To further accelerate video generation, we combine VSQA with NVFP4 linear quantization and step distillation. Although each technique can individually preserve generation quality, jointly adapting to all three remains challenging, particularly under aggressive NVFP4 quantization. Through empirical studies, we develop an effective staged training recipe that enables stable adaptation to quantization, sparsity, and few-step denoising. On Wan2.1-14B, the resulting model achieves 273x end-to-end video generation speedup on the RTX 5090 while maintaining comparable video quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We introduce CoSMo, a compact spectral space of body motion in video, in which each motion is a single code that can be compared and edited without training, transferred to a new subject, interpolated, composed, scaled in intensity and detail, or sampled anew. Our encoder expresses how a clip's DINOv2 features change over time in a fixed temporal basis, the discrete cosine transform (DCT), and compresses this change into 384 numbers. Because each frequency band is the same cosine pattern in every code, codes are aligned in time, ordered from coarse to fine, and can be blended or added without matching frames. What stays constant over a clip cancels, and keeping fewer frequencies leaves less of the performer and scene. The encoder is trained on real videos and on synthetic motions of several characters, and lightweight adapters let a frozen image-to-video model render any code with any subject. On unseen videos, our code ranks the same motion on other bodies higher than DisMo, a per-frame representation, while its nearest neighbors show the same performer about 40% less often with a seven times more compact code. In generation, without per-motion training, CoSMo transfers motion more faithfully than FlexiAct and a text-prompt baseline, at least as faithfully as DisMo on automatic metrics and clearly better between humanoid bodies, and its blends keep much more of both source motions than DisMo's. Users rank CoSMo first nearly twice as often as DisMo for motion transfer (59% vs. 30%), place it top-two 94% of the time, and prefer it in 76% of the interpolation comparisons.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Model merging combines multiple task-specific expert models into a single model. However, merging preserves expert capabilities unevenly across tasks. This imbalance is particularly pronounced in large-scale settings, where some tasks can suffer severe capability losses even when average accuracy remains high. To address this imbalance, we introduce Recovery-aware Task Weighting (RaTW), a model merging framework that assigns larger weights to tasks whose expert capabilities are poorly retained by an initial merged model. We quantify these recovery opportunities as the fraction of a task's labeled validation samples that its expert predicts correctly but the initial merged model gets wrong, and use the resulting task weights to re-merge the experts. To evaluate merging at large scale, we construct CLIPMerge-100, a 100-task benchmark with task-specific experts on CLIP ViT-L/14 and ViT-B/32. Across eleven merging methods with 20, 50, and 100 tasks on two CLIP backbones, RaTW improves both mean accuracy and tail20 (the mean expert-normalized accuracy of the 20% worst-retained tasks) in all 33 ViT-L/14 settings and 32 of 33 ViT-B/32 settings. We further introduce ReForge++, which combines RaTW with iterative model refinement and outperforms all evaluated baselines, including a matched-budget control, in both mean accuracy and tail20 across all settings.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training has become a standard way to improve text-to-image flow models: Flow-GRPO lifts the GenEval score of SD3.5-M from 0.63 to 0.95, a gain read as the model having learned compositional generation. We ask how much of that gain the base model's own samples already contained, by measuring pass@k over shared noise seeds, one skill at a time, before and after RL, on prompts never used in training. The answer differs by skill. On four of GenEval's six skills, where 32 base samples already solve almost every prompt, the gain is sharpening: one post-trained sample is worth 4 to 10 base samples, and pass@32 barely moves. On spatial relations it is unlocking: pass@32 rises from 0.69 to 1.00, 128 base samples do not match one post-trained sample, and prompts the base model never solved in 32 samples are solved 93% of the time, as often as those it solved. The benchmark average, 31 base samples per post-trained sample, describes no skill. Relations carry 35% of the gain; of the other 65%, selecting among 8 base samples with the judge behind the reward recovers nine tenths. We propose an account of when each occurs: within a prompt, a group-relative update re-weights only the outcomes its samples contain, and a flow policy cannot widen the noise that produces them; across prompts, shared parameters let successes on some prompts repair a deficit common to many. The same anatomy applies to a text-rendering checkpoint. The account's predictions are tested with a checkpoint trained without KL, within-prompt diversity, and controlled training that holds object classes out, where sharpening comes first and transfers, and unlocking follows, on practised objects before unseen ones.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Attention injection offers a remarkably simple route to training-free style transfer: content queries retrieve style keys and values inside a pretrained diffusion model. We show that this operation also creates an overlooked failure mode. Position signals that are useful within one image become a spurious shared coordinate system across two spatially unrelated images, causing content tokens to favor style tokens at similar coordinates. This cross-image positional leakage appears in both convolutional U-Nets and diffusion transformers (DiTs), despite their different position mechanisms. Removing the positional shortcut exposes a second problem: unconstrained global retrieval lets related content tokens select incompatible style regions, producing fragmented appearance. We present StyleID++, a training-free framework that addresses the two problems jointly. Position-Decoupled Retrieval reduces coordinate-dependent matching through circular padding in U-Nets and injection-only RoPE removal in DiTs, while preserving native position modeling elsewhere. Affinity-Coherent Refinement then regularizes style assignments with the content self-attention graph, encouraging structurally related tokens to retrieve compatible style evidence. We quantify the leakage with a coordinate--centroid correlation and a permutation test on the style reference. The permutation error can be driven to zero in both backbones, which identifies the position mechanism as the carrier of the leakage, and StyleID++ obtains the best ArtFID of all methods we compare.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative priors can recover anatomical detail from incomplete medical measurements, but may erase the very lesions that imaging aims to detect, even when the measurements contain evidence of them. We introduce selective anchoring that protects reliably measured image components from prior-driven changes, while allowing expressive generative models to reconstruct unresolved structure. Acquisition uncertainty in the protected components is either added deterministically to the downstream test or carried by the ensemble through stochastic perturbations of the readings. We implement this principle as a wrapper for pretrained diffusion and flow samplers and as AnchoredFM, a fast constrained flow model. Under a linear-Gaussian measurement model, the protected coefficients are unbiased in expectation and achieve nominal interval coverage across repeated acquisitions, independently of the learned prior. Across sparse-view CT, accelerated brain MRI, and photoacoustic tomography, selective anchoring improves lesion contrast preservation and uncertainty reliability, and can reduce reconstruction error within lesions. For tumor detection in accelerated MRI, with a healthy reference estimated from the measurements alone, deterministic anchoring raises mean AUROC across eight samplers from 78.4% to 84.6% and lesion sensitivity at 0.25 false-positive marks per slice from 38.8% to 47.5%, with gains in all four predefined tumor-size groups. At thresholds frozen on healthy validation data, mean sensitivity rises from 20.2% to 44.1% (37.6% with stochastic anchoring). These findings show how expressive generative priors can reconstruct unresolved anatomy without healing away the abnormalities that the measurements support.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A surgeon first finds what is wrong, then fixes only that part. We use this idea for video grounding, where video-language models often find the right moment in a long video but write its time wrong. In Temporal Weight Surgery (TWS), a general-purpose LLM reads the model's weights and activations, finds where the error arises and repairs it there, without gradients. Separate sessions find the same cause, and this diagnosis of where and why the model fails is our main contribution. Three GPT-6 Astra sessions trace a dropped digit in TimeLens-8B to one MLP layer, and the largest edit gains 7.9 mIoU on 1,215 unseen questions and 18.3 on videos of 30 minutes or more. Two Claude Opus 5.5 sessions trace minutes written as seconds in Qwen3-VL-8B to the separator and gain 2.9 and 1.3. Before any edit, its answers often have the right minute and the wrong separator, and fixing only the separator lifts their mIoU from 13.1 to 19.2. Every improving edit beats a random edit of its size, and the edited models run as fast as before.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Class-agnostic counting requires the user to specify what to count, usually with a class name or a few annotated boxes. We propose a richer text-based specification: a structured layout that names the target, the region that holds it, the distractors to ignore. A prompt upsampler writes this layout from the image and the requested category alone, and an image-editing diffusion model, adapted into a single-step counter, turns it into a density map. Because counting datasets pair each image with a single valid density map, a counter trained on it learns to ignore the layout. We therefore train on synthetic counterfactuals in which one image admits several valid density maps, so that the layout cannot be ignored. On FSC-147 we improve on the previous zero-shot state of the art and we set the best reported result on LOOKALIKES while being told the least about each image. A single photograph can be counted by category, by region, or by granularity without retraining.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In differential diagnosis (DDx), a clinician explains a diagnosis by the few findings that separate it from the alternatives. Medical image classifiers cannot yet explain themselves this way. Existing explanations are either faithful but spread over hundreds of concepts, or compact but leave part of each decision unexplained. We present DDxBasis, which explains a classifier on a frozen foundation encoder as a differential diagnosis in three steps. First, a concept bottleneck reads all patch tokens at every encoder layer using gated, second-order slot attention. A concept basis then regroups the concepts into two-pole axes, each a differential between groups of diagnoses. Finally, we read a differential diagnosis from each axis through the typical presentation of its poles, the pertinent findings of the case, and counterfactual edits of borderline cases. Across five medical datasets, DDxBasis is the most accurate frozen-encoder model and close to the best published domain-specific results. Because every concept adds its own evidence, its reproducible concept basis splits each decision exactly, at no cost in accuracy. Without ordered stage or concept labels, the axes recover cell lineages and rare subtypes, agree with dermatologists' structure annotations, and expose planted and real shortcuts.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Understanding ultra-long videos requires retrieving sparse, query-relevant evidence from extensive video histories. Existing similarity-based retrieval methods are efficient, but they encode the question and the video content independently and thus often miss evidence whose relevance requires reasoning over both. Generative large language models (LLMs) can perform such reasoning, but they typically generate text over multiple rounds of search, which makes each query slow and costly. In this work, we introduce DeLoVi (Decision Models for Long Video), a framework that leverages pretrained decision models to retrieve evidence from ultra-long videos. Like an LLM, a decision model reads the question together with the candidate evidence, so it can reason over both. Unlike an LLM, it directly outputs a relevance score without generating text, which makes it fast and inexpensive. We first present DeLoVi-S, a simple method that generates captions for short video clips and uses a decision model to rank those captions by their relevance to a question. A generative LLM then answers the question using the top-ranked captions. Despite its simplicity, DeLoVi-S achieves state-of-the-art performance on three ultra-long video understanding benchmarks, EgoLifeQA, EgoMemReason, and MM-Lifelong, and generalizes effectively to long-video understanding benchmarks, VideoMME-L and LVBench. However, DeLoVi-S scores every caption for every question, so its cost grows linearly with video length. To reduce this cost, we propose an automated policy evolution framework that iteratively refines the memory construction and retrieval policies around a fixed pretrained decision model. The resulting method, DeLoVi-E, maintains comparable performance while reducing input tokens by 34x on average compared with DeLoVi-S. It also runs over 3x faster than the best prior method on EgoLifeQA while achieving higher accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mangroves are dynamic coastal ecosystems that contribute to carbon storage, coastal protection and biodiversity conservation, but are vulnerable to climate change. Very-high-resolution imagery such as Pléiades enables detailed mangrove mapping but it is costly and have a low revisit rate. Large-scale monitoring requires free and regularly available data such as Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 but their spatial resolution poses challenges for detailed mangrove mapping. In this work, we benchmark deep learning approaches for mangrove mapping from Sentinel-1 radar and Sentinel-2 optical imagery using reference maps derived from very-high-resolution Pléiades imagery. We compare four segmentation configurations based on CROMA and BigEarthNet-pretrained ResNet50 representations with different segmentation decoders. We construct a mangrove mapping dataset covering French Guiana, Suriname, and Amapá. CROMA + UPerNet achieves the best performance, with an F1-score of 0.9218 and an IoU of 0.8549. These results highlight the potential of pretrained multimodal Earth observation representations and open Sentinel data to support mangrove resilience through scalable monitoring.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Expressive style transfer must reproduce a painting's palette, brushwork, and characteristic motifs while retaining the content image's structure. Attention injection is effective in U-Net-based diffusion models, but adapting it to FLUX often transfers color without capturing the style's distinctive brushwork. Our analysis across backbones links this gap to how injected features are integrated across neighboring spatial locations. We introduce MotifFlow, a training-free, self-contained framework that combines style injection with style-alignment guidance in FLUX. Injection supplies style features during generation but does not explicitly encourage the model's native attention output to align with them. We therefore use the discrepancy between native and injected attention outputs to guide latent-space updates, encouraging the evolving image to develop the style's distinctive brushwork and motifs. Style encoding, guidance, and synthesis all use the same frozen backbone, without auxiliary image encoders or trained adapters. A single strength parameter controls the transition from color transfer to pronounced brushwork and motifs, with additional support for regional stylization and style mixing. Experiments on 1,000 content-style pairs show that MotifFlow better matches the local textures of the style image than baseline methods and produces more consistent stylization than injection alone. The same design extends to FLUX.2.

Abstract (英文): Recent dynamic Gaussian splatting methods encode temporal change, either as smooth functions of time or as constrained updates between frames. We observe that their reconstruction quality degrades significantly under fast motion. In challenging dance sequences, their PSNR drops by 1.1-4.0 dB on high-motion frames compared to low-motion frames. To overcome this limitation, we propose VersionGS, a method that directly encodes per-frame Gaussian states and uses motion estimation only to guide reconstruction. Within each group of frames, unchanged Gaussians keep their current versions in a shared dictionary, while modified Gaussians are appended as new versions. To maximize version reuse, we introduce selective sequential construction, which refines the Gaussians selected by optical flow, photograph differences or rendering errors and leaves the others unchanged. The dictionary is then pruned, fine-tuned with simulated quantization and encoded as images. Adjusting the pruning ratio trades off storage and quality without rebuilding the dictionary. On the fast dance sequences of DanceNet3D, VersionGS improves foreground PSNR by 2.7 dB over the best baseline, and its PSNR decreases by less than 0.3 dB on the highest-motion frames. On N3DV and Technicolor, it requires 6.0x and 5.7x less storage than TAD-GS, the strongest baseline overall, while achieving even higher PSNR. Finally, playback is fast and requires no motion model or neural network, only a 3DGS rasterizer.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video diffusion and world models generate plausible videos but do not reliably match the requested camera direction and travel distance. We present DreamTour, a training-free framework that generates camera tours from a single image with more accurate control over camera motion. DreamTour first uses a frozen image inpainting prior to extend the photograph beyond its original field of view while preserving the input pixels. Depth-based rendering of this extended scene along the requested camera path produces approximate target views that serve as spatial guidance. We introduce Layout Steering, which aligns early video latents with these views through decaying low-frequency corrections, leaving later denoising to refine appearance and dynamics. For longer tours, we autoregressively generate the video segments, repeating guidance construction from each segment's final frame to maintain camera control across the full trajectory. DreamTour improves directional accuracy and travel calibration on VBench while maintaining competitive video quality. On WorldScore, it strengthens camera control and scene consistency across chained movements. Together, these results demonstrate that training-free spatial guidance enables more accurate and consistent camera control in pretrained video diffusion and world models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present ArtTet, a method that reconstructs an articulated object from multi-view observations of two articulation states and outputs one watertight mesh per rigid part together with the joints that move them. Mesh splatting gives explicit triangles, but it treats the object as one scene. It extracts a single global surface and cannot keep the triangles of different parts apart; the mesh it exports is layered, self-intersecting, and full of holes. ArtTet follows a divide-and-conquer strategy instead. We first divide the object into rigid parts with dynamic- and geometric-constrained articulation and ownership inference. A multibody registration of the two states recovers the parts and their screw joints. Rigid-body rules on attachment, contact, and swept motion then correct joint types and part labels without any part annotation. We then divide space into part cells with an articulated tetrahedral partition, and conquer each cell with its own differentiable signed distance field on a shared tetrahedral grid. In these partitioned marching tetrahedra the fields are mutually exclusive by construction, so the extracted part meshes are closed and never overlap. All part meshes are optimized jointly from the images of both states through the recovered joints. On Articulate-100, ArtTet gives the most accurate axis directions, part motions, and part geometry over all objects among methods that use no part masks, and its part meshes have virtually no open boundary edges. We also show reconstructions of real captures from the SplArt dataset.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In this paper, we synchronize multiple videos of the same dynamic event through object relationships. Driven by dynamic reconstruction and novel view synthesis, many recent synchronization methods rely on pixel-level evidence, such as point correspondences across views or the photometric losses of a reconstructed scene. In dynamic scenes, however, fast motion and occlusions make the moving regions that carry much of the timing information hard to match across views. We thus propose to bring back the traditional wisdom that objects are reliable cues for global reasoning about cameras. Measured in 3D, the relationships between objects, such as the distance between two people, are stable across all views up to scale; they can serve as cues for alignment. To compare these relationships across cameras, we only need the identity of each object in every view, as the cues are relational. We obtain these identities by giving a vision-language model a few frames of every camera and prompting it to identify the objects in the scene. We then segment and track each identified object through every video with an open-vocabulary segmentor. To obtain the 3D locations of objects, we use a pretrained depth estimator. Where depth or segmentation fails, we complement these 3D cues with 2D motion from point tracks. Finally, we synchronize the cameras by finding the temporal offsets at which the cues of different cameras agree best, first for each camera pair and then for all cameras jointly with robust optimization. We evaluate our method on four standard benchmarks, outperforming the state of the art.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Degraded images make vision-language models answer wrongly. Using synthetic paired blur, noise, and low light, we trace which clean image states can still repair those answers from the vision encoder through the language model. In four encoder models, 12 to 23 ranked tokens recover half of the full-patch effect, whereas random orders require four to fifteen times as many; encoder-free Gemma needs 22.2 versus 53.1. Full-block repair expires at a model-specific language-model crossing. The matched reverse-direction intervention is algebraically identical to text-token restoration, so its gap from image restoration measures a redundancy band rather than an independent predictor. A clean-image gray-ablation crossing independently predicts held-out repair with 1.53-layer error, against 3.65 for shared depth. Gemma MMBench has the only large attention-to-repair point gap; Gemma GQA does not. Restormer provides a non-oracle consistency check on two models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Four-dimensional cone-beam computed tomography (4D-CBCT) images the anatomy of a breathing patient, but sees each breathing phase from only a few angles. Breathing, however, is a smooth motion that repeats; it has a phase. Existing methods exploit this either by reconstructing the anatomy as a function of the breathing phase, often through bins, or by estimating smooth dynamic motions in time. Yet none of these grounds one smooth motion in the phase. We thus propose to share projections across breathing phases with splines. Specifically, we represent the 4D volume with dynamic Gaussians whose motion uses B-splines in space and time. We ground the motion state in the respiratory phase, which we read from well-studied conventional surrogates. Projections at the same phase in different breathing cycles then constrain the same anatomy and motion. On simulated lung scans, our method, BreathSplat, outperforms recent dynamic methods in reconstruction and motion accuracy, including those given the ground-truth phase. We further show qualitatively, on two real patient scans, that our method provides clearer reconstructions than the state of the art.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An RGB-D camera that films an object being moved, often from one viewpoint, sees one side of it and how its parts move. We present ArtKit, which takes such a video as input to create an articulated model with complete parts, a kinematic tree, and typed joints. Our central idea is generating the hidden geometry and verifying it against what the camera sees. A generative part prior proposes complete, part-decomposed geometry based on the first frame. Point tracks, grouped into rigid trajectories, split and merge the generated parts into links. We then fit revolute and prismatic joints, with a visibility-aware energy ensuring that the articulated model explains the depth observed across the video. A rule checks against the video to choose between synchronous group fits and per-frame chain fits, so that one pipeline serves a diverse range of articulated objects, such as household items and robotic hands. Finally, we regenerate each link of the chosen fit as one part and keep it wherever it agrees better with the observations. We evaluate on Articulate-100 and on HArt, a new benchmark of 60 PartNet-Mobility objects and 8 robotic hands and grippers rendered as RGB-D videos with ground-truth parts and joints. ArtKit reduces the axis and part motion errors of the strongest baseline by more than half on the objects, as well as the axis error on the hands. ArtKit also runs on real recordings and generated videos.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We propose the Audio-Visual Query Transformer (AVQFormer), a highly efficient architecture built upon a 2.7B parameter LLM featuring a fixed-length $K=16$ semantic alignment module, stabilized via Adaptive Self-Critical Sequence Training (ASCST) with Entropy Regularization. Multimodal Large Language Models (LLMs) often rely on global audio representations to compensate for inefficient, frame-based visual encoders. While our Phase 1 prompted evaluations demonstrate that the Multimodal Base Model achieves strong deep semantic alignment (scoring 0.889 on BERTScore F1), optimizing this architecture for legacy metrics reveals a performance gap. We explore this gap as a hypothesis we term temporal alignment friction: a conflict where global audio representations disrupt the chronological sequence of visual frames, leading to penalties under visually-focused evaluation metrics. Consequently, by stripping away the audio, our Video-Only ablation conforms more closely to this rigid syntax, achieving 63.0 CIDEr. While trailing larger models such as Qwen2.5-VL (67.4 CIDEr), this establishes a strong baseline for sub-3B parameter LLM-based models. This indicates that CIDEr, as applied to datasets such as MSR-VTT, may exhibit a bias toward visually grounded syntax, limiting its ability to fully capture multimodal semantic richness.

Abstract (英文): Gradient-based transferable adversarial attacks achieve state-of-the-art attack success rates against black-box vision-language models (VLMs). However, they require hundreds of costly optimization steps for each image pair. One possible reason is that these attacks typically start from random perturbations that contain no information about the source or target image. In this work, we study whether amortized adversarial generation can provide better initializations for gradient-based attacks. By learning across many image pairs, amortization allows a generator to produce adversarial perturbations for new inputs without optimizing each perturbation from scratch. We introduce SPARK, a lightweight looped perturbation generator conditioned on both the source and target images. SPARK repeatedly applies the same network to refine a perturbation, producing effective adversarial attacks in a few forward passes without requiring gradients through surrogate vision encoders at inference time. We show that these perturbations provide effective warm starts for existing gradient-based attacks, substantially improving attack success rates while reducing the number of optimization steps required. These improvements generalize across different attack methods, VLMs, and unseen image categories. SPARK also produces effective standalone attacks at a fraction of the computational cost of gradient-based optimization. Code and model weights are available at https://github.com/Patchwork53/SPARK.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Confidence-gated co-training assumes that pseudo-label errors are uncertain and concentrated at organ boundaries. We argue that for pancreas CT, where the target organ is a single connected structure and detached fragments are anatomically implausible, the dominant failures arise at three levels: where the organ is, what its boundary should look like, and how a correct boundary is penalized. Anatomical Rectification relabels implausible fragments of a teacher prediction as confident background, without adding any loss or parameter; an audit confirms that its rejections are overwhelmingly detached hallucinations and that thin, tail-like slices are not eroded. Boundary Grafting transplants labelled surface regions with their annotation into unlabeled volumes, replacing unreliable boundary pseudo-labels with ground truth. The residual error is coherent surface displacement, weakly penalized by permutation-invariant Dice and cross-entropy losses. Homotopy-Annealed Hausdorff (HAH) supervision anneals a distance-weighted penalty from an annotation-anchored objective that is convex in the prediction to a Hausdorff surrogate, admitting the prediction-dependent distance term only after the predicted surface becomes reliable. On NIH pancreas CT, under the standard semi-supervised split, the full method reaches 84.60% Dice and 4.97 voxels 95HD, improving all four metrics over compared methods; on MSD Task07, 78.90% Dice and 4.19 voxels 95HD. We further contribute PanTS-SSL, a semi-supervised evaluation protocol derived from the public 9,901-case PanTS corpus, with a curated 1,015-volume main pool, labelled tiers of 50, 100 and 250 volumes, an 80-case tumor track, shift probes for acquisition and anatomy change, and a fixed 901-volume test set. On it, the method exceeds the co-training baseline at 100 labels, scales to 85.29% Dice at 250 labels, and reaches 85.50% Dice at 100 labels with HAH.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual reasoning requires models to identify relevant visual evidence and use it to derive correct answers. Outcomebased reinforcement learning (RL) faces two key challenges in this regime: it yields no learning signal when perceptual errors cause all sampled responses to fail, and it broadcasts a uniform advantage across every token within a response. Conversely, while visual self-distillation offers dense guidance from evidence-centered image crops, student learning from token distributions alone often fails to elicit correct final answers. Motivated by an audit of training rollouts that exposes these complementary failure modes, we introduce ViCARD (Visual Credit Assignment with RewardGated Distillation), which integrates per-token visual supervision with verified correctness rewards. For each rollout group, we leverage a teacher with its input image cropped to the relevant visual area, and use this to provide per-token supervision to the student while also valuing correctness. If all sampled rollouts fail, ViCARD samples an additional crop-teacher answer and activates on-policy distillation only if that answer is correct. Together, these mechanisms provide fine-grained visual credit within informative reward groups and a learning signal for otherwise uninformative all-fail groups. Extensive comparisons across model scales demonstrate that ViCARD improves fine-grained visual reasoning performance over RL and distillation baselines, while retaining broad visual reasoning capabilities.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reliable deployment of Vision Language Models (VLMs) requires that a model's expressed confidence remain grounded in what it can actually see, and that it recognize when the visual evidence is too ambiguous to commit to a confident response. Standard post-training uses outcome reward models (ORMs) that supervise only the final decision, rewarding a confident answer regardless of whether the image supports one, while existing methods either tend to detect uncertainty post-hoc or assume an objective verdict always exists. We instead split multimodal reasoning into two stages: perception, where the model must factually describe the scene, and calibration, where it must judge whether that evidence is sufficient to commit to a conclusion. We introduce HumblePRM, a lightweight process reward model (PRM) that scores each stage independently, rewarding agreement between evidence and expressed confidence rather than outcome correctness alone. Training with HumblePRM increases abstention due to uncertainty on ambiguous inputs considerably while leaving accuracy on clear images roughly unchanged. We also find that the improvement transfers to Gemma 4 E4B, a larger, popular edge model. Our findings show that process reward supervision is a promising route to VLMs with better calibrated abstention, ensuring more reliability rather than unpredictable behavior.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reference-based super-resolution (RefSR) aims to recover missing details from high-quality references. However, existing methods face two fundamental challenges. First, perceptual plausibility does not guarantee content fidelity. Existing methods often produce visually convincing details without faithfully recovering reference-specific information, such as text, codes, and identity cues. Second, faithful reference reading does not guarantee reliable information transfer. Increasing reference utilization also exposes the restoration process to misleading content, as global semantic relevance does not necessarily imply local correspondence consistency. To address these challenges, we propose PreciseRef, a reference-aware generative framework for faithful and reliable RefSR. At its core, Asymmetric Twin-Attention (ATA) allows target and reference images to participate in a unified attention space while the reference keeps its own shape. By letting their image tokens interact directly through joint attention, ATA facilitates fine-grained reference information retrieval without explicit image alignment. To ensure reliable information transfer, we introduce Dual-Scale Reference Gating (DRG), which combines Semantic Relevance Gating (SRG) to filter globally irrelevant reference context and Bidirectional Consistency Gating (BCG) to suppress unreliable local correspondences. Furthermore, we establish VeriRef, a benchmark evaluating exact reference-dependent content recovery and robustness to mismatched references. Extensive experiments show that our method achieves 85.6% character accuracy and 76.3% exact code decoding, compared with 17.4% and 0% for existing methods, while effectively suppressing erroneous reference transfer. It also achieves state-of-the-art perceptual quality on CUFED5 and WR-SR.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Coding agents can generate runnable games from natural-language descriptions. However, a runnable game does not guarantee an excellent gameplay experience for players. In our view, a playful game should provide 1) reliable core functionality, 2) appealing visual design and assets, and 3) rich gameplay that supports exploration and progression. How agents can improve these aspects of playfulness remains underexplored. In our work, we propose Play2Evolve, a recursive game development framework with three stages: Initialization, Refinement, and Evolution. Using playfulness as feedback, agents playtest the game and diagnose issues in its functionality, visual presentation, and gameplay. Their feedback guides bug fixes and asset improvements. It also helps agents propose new gameplay and aesthetic goals for continued game evolution. To enrich game materials, we combine a video generation model with code to create appealing static and dynamic assets and integrate them into interactive gameplay. Finally, Play2Evolve achieves state-of-the-art performance among the evaluated methods, scoring 60.05 on GameCraft-Bench compared with 50.81 after Game Initialization and 52.46 for Play2Code. Human overall-quality ratings reach 3.67, compared with 2.38 and 2.43, respectively. Our code will be released at https://github.com/showlab/Play2Evolve.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scenes inside a scattering medium contain solid surfaces, such as the seabed or rocks, and a semi-transparent medium, such as water, fog, or smoke. Recent Gaussian-based methods produce realistic images of scenes in scattering media, but traces of the medium can remain visible in surface-only renders. Neural fields separate surfaces and medium more accurately, but they are slow to train and difficult to edit. Both approaches evaluate the medium only along camera rays, so its effects along reflected and refracted rays are not modeled. These limitations prevent reliable surface recovery, independent editing of surfaces and the medium, and consistent rendering of inserted objects. We present FoamSurf, a hybrid representation that models surfaces with planar Gaussian surfels and the medium with space-filling Voronoi cells. A single differentiable ray tracer composites both along arbitrary rays, while training priors keep them separate without segmentation masks. Across real underwater scenes, synthetic scenes, and dense fog, FoamSurf achieves image quality on par with the state of the art and the most accurate surface-medium separation among the compared methods. The recovered components can be removed, edited, or stylized independently, and the same reconstruction supports inserted mesh objects with attenuation and scattering along both direct and reflected or refracted rays. Together, these capabilities enable scene restoration, editing, and composition within a single reconstructed model.
  Project page: https://grzegorzwilczynski.com/foamsurf/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Digital signers, also known as signing avatars, convey sign language through animated virtual humans, where visual fidelity and linguistic intelligibility hinge primarily on the hands. When one hand crosses over the other, two failure modes dominate. First, monocular expressive-human tracking suffers from ordinal depth ambiguity: either hand can satisfy the same 2D landmarks, and the baseline tracker orders wrists at chance level on 365 annotated overlapping frames from How2Sign. Second, hand appearance remains blurred in neural rendering even after geometry is corrected. We propose OrdiHand-GS to address these errors separately. Relative depth from a video depth estimator and apparent hand size vote, with confidence weighting and abstention, for a front-back relation, which enters expressive-human tracking as a hinge on median hand-surface depth. A source-conditioned residual head then refines the local positions of hand-associated UV Gaussians with the avatar backbone and renderer frozen. We instantiate the framework with GUAVA. Against matched unconstrained refinement, wrist-order accuracy rises from 32.9% to 77.3% at a cost of 0.39 pixels of reprojection error, while a random-sign placebo gives no balanced-accuracy gain. A 2x2 factorial evaluation attributes most of the interaction-rendering improvement to tracking-stage refinement, with a smaller additional benefit from the residual.

</details>

We present SplatWAM (Splatting World Action Model), a generative world model that learns action-conditioned dynamics directly over compact 3D Gaussian primitives. A tokenizer SplatWAM-T first encodes a variable number of unposed views into a fixed budget of Gaussian tokens. A dynamics model SplatWAM-D then predicts future Gaussian states in a single forward pass per action, using log-Euclidean covariance coordinates and differentiable rendering supervision to facilitate learning in an explicit 3D space. Each predicted state can be rasterized from arbitrary viewpoints, enforcing a shared 3D representation across views by construction. On OGBench Scene, SplatWAM renders novel views 12x faster than video world models (~80 FPS on an RTX 5090) with up to 2.0 dB higher PSNR. We further introduce SplatWAM-P, a lightweight flow-matching policy head jointly trained with the dynamics model. Across 8 task suites, model-free Gaussian-token policies outperform pixel-based counterparts by up to 24.8 percentage points. SplatWAM-P improves over Latent Particle World Model (LPWM) by 12 percentage points and matches HIQL without a learned value function. On LIBERO, our ~1B model outperforms WorldVLA (~7B) and the robot-pretrained OpenVLA (~7B), while matching OpenVLA-OFT when both are trained without large-scale robot pretraining. Code and checkpoints will be released.

Abstract (英文): Most vision-language models (VLMs) specialize in offline video understanding; in contrast, their post-trained versions, streaming VLMs, can perform live interactions. We investigate whether streaming VLMs can replace their offline parent models on offline tasks. Such replacement could reduce deployment costs and improve efficiency. We propose Streaming Tax to systematically evaluate streaming VLMs on offline tasks. Our exploration spans three core dimensions: understanding, trustworthiness, and generalization across 15 benchmarks. Our findings reveal a critical gap between offline capability preservation and practical replaceability. This gap emerges at two stages: streaming adaptation can alter trustworthy behavior even when offline understanding is largely preserved, while the native streaming protocol can introduce further performance losses. Our analyses show that some of these losses arise from restricted access to historical visual evidence and failures to deliver usable responses. Importantly, regarding safety, streaming models often leave both harmful and legitimate requests unanswered rather than genuinely refusing unsafe ones. We then propose Stream++, a training-free plugin to improve streaming VLMs on offline tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We investigate whether explicit memory can serve as the organizing principle of a general-purpose visual backbone. Inspired by the interaction between working and long-term memory in human cognition, we introduce DuSAM (Dual-Store Associative Memory), a hierarchical memory-native visual backbone coupling image-local working memory (WM) with a fixed-capacity persistent semantic memory (PM). WM organizes local visual evidence into compact, spatially anchored states that are updated and transported across stages, then read back to refine dense features. PM consolidates semantic evidence from training images and annotated regions into prototypes, whose retrieved content enriches the same working states. With these memory operations in place of self-attention or state-space blocks, DuSAM achieves competitive performance relative to representative, similarly sized backbones on ImageNet-1K classification, ADE20K semantic segmentation, and COCO object detection and instance segmentation. Ablations show contributions from both stores, with PM particularly benefiting class groups with fewer annotated training pixels. At $2048\times2048$, the complete DuSAM-based UPerNet segmentor achieves $2.36\times$ the throughput of the evaluated DeiT-S/16 counterpart while using $59.5\%$ less GPU memory, and $1.24\times$ the throughput of the linear-complexity VMamba-T. Memory analyses reveal spatially selective WM access and semantic associations between retrieved prototypes and training regions. Class-targeted removal and restoration further demonstrate that category-specific experience can be replenished through state updates without changing network parameters. These results establish explicit memory as a viable organizing principle for visual backbones and provide a foundation for jointly designing contextual computation and addressable semantic memory.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models (VLMs) are often asked which of two actions in a video happened first, given only a few sampled frames. Such frames can show that both actions occurred without showing which came first, so a reliable model should state an order only when the frames establish it. Forced-choice temporal benchmarks treat every input as answerable and reward lucky guesses, while answerability benchmarks do not separate frames that miss an action from frames that show both actions but not their order. We introduce EvOrderBench, a real-video benchmark of 579 event families. Each family fixes an episode and a question and varies only the frames: of two four-frame inputs, only one establishes the order, and two six-frame inputs extend the other one with either the frames that resolve the order or frames that do not. Every input is labeled for whether its frames establish each action and their order. Across sixteen open-weight configurations and three closed-source references, the best open models order nearly as accurately as the references when the frames establish the order, but withhold correctly on at most 83% of the inputs where they do not, against at least 90% for every reference; forced-choice accuracy cannot reveal this gap. Open models withhold mainly when the frames show neither action; once the frames show one or both, most of them commit to an order. Thinking narrows the gap, even when it is switched on within a single checkpoint, mainly by separating sufficient from insufficient evidence rather than by making models more cautious overall. Accurate ordering thus does not imply knowing when the frames fall short, and benchmarks should reward withholding alongside ordering.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Passive single-photon avalanche diode (SPAD) cameras provide short-exposure observations for low-light perception, but collecting task annotations for these sensors remains difficult, and annotated datasets are scarce. We present a 3D simulation pipeline that generates realistic photon detections together with camera-motion and semantic labels, enabling perception models to be trained on synthetic SPAD observations and applied directly to real-world data. The pipeline couples controllable scene geometry and motion with a sensor-informed observation model that accounts for photon shot noise, dark counts, and spatial crosstalk. We evaluate the pipeline through calibrated photon-count comparisons and zero-shot sim-to-real transfer across three downstream perception tasks. We show that classifiers trained only on synthetic SPAD data achieve high accuracy on real low-light recordings at illumination levels less than 2 lux, without real-data fine-tuning. Similarly, we apply models trained entirely on simulated camera sweeps to real-world camera-rotation estimation and achieve mean absolute rotation errors of less than 1 degree on real 20-degree and 30-degree sweeps at 0.5 and 5.0 lux, when deployed zero-shot. We additionally train semantic segmentation models on synthetic data with automatically generated dense labels and perform direct inference on real-world low-light recordings; the results further demonstrate the usefulness of synthetic dense supervision for real low-light perception. Our simulator thus provides a practical source of annotated training data for real SPAD perception.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In real-world deployments, vision systems must operate under varying environmental conditions, such as illumination changes. Recent work has explored adaptive sensing strategies that select camera parameters according to the current environment and downstream task to improve task performance. Reliable physical benchmarks are therefore essential for evaluating such strategies. We study ImageNet-ES-Diverse, a physical benchmark that evaluates the adaptive exposure control strategy Lens by recapturing printed ImageNet images under varying illumination conditions. We construct four controlled acquisition pipelines that vary printed-image scale and camera zoom, and conduct a pilot study with directly captured physical objects. We find that the acquisition choices substantially change benchmark difficulty on all downstream baselines. Moreover, the average classification-accuracy advantage of Lens over auto exposure observed on the original benchmark does not persist in either our collected pipelines or real-object pilot study. Oracle exposure selection nevertheless retains substantial gains, indicating remaining potential for sensor control. These findings highlight acquisition protocol as an important factor in physical benchmark evaluation and motivate explicit protocol reporting and evaluation across multiple acquisition setups when assessing adaptive-sensing benefits.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language that describes an articulated object is often about its state: the drawer pulled out the furthest, the door opened halfway, push the drawer all the way in. Existing 3D language fields attach one feature to each point and cannot tell these descriptions apart, and 4D language fields condition on video time, which has no kinematic meaning and cannot be set by a user. We present ArtLang-4D, which reconstructs an articulated object from a single monocular RGB-D video and equips it with a dynamic language field indexed by articulation time t, the normalized state of its joints, instead of video time. The geometry is a canonical triangle field whose triangles follow typed screw joints, discovered from the video without any joint initialization. The language field is supervised by a t-tagged caption bank that describes each part with comparative state phrases in a whitened open-vocabulary text space. A free-form query is answered by maximizing part relevance jointly over parts and states, which returns both the part and the articulation state it describes; a command is then executed by moving only that part along its recovered joint. On the 100 objects of Articulate-100, ArtLang-4D recovers more accurate joints and part geometry than reconstruction and articulation baselines, retrieves the queried state far more reliably than a state-agnostic language field, and executes language commands with lower motion error.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision Language Models (VLMs) are vulnerable to visual prompt injection, in which an adversarially perturbed image forces the model to respond with the attacker's chosen content, such as malicious tool calls, website links, or advertisements, sometimes regardless of the user's question. Existing defenses, such as diffusion-based purification and image-token masking, mitigate these attacks at a substantial cost to the model's utility on benign inputs. To address this challenge, we propose AVERT, which removes adversarial feature directions only when the model's answer depends on them. Our key procedures involve identifying anomalous value subspaces and detecting answer dependence. To identify the anomalous value subspace, we compare the statistics of each input's attention-head outputs with those of clean images and extract the directions along which the input deviates the most. We then project out these directions to remove the injected content. Because the same directions also carry benign visual information, we apply the removal only to answers that depend on them, namely answers whose likelihood collapses once the directions are removed or that are as likely under unrelated questions as under the user's question. Against white-box attacks on four VLMs (Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, LLaVA, InternVL2.5) and four injection benchmarks, AVERT lowers average attack success from 86-90% to at most 0.6% while retaining at least 96% of undefended utility averaged over thirteen benchmarks. Its defense-utility F-score ($F_{DU}$), the harmonic mean of attacks stopped and utility retained, is 98.0-99.1 and the highest among all compared defenses on every model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automated readings of pennation angle, fascicle length and muscle thickness from B-mode ultrasound depend on the geometry of the stored frame and on how fascicles that leave the image are extended, and challenge entries add settings chosen on a small public leaderboard. This study measures all three in one entry of the UMUD Challenge, which scored 309 frames from six screen layouts, 103 on a public and 206 on a private leaderboard. The pipeline applies layout-specific values of scale and pixel aspect, resamples each frame to square pixels, segments aponeuroses and fascicle fragments with U-Nets and measures the three readings geometrically; the submitted entry blended its angle and length with public notebooks. Because the score is additive, 65 single-change contrasts from 13 base entries measured each decision on both splits. The primary selected entry scored 0.27608 on the private split, the 69th reported position of 690 teams. Reading every frame with square pixels cost 0.029 public and 0.037 private. Of 20 late-stage corrections, 9 kept the sign of their effect on the private split. An audit of the UMUD expert benchmark found that 26 of its 35 frames share their tissue with training images of both DL_Track_US and the challenge. On the 9 frames with no detected match in either set or orientation, the recipe scored 0.213, two experts against two others 0.218 and DL_Track_US 0.263 (0.209, 0.196 and 0.259 when both raters of a reference pair are required), with no difference established. Frame geometry and the extension rule moved readings as much as the choice of model, and small corrections tuned on about 100 public frames did not carry over.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Egocentric video records what a person looks at, but often misses what their body does: the hands leave the frame, and arm motion goes unseen. An exocentric camera recovers this context, but it must be installed wherever the activity happens. Active acoustic sensing can recover it from the wearables themselves: a device's speaker emits inaudible chirps, and its microphone senses the motion of the hands, arms, and nearby objects from their echoes. Yet no public dataset pairs it with egocentric video. We present EgoCARBS, with two deliverables. The first is a multimodal dataset and benchmark: 15 participants cook unscripted meals in their own kitchens wearing camera glasses and an off-the-shelf smartwatch that both sense with active acoustics, synchronized with egocentric and exocentric video and the watch's audio and IMU, with 4,019 verb-noun action annotations in 11.4 hours. The second is a study of what the wearables add to egocentric video. On verb recognition, they add at least as much as an exocentric camera for participants seen in training, and even more for new participants, for whom the exocentric camera does not help. Active acoustics alone provides most of this gain, and helps most where the camera does not see the hands. Trimmed action recognition, detection, and anticipation remain open. We will release the data, our data collection and annotation tools, and pre-trained models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Implicit neural representations (INRs) capture global low-frequency structure during the early stage of training and then refine localized high-frequency details, afterwards. However, standard optimizers are agnostic to the coarse-to-fine learning behavior of INRs. Such optimizers accumulate a gradient matrix in momentum buffer and retain components misaligned with the dominant low-frequency directions in the early stage. We propose a progressively growing rank scheduler and a rank-aware learning-rate scheduler for enhancing a subspace-based momentum optimization, where a momentum matrix is updated within a low-rank subspace. The rank schedule expands the subspace during training, and the scheduler holds the peak learning rate through rank growth so that the added subspace contributes to fine-detail recovery. Experiments on image fitting, single-image super-resolution (SISR), and neural radiance field optimization show that the proposed method improves convergence and peak reconstruction quality over competitive optimizers. The largest gains appear in the high-frequency refinement stage, mirroring the coarse-to-fine progression that the rank schedule targets. A significant study on hyperparameter optimization (HPO) on NeRF further confirms that these gains persist under matched search budgets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI dose-prediction models generate patient-specific 3D dose distributions from patient anatomy. Yet these predictions can still miss structure-level clinical dose targets. Once deployed, most existing predictors offer no direct mechanism to correct such deviations, deferring target enforcement to downstream plan optimization. We introduce DoseSteer, an inference-time framework that steers frozen diffusion-based dose prediction models towards specified clinical targets without retraining. To meet clinical targets while preserving overall dose prediction quality, we propose Dual-Response Guidance (DRG), a structure-centric algorithm that probes the model's responses to perturbations in each structure's noisy dose and context and jointly solves for per-structure steering strengths. Across five dataset-model configurations spanning three diffusion architectures and three datasets, DRG outperforms 12 training-free baselines under identical reference-derived target proxies, removing at least 97% of the target error, reducing the official dose error by 7.8% on average, and improving dose fidelity outside the steered structures. DoseSteer reconciles learned anatomical dose priors with explicit structure-level targets at test time, establishing a principled, training-free mechanism to refine AI dose predictions prior to plan optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/csp2002/DoseSteer.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent generative video methods achieve strong perceptual compression by encoding videos into compact latent representations and reconstructing them with powerful generative decoders. However, visual encoders must compress both high-level semantics and dense visual details into a unified latent space, which complicates entropy coding and decoding and may cause semantic drift under aggressive compression. In this work, we propose Large Language Model-Guided Video Compression (LLMVC), which leverages the summarization capability of frozen video large language models (Video-LLMs) to generate compact semantic tokens aligned with temporal latent slices for guiding video compression. Specifically, Hybrid Bidirectional Bimodal Attention (HBBA) enables bidirectional interactions between visual and semantic features during encoding and decoding. Semantic-Conditioned Cross-Attention (SCCA) incorporates semantic features into probability modeling during entropy coding. These semantic features provide high-level guidance throughout the pipeline, improving representation quality and encouraging semantic consistency through a transmitted text side channel. Experiments on UVG, MCL-JCV, and HEVC show that LLMVC consistently improves compression performance over traditional and recent neural video compression methods in terms of LPIPS and DISTS, while also achieving better fidelity than recent perceptual codecs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D sign language generation aims to produce signing motion from written text. Motivated by the linguistic nature of sign language, recent methods combine a motion tokenizer with a language model to predict motion tokens from text. However, both components are optimized through a two-stage pipeline with mismatched objectives: the tokenizer minimizes reconstruction error in isolation, while the generator learns from rigid one-hot reference tokens. A straightforward end-to-end extension bridges discrete token selection with straight-through estimators (STE), suffering from biased gradients and codebook collapse. To address these limitations, we propose SEEL, an end-to-end framework that aligns tokenization and generation with motion quality through two complementary mechanisms. First, to overcome rigid one-hot supervision, reconstruction-aware generation decodes candidate tokens in context and converts their 3D motion errors into soft targets, guiding the generator with physical motion quality. Second, to propagate generation feedback without STE, generation-aware tokenization passes continuous codebook expectations through the frozen generator, routing generated-motion gradients to the tokenizer to prevent codebook collapse. Combining both mechanisms enables the tokenizer and generator to co-evolve, achieving state-of-the-art performance on CSL-Daily, How2Sign, and Phoenix-2014T. Code and models will be released to facilitate future research.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon video generation is key to interactive world modeling, physical intelligence, and spatial reasoning, yet maintaining geometric consistency and accurate camera control remains challenging without 3D grounding. We introduce \textbf{VivaWorld}, a framework for generating geometry- and view-consistent long videos from an image. We first construct a compositional hybrid 3D representation that combines reconstructed point clouds, which preserve observed scene geometry and appearance, with generated textured meshes, which provide complete object geometry across $360^\circ$ novel views. We also introduce a connected-region filter removing inconsistent floating components for robust cross-view reconstruction and propose a hybrid camera embedding that combines global and object-anchored Plücker rays to capture both scene-level camera motion and camera-object relationships, and inject it together with the visual guidance into a video generation model via a control adapter. Extensive experiments show VivaWorld achieves state-of-the-art visual quality and camera control, while supporting controllable novel-view synthesis, long-horizon scene exploration, and physical AI. Project page is available at: https://research.nvidia.com/labs/sil/projects/vivaworld/

Abstract (英文): Multi-scale architectures are widely adopted in all-in-one image restoration (AIO-IR) to capture larger-scale context at low-resolution stages and refine details at high-resolution (HR) stages. However, processing spatially large feature maps at HR stages incurs substantial computational costs and memory traffic, making efficient HR processing challenging. To address this challenge, we propose High-resolution Global-Local Restoration (HiGLR), an efficient multi-scale AIO-IR network grounded in an analysis of spatial redundancy in self-attention and convolutional feed-forward networks (ConvFFNs). Specifically, this analysis identifies three key redundancies: per-query readout at the finest-scale stage, key routing across neighboring queries at intermediate stages, and spatial mixing in the expanded feature spaces of ConvFFNs. Accordingly, HiGLR replaces costly self-attention at the finest-scale stage with a learned input-independent kernel. At intermediate stages, we propose a Block-Routed Hybrid Attention, which combines coarse-scale dense attention with fine-scale block-sparse attention over value residuals relative to coarse summaries, and shares routing indices across neighboring queries. We further introduce a Dual-Space FFN that performs spatial mixing in a compact space and pointwise nonlinear transformation in an expanded space, enabling efficient kernel fusion. Combining these designs, HiGLR achieves state-of-the-art performance while delivering up to a $4.24\times$ inference speedup over existing methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dental caries assessment is a structured clinical process requiring lesion localization, identification of the affected tooth and surface, and severity grading rather than simple image-level recognition. 3D intraoral scans (IOS) preserve detailed dental geometry and surface morphology and support standardized rendering from complementary viewpoints, enabling fine-grained visual assessment while introducing a distinct need for anatomically grounded multi-view reasoning. Despite recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and dental vision-language benchmarks, existing evaluations do not jointly assess hierarchical arch-to-tooth-to-surface reasoning, cross-view evidence integration, and surface-level caries assessment. To address this gap, we introduce CariesBench, a hierarchical benchmark constructed from 5,932 3D IOS, comprising 85,455 standardized multi-view renderings and 133,994 clinically grounded visual question answering instances with expert quality control. CariesBench comprises three complementary task families: Arch-level Clinical Screening (ACS), Tooth-level Diagnostic Reasoning (TDR), and Structured Clinical Decision-Making (SCD). Using the CariesBench training set, we further fine-tune Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct to obtain IntraoralGPT. Extensive evaluation of 24 representative baseline MLLMs reveals a substantial benchmark-level performance disparity between arch-level screening and tooth-level diagnostic reasoning, with higher accuracy on ACS than on TDR. CariesBench-specific supervision substantially improves the Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct baseline, increasing the 19-task macro-average clinical accuracy from 52.7% to 69.5% and TDR Overall from 34.4% to 44.7%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Transformer-based 3D reconstruction models such as VGGT and $π^3$ predict cameras, depth and point maps directly from many input views. Causal attention models such as StreamVGGT extend them to online video but cache all past tokens, so their total compute grows quadratically with the stream length. Recurrent models such as CUT3R and TTT3R keep a fixed-size state at constant cost, but they progressively forget early geometry. We present LinearVGGT, a hybrid model distilled from StreamVGGT, which uses Mamba2 layers for efficient sequence modeling in three of every four global layers. The remaining layers use register-guided sparse attention to retrieve a few relevant past frames, compensating for the limited exact long-range recall of Mamba2. Our distillation needs only 9\% of the GPU hours and 31\% of the training frames that StreamVGGT uses to learn from VGGT. LinearVGGT keeps an almost constant latency of 27 ms per frame over 1,024 frames and is on average $12 \times$ faster than StreamVGGT. It outperforms its teacher and is competitive with the state-of-the-art streaming model LingBot-Map, while running $2.5\times$ faster and using $2.7\times$ less memory.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fine-grained ego-vehicle action recognition turns large-scale driving video into structured, searchable driving scenarios, providing a foundation for discovering critical maneuvers and systematically developing and evaluating autonomous driving models. However, large-scale driving videos often lack explicit ego-action labels, and existing datasets vary in action definitions, annotation granularity, and available supervision. We introduce EgoManeuver, a unified benchmark of 16 lateral and 6 longitudinal actions constructed from seven public driving datasets. Our multi-source labeling system leverages existing annotations and complementary evidence available in each source dataset, including vehicle signals, ego poses, HD-map topology, and captions, to assign labels under a unified fine-grained action taxonomy. We further standardize temporal action boundaries across datasets and validate the resulting labels through kinematic consistency checks and targeted human review. The resulting benchmark contains 86.6K segments spanning 190.8 h across 26 countries and provides a long-tail-aware evaluation protocol. We also introduce DenseEgo, a video-based baseline that combines spatially aware representations with dense per-step action supervision. On the EgoManeuver, DenseEgo achieves 82.7 lateral and 83.5 longitudinal mAP, outperforming the strongest existing video model by 1.5 and 5.0 points, respectively. Finally, we demonstrate one way to integrate DenseEgo with a language-model agent for retrieving driving videos that match user-specified actions and scenarios. All code, datasets, and model weights will be publicly released.

</details>
