Abstract (英文): Visual process reward models (PRMs) can detect calculation errors yet assign high scores to steps that misread visual relations. Supervision based on subsequent answer success does not directly establish whether a step's relational claim holds. To strengthen visual-relation verification, we first build an automated pipeline that programmatically verifies each claim and identifies the required objects, relations, and image regions. It produces RelProcessTrain and RelProcessBench (RPB), which tests whether scores respond appropriately to relational evidence through controlled image edits and step rephrasings. Building on this pipeline, we further introduce RelPRM, a visual-relation-oriented method for process reward modeling with two training mechanisms. Relation-evidence self-distillation constructs views preserving necessary objects, relations, and dependencies, reducing the burden of finding decisive evidence in complex scenes and transferring reliable evidence-assisted judgments to full images. Relation-evidence-driven consistency training encourages appropriate score changes when relation edits reverse a step's truth, stability under irrelevant edits, and similar score changes across paraphrases. At inference, RelPRM directly scores the current step using only the original image and reasoning context. RelPRM achieves 66.2% on VisualProcessBench (VPB) and 68.4% on RPB, gains of 4.2 and 9.9 percentage points over VisualPRM-8B. These results show that training for visual relations improves both relational verification and step assessment across broader multimodal reasoning tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate impressive multimodal capabilities, yet suffer from hallucination - generating factually inaccurate content. Contrastive Decoding (CD) mitigates this by contrasting amateur and expert branches at the logit level. However, our investigation reveals that such logit-level interventions fundamentally compromise generation coherence, necessitating restrictive penalty constraints unrelated to hallucination suppression.
  We introduce Attention Contrastive Decoding (ACD), a training-free plug-in that complements logit-level CD by relocating part of the contrastive operations to the attention mechanism. Operating at an earlier stage of the forward pass, ACD performs smooth semantic-preserving interventions through an Adaptive Subtraction Strategy (ASS), which attenuates hallucination-associated attention patterns while amplifying critical visual information. Extensive experiments demonstrate that combining ACD with existing CD methods (e.g., VCD+ACD) produces substantially more coherent outputs with further reduced hallucinations, eliminating restrictive penalties while enabling trustworthy multimodal generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has demonstrated remarkable performance in aerial scene reconstruction, motivating its recent extension to true digital orthophoto maps (TDOMs) generation. We propose Tortho-Gaussian++, a novel 3DGS training framework that suppresses geometric drift and floaters and automatically prunes unreliable peripheral regions, coupled with a robust gravity-aligned TDOM rendering solution. First, we explore geometric support via viewing-ray-based geometric observability analysis, quantitatively characterizing the reliability for each Gaussian and subsequently pruning peripheral regions with particularly weak geometric support. Second, the optimizer state initialization is refined for newly created Gaussians during densification, intrinsically suppressing drift and floaters without external priors. Lastly, a robust gravity direction estimation solution is designed, combining image-derived gravity cues, camera-distribution priors and nadir views, to achieve a gravity-aligned orthogonal projection reference for TDOM rendering. Extensive experiments on multiple large-scale UAV (Unmanned Aerial Vehicle) datasets demonstrate that our Tortho-Gaussian++ outperforms vanilla 3DGS by 0.5-3.5 dB in average PSNR and yields comparable or superior performance to prior-guided 3DGS methods. Notably, it reduces the training time, VRAM and Gaussian number by 45.5%, 47.2%, and 58.6%, respectively. Qualitative evaluations confirm that our method effectively suppresses drift and floaters, while robust gravity direction estimation reduces tilt-induced facade artifacts in TDOMs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Cross-modal self-supervised learning for paired side-scan sonar and underwater optical imagery is a promising direction for benthic habitat understanding, but it remains underexplored because existing methods do not fully exploit the one-to-many correspondences and geographic metadata available in real opti-acoustic surveys. This limits the ability to learn representations that are both semantically meaningful and geometrically consistent from abundant unlabeled marine data. To address this problem, we present GeoCAM, a geography-guided framework that learns a shared embedding space for sonar tiles and their associated optical views without semantic annotations. GeoCAM achieves its goal by combining three components: (i) a Set Attention Aggregator that adaptively summarizes multiple optical observations into a single tile-level representation; (ii) a geometry-weighted InfoNCE objective that converts GPS-derived footprint overlap into soft positive supervision; and (iii) an optional cross-modal masked reconstruction branch that encourages physically grounded cross-modal alignment. Experiments on the BenthiCat SSS-CAM dataset show that GeoCAM can be effectively exploited to learn which sonar and camera samples describe the same seabed patch, improving cross-modal retrieval and alignment over strong contrastive baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/FadiZidiDz/GeoCAM.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World Action Models (WAMs) leverage future visual modeling for robotic action learning, with recent studies exploring alternative or complementary objectives beyond conventional RGB prediction. We introduce Event-WAM, which incorporates event-derived visual changes as auxiliary future supervision alongside RGB. Event-WAM constructs temporally structured event representations from RGB demonstrations and encodes them using a frozen pretrained RGB video VAE, without training an event-specific VAE. Joint RGB--Event latent flow matching provides appearance- and change-oriented supervision without requiring event observations or explicit future generation at inference. Across simulated benchmarks, Event-WAM maintains comparable performance under standard conditions, while robustness gains under perturbations are benchmark-dependent, including improvements on LIBERO-Plus. Real-world evaluations further demonstrate improved manipulation success under both standard and unseen perturbation conditions. Further analyses show that policy improvements are not consistently accompanied by higher future prediction fidelity, suggesting the potential of change-oriented supervision for action learning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern video foundation models generate high-quality short clips, but preserving long-video consistency and correctly depicting event-driven state changes remain challenging. Existing agentic workflows face these challenges because continual planning and repair accumulate context, while reference updates remain limited. We introduce StateWeaver, which combines Active States and Hierarchical Collaboration to connect review-driven visual reference updates with cross-shot state inheritance. Active States translate state discrepancies identified during review into concrete visual asset requirements that guide reference construction, validation, and regeneration, preserving identity and already-correct content while repairing target attributes. Confirmed generation results are linked to the corresponding asset states, story time, and reference versions for verification and reuse by subsequent shots. Hierarchical Collaboration isolates execution contexts by task, clip, and repair round, and uses hierarchical memory to organize this process across multiple sets. Across multiple benchmarks, StateWeaver outperforms existing methods and produces long videos with sustained cross-shot consistency under predefined and autonomously planned storyboards.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate novel-view synthesis of B-mode ultrasound images requires modeling acoustic propagation through tissue and the resulting appearance variations across probe poses. We propose Ultrasound Propagation Physics-informed Gaussian Splatting (UPP-Gauss), which integrates these acoustic interactions into an explicit Gaussian representation. UPP-Gauss constructs acoustic maps by sampling three 3D Gaussian fields using the probe pose and acoustic beam propagation direction, then combines these maps according to ultrasound propagation to synthesize B-mode images. Across three acoustically diverse datasets, UPP-Gauss achieves the best reconstruction quality with efficient rendering, improving PSNR over the best baseline on each dataset by 1.43 dB on average. These results demonstrate accurate and efficient B-mode novel-view synthesis through explicit Gaussian modeling of ultrasound propagation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Although multimodal large language models translate single screenshots into static HTML, generating interactive functional websites from design specifications still struggles with multi-page routing and stateful widget transitions. Examining existing training pipelines reveals two bottlenecks: (1) in forward data synthesis (X -> Y), writing the specification X before code execution leaves unimplemented or altered requirements in X as supervision noise; and (2) in reinforcement learning, scalar rewards discard the rich state transitions observed during replay. Based on these findings, we introduce WebVibe and Interactive-SDPO to train InteractiveCoder. WebVibe reverses synthesis into Y -> X by verifying cross-page and in-page interactions in the browser before deriving multimodal inputs from replay-confirmed states to build the 31,542-site WebVibe-32K corpus with reusable execution contracts. Interactive-SDPO then conditions a weight-sharing self-teacher on the replay trace and a 2x2 state-transition montage to provide token-level supervision at zero inference-time overhead. On the public Vision2Web L2, one-shot InteractiveCoder-9B nearly 3x its base model's functional score (20.2 to 55.2 FS) and matches a 1T-parameter coding agent in a single pass with 111x fewer total parameters and 78x fewer tokens. On our WebVibeBench, InteractiveCoder-4B and 9B further outperform all evaluated baselines by over 23 points.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Coding agents are increasingly capable of generating 3D models, yet enabling users to effectively control the modeling process remains challenging. Existing workflows rely primarily on text prompts and reference images, which often struggle to convey precise spatial intentions and require iterative corrections. We introduce SemProxy, a lightweight intermediate representation that bridges intuitive human interaction and agentic 3D modeling. Instantiated as labeled 3D bounding boxes, semantic 3D proxies allow users to directly specify object parts and spatial arrangements while leaving geometric details to coding agents. These proxies can be serialized into structured text and rendered as images, making them directly accessible to existing coding agents without additional training or modifications. We further develop ProxyStudio, a user interface that integrates text, image references, and interactive 3D proxy editing into agentic modeling workflows. We demonstrate the versatility of SemProxy across object- and scene-level modeling tasks with different coding agents, and evaluate its effectiveness in supporting controllable human-agent collaborative 3D modeling through a user study.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diagnosing whole-slide images (WSIs) requires hypothesis-driven reasoning: pathologists form a differential diagnosis, seek discriminative evidence, weigh its reliability, and stop once it suffices. However, existing training-free WSI agents lack an explicit framework for such reasoning. They typically use a large language model (LLM) to aggregate the region descriptions of a multimodal LLM (MLLM), without a belief over the candidates or regard for the descriptions' reliability. Consequently, a repeated model error counts as independent evidence for an incorrect diagnosis. We introduce BayesPath, a training-free WSI agent that casts the pathologist's Scan-Look-Weigh-Decide process as reliability-aware Bayesian reasoning. An explicit belief over the candidates is updated with each observation through a reliability-aware likelihood and guides where to look next and when to stop. This likelihood corrects for the models' answer bias, discounts correlated observations, and captures diagnostic confusions estimated without labels. The updates form an interpretable evidence trail of how each observation shapes the belief. On five MultiPathQA tasks spanning slide classification and slide VQA, BayesPath achieves the highest average accuracy among training-free agents, 58.6% against 34.1% for the next best, with large gains on slide classification. On identical observations, Bayesian updating outperforms LLM aggregation on average, even with a larger LLM. BayesPath's belief is also better calibrated than naive Bayes, so uncertain cases can be deferred to pathologists. Code is available at https://github.com/bryanwong17/BayesPath

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-vocabulary change detection (OVCD) aims to localise user-specified changes without retraining for predefined categories. Existing approaches typically rely on textual descriptions and combine separate models for region proposal, semantic recognition, and temporal comparison. This limits how target concepts can be expressed and makes detection dependent on several intermediate predictions. We introduce OVTSID3, a training-free framework that unifies region grouping, concept identification, and temporal comparison in the representation space of DINOv3. OVTSID3 supports two complementary modes of concept specification: zero-shot textual queries or one-shot visual prompting through a masked reference pair. OVTSID3 outperforms all prior training-free OVCD methods on two building and two natural-disaster benchmarks, raising the average F1 by 8.9 points. OVTSID3 also supports semantic change detection and achieves state-of-the-art on SECOND. These results demonstrate the flexibility of a unified representation for text- or example-guided change detection without task-specific model training.

Abstract (英文): Background: Testicular appendage torsion (TAT) is the leading cause of acute scrotal pain in prepubertal children. Yet, its diagnosis remains challenging due to nonspecific clinical manifestations and the absence of standardized datasets for pediatric acute scrotal emergencies.
  Results: We present TAT-Det, an expert-annotated ultrasound dataset specifically designed for TAT. TAT-Det comprises 2,600 ultrasound images from 1,203 patients collected at the Children's Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine. Each TAT-positive image is accompanied by lesion-level bounding boxes for detection and pixel-level segmentation masks for fine-grained delineation. Annotations were produced through a multi-stage workflow involving independent annotation by board-certified pediatric sonographers, consensus review, and refinement. Baseline benchmarks for object detection and semantic segmentation using representative deep learning models are further provided, with YOLO11m achieving the best detection performance and RWKV-UNet the best segmentation performance.
  Conclusions: TAT-Det addresses the lack of publicly available resources for pediatric emergency ultrasound and establishes a foundation for developing and evaluating artificial intelligence methods for automated TAT analysis.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern video generators excel at producing visually plausible futures guided by text and image prompts. Yet their emphasis on semantic plausibility does not guarantee fidelity to the physical consequences of specific actions. Action-conditioned world models (ACWMs), in contrast, aim to simulate how the world responds to robot actions, enabling scalable policy evaluation and improvement without costly real-world rollouts. The challenge is not merely to generate a plausible future, but to predict what follows from a specific physical action. As video priors grow stronger, we ask how little adaptation is truly needed to bridge this gap. We introduce Dianjing, a minimal recipe that preserves and unlocks the capabilities of strong video priors through unified physical action conditioning, training concentrated at the high noise levels where motion and layout, and thus the consequences of actions, are decided, and native history conditioning. Built upon MiniMax H3, a 33B video foundation model, Dianjing freezes the pretrained backbone and trains only 0.34% of its parameters using approximately 65 hours of robot video across two embodiments, without additional robot-specific pretraining or camera calibration. Controlled ablations show that where training is allocated across noise levels matters more than how actions enter the network. On WorldOracle, which evaluates predictions against paired real-world executions, Dianjing achieves the best scores on four of the five evaluation tracks and ranks second on AgiBot-Long. Our findings suggest that strong video priors already bring us most of the way to world modeling; lightweight physical action grounding can unlock their potential for real-world prediction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recognizing an image we have seen before is a basic function of human visual memory. Understanding human continuous recognition memory (CRM) can help machine vision systems predict which images people are likely to remember and generate more memorable images. It can also inform the diagnosis of memory impairments and the development of interventions to improve memory. This paper proposes the first computational model for CRM. The model captures three key components of continuous recognition memory (CRM): image encoding, memory decay, and decision making. It encodes each image as a distinct random variable in a perceptual space. As the memory of an image fades, the distribution of its random variable drifts within that space. To decide whether the current image has been seen before, the observer retrieves samples from the distributions associated with previously encoded images and compares them with the current image. Our model predicts human observers' trial-by-trial responses in CRM tasks within the variability observed across human participants. Our model is parameter efficient, requiring only four shared parameters and two parameters per observer. It can accurately predict the memorability of an image no worse than prior methods that require millions of parameters.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent video generators produce realistic videos, yet controlling how scenes unfold remains challenging. Structural proxies, such as animated 3D blockouts, guide generation by specifying scene layout, motion, camera behavior, and event order. However, these coarse inputs leave details unspecified and may contain artifacts that should not be reproduced. Successful proxy-guided generation must therefore Preserve intended structure, Complete missing content and correct proxy artifacts, and Bind text, image, and audio conditions to the right entities and events. We introduce ProxyGenBench to study these interacting requirements, with 312 evaluation cases from 60 scenes across three scene tasks and controlled variations in proxy fidelity, text detail, and multimodal inputs. We evaluate five video generators using verified, case-specific checklists. Experiments show that proxies improve spatiotemporal control, but greater proxy detail can coincide with poorer completion, while strong structural adherence does not ensure correct binding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Looped multi-view reconstruction refines scene features by repeatedly applying a shared transformer block. Making these updates cheaper requires knowing how an approximation at one step affects the rest of the refinement. We study this with paired interventions in Déjà View and stacked reconstruction transformers, separating the error an approximation injects, how that error propagates, and how it affects the output. In Déjà View, approximation errors are attenuated more strongly than matched random perturbations under a scale-adjusted state-error measure, and a local Jacobian decomposition separates the contribution of channel gating from the response to spatial error structure. Spatial approximations made early benefit from subsequent refinement, whereas cross-step reuse depends on which quantity is cached and how reuse steps are spaced. At equal final norm, approximation errors change the predicted rotations 1.55 times as much as random errors. These findings guide our proposed method, which reduces spatial resolution and pools keys and values early in refinement, then interleaves attention-output reuse with full attention, and is adapted through lightweight self-distillation. Across five reconstruction benchmarks, the balanced configuration runs $1.63\times$ faster than the official 16-step Déjà View model with a 1.86-point reduction in average pose AUC@$3^\circ$. At matched latency, it exceeds the interpolated curve of a trained baseline using full refinement blocks by 4.73 points in AUC@$3^\circ$ and improves the corresponding geometry metrics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Dual-domain networks combine projection and image processing for sparse-view low-dose computed tomography (CT). We quantify the contribution of angular interpolation before learning and distinguish its roles in sinogram initialisation and the image residual base. On synthetic parallel-beam data, parameter-free interpolation reduces noise-free missing-view mean absolute error by 99.7% relative to zero filling at 60 views. Full-view filtered back-projection (FBP) of the jointly learned completed sinogram adds 1.27 dB of peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) over interpolated FBP. In a data-consistency dual-domain network (DC-DuNet), a 2x2 configuration ablation controls branch width, depth and training schedule while varying these placements and their associated input channels. After 20 epochs, the PSNR contrasts are +1.98 dB for sinogram initialisation and -2.16 dB for the interpolated image base; the image-base ranking also reverses during training. The sinogram-initialised variant exceeds an image-only U-Net by 0.96 dB at 60 views and 10% dose, and by 1.28 dB on independently generated synthetic data. The U-Net leads at higher view counts and with a shared interpolated image base. Without retraining, the variant retains a 0.12 dB advantage on held-out CHAOS abdominal CT under simulated degradation; the image-base contrast rounds to zero. The implemented projection/backprojection pair has empirical dominant gain of approximately 0.85NK, where N is image-grid width and K acquired view count. These results distinguish interpolation placement from network architecture and support NK normalisation for step calibration.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video large language models incur substantial inference costs from long visual token sequences. Existing video token compression methods mitigate these costs by exploiting spatial redundancy within frames and temporal redundancy across frames. However, many approaches impose predefined processing orders or fixed allocation ratios for spatial and temporal compression, limiting their ability to adapt to the varying balance of spatiotemporal redundancy across videos. To address this limitation, we propose PURA, a training-free method that prunes visual tokens through unified spatiotemporal redundancy assessment. For each token, PURA constructs spatial and temporal reference candidates and selects the reference with the lowest representation error across the two dimensions. The representation error is combined with token importance to determine each token's removal cost. To avoid the time overhead of removing the lowest-cost token one by one, we introduce Coordinated Pruning, which iteratively groups mutually compatible low-cost removals into batches for simultaneous execution and updates the pruning state after each batch until the target budget is reached. Extensive experiments on three video large language models and five video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that PURA achieves a favorable trade-off between task performance and inference efficiency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Free-text 3D grounding, segmenting in a CT volume the region described by one sentence of the radiology report, could link reports to images at scale, but it is unclear where current models fail. We analyse the publicly released VoxTell model on all 381 findings of the 200-scan ReXGroundingCT validation set, where it grounds 53.5% of findings. Its failures are rarely about which lesion or side is meant. No miss lies on the side opposite to the one named, and findings are almost never confused with one another. Instead, predictions are too large, land on a neighbouring lesion on the correct side, or are missing for small findings, and diffuse findings are rarely grounded. Removing location words halves performance, to the level of label-only prompting, and anatomical rules derived from the sentence never improve the full-sentence output. The same pattern holds on the hidden 300-scan test set. Further progress therefore depends on extent estimation and small-lesion sensitivity rather than on anatomical post-processing.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Due to context-length constraints, most MLLMs cannot process full-length videos and therefore rely on sampling a subset of frames as input. However, existing sampling methods, ranging from uniform sampling to relevance-based selection, are often driven by a single sampling principle and thus struggle to accommodate the heterogeneous evidential requirements of different queries: some demand a holistic understanding of how the video unfolds over time, while others hinge on fine-grained events within a short temporal window. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{Hierarchical Adaptive Frame Sampling (HAS)}, a two-stage frame sampling framework. In the first stage, \emph{Backbone Frame Construction}, we apply a Determinantal Point Process (DPP) to sample frames that are both query-relevant and non-redundant. These selected frames capture key moments across the video and form the backbone of the sampling set, providing a foundation for subsequent enrichment. In the second stage, \emph{Adaptive Contextual Enrichment}, we analyze the temporal distribution of the backbone frames to infer the query's requirements and adaptively allocate the remaining frame budget between Local and Global Context. The Local Context enriches the backbone with fine-grained temporal dynamics and short-range causal relations, whereas the Global Context connects temporally isolated evidence and provides a holistic view of the entire video to support global understanding. Through such a hierarchical two-stage process, HAS effectively addresses diverse query requirements. Incorporated into three leading MLLMs, it demonstrates consistently superior performance across the Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and MLVU benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in video understanding, yet existing video question answering (VQA) benchmarks mainly evaluate models in offline settings. Real-world streaming scenarios instead require models to answer context-dependent queries by retrieving and reasoning over evidence distributed across the video history. We introduce StreamRecall, a benchmark for context-aware Multi evidence reasoning in streaming video QA. StreamRecall requires models to retrieve, associate, and reason over temporally scattered evidence to answer queries at the appropriate moment. It contains 4,983 questions over 462 videos, with context-aligned query times and fine-grained temporal evidence annotations. We further propose the Evidence Utilization Cascade to diagnose model reasoning through evidence localization, grounding, chain completion, and answer derivation. To enable efficient long-term streaming reasoning, we present EviStream, a training-free causal visual memory mechanism that preserves reasoning-critical evidence under a fixed KV budget. Experiments reveal substantial gaps between current VLMs and realistic streaming requirements, establishing StreamRecall as a foundation for developing evidence-aware streaming video intelligence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Departing from the paradigm of diffusion and autoregressive models, Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) enables high-quality image generation through iterative refinements. However, this repeated processing over the entire visual representations results in substantial computation overhead. Furthermore, the binary representations and global refinement mechanism in GRN disable the direct application of acceleration strategies developed for other generative models. To accelerate GRN model, we first observe several key properties by analyzing the entire generative process. That includes the stage characteristic, dual branch similarity and more importantly, the $\textbf{refinement redundancy}$. Motivated by the observations, we present $\textbf{FasterGRN}$, a training-free acceleration method for the Generative Refinement Network. $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ first divides the GRN into the semantic establishment stage and detail refinement stage according to its semantic establishment. For alleviating the refinement redundancy mainly resulting from the detail refinement stage, we propose Adaptive Retention Expansion ($\textbf{ARE}$) and Selective Token Freezing ($\textbf{STF}$) to shrink refinement steps and reduce token computations, respectively. In the experiments on GenEval and DPG-Bench benchmarks, our method $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ achieves a $2.03\times$ sampling speedup while largely preserving image generation performance. Our evaluation on COCO datasets additionally demonstrates that $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ even outperforms the original GRN in image fidelity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Establishing effective scaling laws for medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) remains a persistent and fundamental challenge. Our systematic evaluation shows that existing medical MLLMs tend to underperform frontier general-purpose MLLMs across a range of medical tasks, while scaling model size primarily improves text capabilities rather than multimodal perception, and enabling reasoning modes fails to close this visual gap. To investigate how medical multimodal intelligence scales in practice, we introduce Sage-Med-Preview, a 35B-parameter medical multimodal model with 3B activated parameters, built by combining stage-specific curation over 322.7 million candidate records with a progressive training curriculum spanning medical pretraining, staged supervised fine-tuning, and adaptive reinforcement learning. Across this curriculum, progressive supervised refinement and adaptive reinforcement learning yield consistent stage-wise gains, substantially advancing both specialized visual understanding and medical language capabilities. Evaluated against 77 competing models across 238 clinical tasks spanning 61 public and 13 private multi-center benchmarks in more than 40 modalities, Sage-Med-Preview achieves an average score of 58.26% on the 31 public multimodal benchmarks. It outperforms the state-of-the-art open-source medical model Hulu-Med-235B-A22B at 50.99% and the general scientific model Intern-S2-Preview at 52.24%, and approaches the advanced proprietary model GPT-6.2-Sol across multiple tasks such as comprehensive multimodal understanding, report generation, and visual grounding. Although substantial challenges remain in complex medical reasoning and multi-step clinical workflows, this report provides empirical evidence, actionable data and training recipes, and a rigorous benchmark toward scalable clinical multimodal intelligence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Geospatial foundation model (GFM) research papers are plagued by inconsistent evaluation settings (e.g., different pre-processing and probes), making it difficult to separate performance from evaluation choices. We propose geo-eval-harness}, an open-source framework for controlled model comparisons. We use this harness to evaluate 100 GFMs and general vision models on 6 classification and 14 segmentation datasets, using multiple probes on frozen or low-rank-adapted encoders. We show that under controlled evaluation, general vision backbones are competitive on RGB classification and high-resolution segmentation, but GFMs shine on multispectral tasks. When we compare any two of the top-ten classification models on a single dataset, their 95% confidence intervals overlap 73% of the time. We show that bigger models are not always better: inference FLOPs correlate weakly with segmentation performance. Moreover, translating inference compute into large-area mapping workloads shows how small accuracy differences have outsized deployment cost differences: with frozen encoders on SpaceNet2, a 0.9-point mIoU difference corresponds to 5.9x the compute and days of additional projected GPU time. Our findings stress the importance of comparing models under explicit and fixed settings, and reporting uncertainty and deployment compute alongside accuracy. Our geo-eval-harness makes controlled experiments easy, fast, and fully reproducible. We release the code, configurations, and results at https://github.com/torchgeo/geo-eval-harness.

</details>

Abstract (英文): As agents based on vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly interpret social-media content, errors in their analysis may lend credibility to rumors and amplify their spread. In this paper, we study the problem of visual cognitive collusion, in which coordinated posts combine authentic images and locally faithful captions to induce VLM agents to infer and accept attacker-chosen target claims through their own reasoning. To enable systematic evaluation of this cognitive threat, we introduce a new benchmark comprising 544 target cases and 33.2K candidate images across eight application domains. We further propose VisualMontage, a multi-agent framework for simulating such attacks through narrative planning, image selection and ordering, and caption assignment under iterative review. We evaluate this threat through extensive experiments across 12 vision-language models, examining modality effects, production-team components, and downstream defenses. Using identical attack feeds across models without victim-specific optimization, we demonstrate cross-model transferability, achieving mean attack success rates of 61.93% for proprietary models and 68.71% for open-weight models. Moreover, 92.65% of generated feeds pass independent model-based validity auditing. Even after downstream review, residual attack success rates range from 35.3% to 76.7% across the evaluated configurations. Our additional controlled comparisons further reveal that captions can improve model-rated posting naturalness while reducing attack success. Together, these findings reveal a novel cognitive threat in social-media interpretation and motivate safeguards that assess how authentic posts jointly shape agents' reasoning and conclusions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World-space human mesh recovery can lose spatial consistency even when camera-space body estimates are accurate. A single sequence-wide scale resolves global scale ambiguity but cannot correct local scale distortion along a reconstructed camera path. We present ScaleCurve, a framework that estimates time-varying metric corrections from human-ground geometry. We construct scale observations by comparing predicted foot geometry with locally fitted ground planes. Joint temporal optimization downweights inconsistent observations and couples neighboring scale estimates, allowing calibration to adapt along the camera path without freely following measurement noise. We scale camera translation increments and integrate them into a calibrated trajectory. With this camera fixed, we refine the lifted body motion using 2D image evidence and pretrained 3D dynamics, while restricting global trajectory rescaling. ScaleCurve achieves state-of-the-art performance on most evaluated metrics on both EMDB-2 and SLOPER4D. Consistent gains across three HMR backbones demonstrate its compatibility with diverse body estimates without retraining. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A vision encoder turns an image into one vector per patch, refined layer by layer. A sparse autoencoder (SAE) writes each vector of one layer as a sum of learned directions, most with a coefficient of zero. Researchers call each direction a feature and edit the encoder by removing one feature. An SAE trained from another random seed learns different features. Earlier work matches the two SAEs' features one to one by how closely their directions agree. Matched features can point alike yet respond to different image parts, so removing them may change the encoder differently. We remove a feature and, separately, its match, then compare the changes in the encoder's output on the same held out images. When the second change nearly reproduces the first, we say the removal carries across seeds. A setting fixes a DINOv2 or SigLIP layer and its SAE feature count. At our tolerance, the removal carries for 12% to 70% of matched pairs across three settings. The share is 0% in two controls: an SAE whose directions stay random and, post hoc, matches shuffled among trained features. Post hoc, predicting which removals carry is more accurate with layer contributions than with directions. Some incorrect predictions remain. One persists on independent images. So pairs can be screened at the layer. Measuring the output detects errors this screen misses.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Understanding manga requires connecting who appears and who speaks with the events, character states, and relationships that unfold across panels. We study manga visual parsing through local story understanding: analyzing each double-page spread independently to capture and organize its story information in a reusable structured representation. We propose MangaParser, an agentic reading framework that jointly performs character clustering, speaker diarization, and speaker-to-character binding within a shared multimodal conversation, without additional training or fine-tuning. The agent uses structured story memory to guide character and speaker identification, inspecting relevant image regions when uncertain and refining its predictions and story records together as evidence accumulates. Quantitative evaluations show that our parser substantially outperforms manga-specific models across multiple backbones. Parsing examples illustrate how a unified representation links the character and speaker predictions with narrative content and supporting evidence. We further test reusability through story question answering using outputs generated before the questions are provided. The results confirm output reusability, offering a foundation for manga translation, content retrieval, and question answering.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many embodied world models predict future states in latent spaces optimized for RGB reconstruction, which do not explicitly prioritize object-level semantics and geometry relevant to motion generation. We present MotionRep, a visual representation world model that jointly generates future DINO representations and 3D object flow, coupling semantic and geometric information with object-centric dynamics. To make high-dimensional DINO features amenable to generative modeling, we introduce DINO-VAE, which combines temporal and channel compression with latent regularization to produce compact latents while preserving semantic and geometric information. Conditioned on an initial observation, object query points, and a language instruction, a unified diffusion transformer predicts future visual representations and 3D object flow through joint attention. MotionRep employs asymmetric modality-specific noise schedules to keep the visual stream ahead of 3D object flow during joint denoising, allowing progressively refined future visual representations to guide flow generation. Experiments on simulated and real-world manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that MotionRep outperforms pixel-based baselines in 3D object flow accuracy and generalization to unseen scenes, while requiring only three denoising steps for inference. These results support predictive visual representations as an effective future-state space for object-centric dynamics modeling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unsupervised cross-modal homography estimation is challenging because appearance differences obscure shared geometric structures. Separating modality transfer from homography estimation helps reduce these differences, but accurate alignment still requires effective geometric representations and consistency constraints on the two subtasks. We propose GeoCo (Geometry-Aware and Consistency-Constrained Homography Estimation), a framework that addresses these two requirements without cross-modal transformation labels. Its homography estimator, RVHomo, adapts hierarchical features from a frozen vision foundation model (VFM) and fuses them with local CNN features to capture structural context and spatial detail. Two geometric consistency constraints encourage modality transfer to preserve geometry and homography predictions to remain consistent under synthetic geometric transformations. Through two-stage progressive distillation, we further obtain GeoCo-D, which uses the lightweight RVHomo-D estimator. An intermediate model first learns to perform cross-modal matching without modality transfer, then transfers prediction and feature knowledge to the student, eliminating online VFM extraction. Experiments on GoogleMap, OPT-SAR, FSD, and VIS-IR show that GeoCo reduces mean average corner error by 17.4-46.7% relative to the best-performing baseline on each dataset. GeoCo-D also outperforms all compared baselines while using 98.5% fewer parameters and 70.4% fewer FLOPs than the complete GeoCo teacher.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Answering a knowledge-based visual question requires first recognizing the entity in the image and then recalling knowledge about it. Thinking vision-language models (VLMs) reason for thousands of tokens before answering, yet it remains underexplored how well such thinking VLMs perform each of these two steps. We therefore analyze thinking VLMs on this task and make two key observations. (1) Recognition is the bottleneck, since 76.3% of errors across four models on ViQuAE stem from misrecognizing the entity, and 84.9% of these failures are resolved once the entity name is provided in the question. (2) Recognition is driven by entity-recall clues rather than by general descriptions of the image. An entity-recall clue is a distinctive visual detail that helps the model recall the entity, such as keys, a traditional attribute of Saint Peter. Based on these findings, we propose ClueRL, a reinforcement learning approach that extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with an advantage localized to the entity-recall clue in the thinking trace. Rather than injecting new entity knowledge, ClueRL changes how VLMs think: it leads them to attend more closely to visual details and spot these clues, helping them use the knowledge they already have. Consequently, it outperforms both GRPO and a perception-reward baseline on three entity recognition benchmarks. Moreover, this clue-seeking behavior generalizes beyond entity recognition, the only task used for training. Since recognition is the bottleneck, the gains carry over to knowledge-based visual question answering. They even extend to general multimodal reasoning, raising Qwen3.5-4B by 4.4 and 4.0 points on the ScienceQA and MMMU subsets of MMStar, respectively.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Foundation models (FMs) have advanced visual understanding and generation by providing semantically rich representations. However, this role of FMs remains underexplored in human motion. Large motion datasets offer diverse movements at scale, but often lack action labels for systematically evaluating learned representations on understanding tasks. Skeleton datasets enable such evaluation through established recognition protocols, yet are typically smaller and focus on predefined actions captured in controlled environments. To combine these strengths, we present MoSk, to the best of our knowledge, the first Motion-Skeleton foundation model to integrate motion and skeleton datasets. MoSk learns shared representations through self-supervised masked motion modeling. We incorporate joint-name embeddings to provide a common anatomical reference across various skeleton formats. A cross-attention decoder reconstructs masked motion from the encoder's features, with joint-name embeddings specifying the target joints. Experiments demonstrate strong action-recognition performance across diverse settings, including linear evaluation and fine-tuning. MoSk also substantially improves motion generation across multiple backbones through representation alignment, reducing FID on MotionMillion by up to 9.75 for unconditional generation and 3.83 for text-to-motion generation.

Abstract (英文): World-action models jointly predict future observations and actions, yet visual corruption can disrupt the resulting policies. We introduce HOG-WAM, which adds future Histograms of Oriented Gradients (HOG) as structural supervision derived directly from demonstration images. An auxiliary HOG diffusion transformer (DiT) module reads RGB features through asymmetric attention, allowing its objective to shape the visual representation used for control. The module is used only during training and removed at inference, preserving FastWAM's RGB-to-action architecture. We evaluate four controlled observation corruptions through a visual-corruption extension of RoboTwin, termed RoboTwin-Plus; HOG-WAM achieves the highest success among the compared policies under all four. Mixed-noise success increases from 78.86% to 82.86% relative to FastWAM, with comparable performance on clean scenes. On LIBERO Plus, overall success increases from 74.49% to 75.26%, with the largest gain under sensor noise, from 74.14% to 78.89%. Across four Franka tasks, average success increases from 40% to 59% under clean observations and from 17% to 24% under noisy observations. A representation ablation favors combining RGB observations with auxiliary HOG prediction over replacing RGB with HOG. These findings support future-gradient prediction as complementary supervision for visually robust control, with larger absolute gains on physical tasks than in simulation. Code is available at https://github.com/iPPLab/HOG-WAM.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video generation models are increasingly evaluated as visual reasoners: given a first frame and a natural-language prompt, can the model produce the video in which the specified task is carried out? The paradigm is considered advantageous because the reasoning is native to the visual medium, unfolding directly in the frames rather than through language or code. Whether this native route is in fact the more reliable path to visual reasoning has not been tested against a symbolic alternative under the same conditions. We therefore pose the same tasks, with identical inputs, output specification and evaluator, to both media: rather than denoising pixels, a coding agent writes a program that renders the answer video, which an unmodified rule-based evaluator scores. On VBVR-Pro-Bench (Xu et al., 2026), a 500-instance suite spanning five cognitive categories with symbolic ground truth, the agent receives the same first frame and prompt as the video model, with no access to ground truth, metadata or generation models, and one attempt per instance. Three closed-model agents (Codex with gpt-6-astra, Claude Code with Fable 5.1 and Gemini CLI with Gemini 3.1 Pro) score 0.923, 0.894 and 0.893, each more than 0.22 above the strongest video model. Among 23 runnable open-weight models, the best, GLM-5, reaches 0.572, above all video models except the RL-trained one. Together, we show that for tasks whose solutions can be computed from a first frame and a prompt, program synthesis is currently a substantially more reliable route to visual reasoning than video generation. We further distinguish depictive programs, which navigate the image to recover a transferable scheme, from propositional programs, which operate on hard-coded values, revealing how visual reasoning unfolds in agent-written programs and what it implies for its nature of reasoning in general.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Detailed hand motion contributes to the realism and expressiveness of generated human motion. Completing these details from body motion and language requires connecting the context of an action to the articulation of the hands. We introduce ContextHands, which completes hand motion from an observed body sequence and text descriptions assigned to hands and time intervals. Our approach learns probabilistic hand features from the descriptions and body motion, using auxiliary hand prediction to connect these features to hand articulation. A body encoder preserves joint and body-part features across multiple temporal scales, allowing each hand joint to query these features for joint-specific body information. The diffusion generator combines this joint-specific body information with the learned hand context. Both encoders and the generator are trained jointly, so the final generation objective also shapes the context features. Experiments on the OakInk2 dataset show that ContextHands generates more accurate hand motion and achieves lower Fréchet Hand Distance than existing hand-motion and general human-motion generation methods adapted to the task. In qualitative examples of grasping and tool use, ContextHands more closely reproduces the detailed hand poses in the reference motion. We will release our code and pretrained models to support further research.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text-to-Image (T2I) models turn short text descriptions into images, and the leading models now produce images of comparable visual quality. Whether their outputs still carry model-specific characteristics matters for attribution, for model comparison beyond aggregate quality, and for evaluations that assume anonymity. We ask whether images from frontier T2I models can still be classified by source, and which visual properties make this possible. Here we show that a standard ResNet-50 identifies the source of an image among HiDream-O1-Dev, FLUX.2-dev, HunyuanImage-3.0, and Qwen-Image-2512, the four highest-ranked open-source models on the Text-to-Image Arena, with 100.00% accuracy on held-out prompts. Human participants reach only 62.61% on the pairwise version of this task, so the distinguishing cues are not visually obvious. They are also not tied to a particular classifier and survive severe image corruptions and out-of-distribution prompts. We trace them to visual idiosyncrasies at three levels. Low-level texture statistics alone let a lightweight multi-layer perceptron match ResNet-50. Object layout, depth, and albedo estimated from the images each carry a source signal. Image captions reveal model-specific preferences in style and background that the prompts never specify. A one-sentence hint about these cues raises human accuracy by 9.03 percentage points. These idiosyncrasies make source classification a tool for measuring similarity between models and between real and generated images, and they caution against assuming anonymity in human preference evaluation of T2I models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We introduce BOP-Refer, the first benchmark for monocular 3D and 2D grounding of everyday and industrial objects sourced from 9 diverse BOP datasets. We pair images from these datasets with VLM-generated and human-verified referring expressions, and derive accurate ground-truth 3D and 2D bounding boxes of the referred objects from their 3D models and 6DoF pose annotations. Our systematic evaluation of 17 frontier VLMs across the major proprietary and open-weight families (Gemini, GPT, Claude, Qwen, Gemma) shows that the best model, Gemini 4 Carbon, reaches 72.6% AP_IoU2D in 2D but only 12.4% AP_IoU3D in 3D, despite the more lenient IoU thresholds in 3D. The predicted 3D boxes are usually centered on the target objects in the image but often off in depth by more than the object's size, suggesting that metric scale estimation, which needs reliable object size priors, is currently by far the main source of error.

Abstract (英文): Diffusion and flow-based generative models support high-quality image, audio, and video synthesis. Sampling can require dozens to hundreds of denoiser evaluations, with each pass becoming more expensive as resolution, video length, or model size increases. This cost affects latency and energy use in interactive and edge applications. We examine four approaches to accelerating inference: distillation, consistency training, sampling methods, and denoiser design. We express training objectives, transition maps, and numerical updates in a common notation to compare changes to the learning target, the sampling trajectory, and the work performed within each denoiser pass. Published image and video benchmarks illustrate the quality--cost trade-offs of one- and few-step generation, the interaction between solvers and timestep schedules, the effect of feature caching, and the difference between first-frame latency and sustained streaming throughput. We close with open problems in visual-native architectures and post-compression distillation, solver-aware few-step training, and semantic trajectory alignment with factorized distribution matching.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This work presents a novel application of Earth Observation (EO) embeddings for Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) from the perspective of the geospatial foundation model TerraMind. By operating directly on compressed latent-space representations rather than on the original Sentinel-1 imagery, this approach directly addresses the data gravity bottleneck that increasingly constrains operational EO processing, in line with the objectives of the Embed2Scale project. In particular, linear and non-linear probing of compressed embeddings, latent space representations of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery, is used to detect, localise, and characterise vessels from Sentinel-1 GRD images. Vessel detection achieves a ROC-AUC of 0.95, while vessel localisation has a median error of around 300 m for vessels longer than 100 m, and vessel AIS type-code classification achieves 62% accuracy for the majority classes. The latter, however, is highly correlated with vessel length. These results reveal both the potential and current limitations of Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) when applied to fine-grained MDA understanding in real-world scenarios.
  Additionally, a new maritime remote sensing dataset is released, pairing about 2,000 Sentinel-1 images with temporally aligned AIS signals from commercial vessels. Through manual inspection, each candidate sample was verified for bounding-box quality and for consistency between the AIS-reported vessel length and the observed vessel extent, cross-checking vessel identity and location against publicly available vessel records and, where available, photographs. The dataset was made ready for use with the NeuCo-Bench neural compression benchmark framework, publicly available on HuggingFace through https://huggingface.co/datasets/embed2scale/S1-single-ship-downstream .

</details>

Abstract (英文): Consistent and controllable 3D stylization is critical for visual computing applications such as immersive visualization, augmented and virtual reality content creation, and artistic scene rendering. While 2D image stylization has advanced significantly, extending these methods to 3D remains challenging due to the need for cross-view consistency, where stylized renderings must align across all viewpoints. Applying 2D stylization independently to each rendered view often results in inconsistencies across perspectives. Existing methods attempt to improve consistency by leveraging implicit radiance field representations, which model scene appearance as a function of spatial locations. However, these methods struggle with ambiguity in updating colors, as the indirect dependence between pixel appearance and learnable scene parameters makes it difficult to enforce consistent stylization during optimization. This work presents Surface-based Scene Stylization (Surf-Style), which reconstructs object surfaces in a 3D scene and uses these surfaces to achieve geometric consistency and localized stylization. Surf-Style defines a learnable appearance model over 3D space, which is modified to replicate the visual style of a reference image. Compared to state-of-the-art methods, our method achieves superior consistency and offers greater flexibility in controllable stylization, including the ability to target specific surface regions and combine different styles.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present SPARC, a geometry-guided attention pruning framework that turns the native self-attention of pretrained video diffusion transformers into a unified mechanism for camera control and persistent scene memory. Our key insight is that attention connectivity can simultaneously serve as geometric conditioning for camera control and spatial addressing for historical memory retrieval. Motivated by the asymmetric contextual requirements of reconstructing observed content and synthesizing novel regions, SPARC restricts observed tokens to geometrically corresponding historical tokens and local spatiotemporal context, while preserving dense attention for unobserved regions. Correspondence-aware attention biasing and order-invariant historical positioning further enable stable retrieval across long temporal gaps. Without introducing additional learnable control or memory modules, SPARC achieves precise camera adherence and faithful scene revisitation while substantially reducing attention computation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of aggressive attention pruning in improving camera adherence, revisit consistency, and visual fidelity, with an inference speedup of over 50x when combined with four-step distillation. Project page is available at https://zju3dv.github.io/SPARC.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Given a continuous RGB-D stream and a language request, streaming 3D affordance grounding requires an agent to decide when to respond and where to locate the requested functional part using only observations available so far. Existing 3D affordance grounding methods typically assume complete scene observations, while streaming vision-language models lack fine-grained 3D functional grounding. Our goal is to jointly determine response timing and functional-part localization during continuous scene observation. We propose StreamAff, an evidence-guided response strategy that makes both decisions from the grounding model's own visual evidence. The model scores the visual patches of the frames it reads: their combined support, weighed against a wait option, decides when to respond, and the highest-scoring patch selects the frame to ground in. A conditional look-back policy lets the model answer from an earlier observation at the moment of the request or wait for new evidence, and zoom-and-refine grounding segments the part named in the answer on the 3D points of the selected frame. We further introduce StreamFun3D, a benchmark derived from real-world 3D scenes that evaluates response timing and grounding across four evidence settings: Current, History, Future, and Unobserved, together with StreamIoU, a metric that credits a mask only when the response is timely and grounded in a valid source frame. We adapt offline 3D affordance grounding methods and build external-decider baselines that pair vision-language models with our mask decoder. StreamAff achieves 30.14% StreamIoU on 15-second streams, a 40.6% relative improvement over the strongest of these baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, yet adapting them to sequential tasks requires Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT). Existing replay-free methods expand prompts or routed LoRA experts across multi-layer decoders, incurring heavy parameter and routing overhead while shifting internal feature distributions. Instead, we identify the final linear layer of the vision-language projector as a high-capacity, coordinate-stationary bridge, where sequential updates easily induce interface drift by displacing historical visual tokens. While low-rank subspace adaptation offers a compact remedy, selecting directions solely from historical statistics or task gradients sacrifices plasticity or stability, and unconstrained multi-step updates still accumulate large drift. To resolve both issues, we propose Projector Refinement through Optimized Update Directions (PROUD), a continual subspace optimization framework for the terminal projector layer. At task onset, PROUD combines retained input statistics with initial gradients to analytically extract a metric-orthonormal subspace maximizing first-order task gain under a drift budget. During training, it regularizes updates within this fixed subspace via an exact Frobenius penalty before losslessly merging them with zero inference or routing overhead. Experiments on UCIT and CoIN across MLLM backbones show that PROUD outperforms recent replay-free baselines in continual accuracy while curbing forgetting with a fraction of trainable parameters.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Lightweight trackers have replaced cross-correlation with attention, and on low-power edge hardware they pay for it, since memory-bound softmax, normalization and reshaping operators of attention cost far more time than their arithmetic suggests. Benchmarking efficient trackers on an edge Neural Processing Unit (NPU) and an Arm CPU under one numerically validated protocol, we find that correlation trackers are the fast ones, but they lag in accuracy unless they adopt a ViT backbone, which brings attention back. We revisit what correlation lacks. Template--search interaction serves two roles: conditioning the search features on the target and matching them against it spatially. Attention does both, whereas correlation only matches, and does so on features extracted without knowledge of the target. We present TaCo (Target-aligned Correlation), which supplies the missing conditioning without attention by letting the template enter the per-frame network only as operands computed once per video. A descriptor of the target scales and shifts the search features channel by channel inside the backbone, and kernels aligned with the target box, each with a weight predicted from the template, correlate with them in one matrix product. With the backbone fixed, our correlation remains essential while incurring negligible NPU overhead, offering a better speed-accuracy trade-off than the attention-based interactions we tested. TaCo-T achieves 62.3% LaSOT AUC at 184 FPS on the Rockchip RV1126B NPU and 96 FPS on a Raspberry Pi 5 CPU, 3.0x and 3.5x the NPU speed of HiT-Tiny and AsymTrack-T at higher accuracy. TaCo-B reaches 65.6% at 50 FPS on the NPU, more than twice the speed of HiT-Base, AsymTrack-B and LightFC-vit, again at higher accuracy. Code and models are available at https://github.com/TsingWei/TaCo-Tracker.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal drafts learn from multimodal (MM) and text-only (TXT) supervision, yet their effects on alignment and how to combine them remain unclear. We diagnose draft-target alignment through paired predictions with and without visual input, decomposing distributional error into text-conditioned and visual-response components. Drafts with similar text-conditioned alignment can still differ markedly in visual-response risk and acceptance. Controlled continuation training shows that high-risk groups outperform random and low-risk groups, and visual-response risk guides MM training more effectively than aggregate risk. Building on these diagnostics, we propose MNRS-Joint, which configures joint supervision from a given draft's component-risk distributions, using modality-specific mean risks to determine the MM:TXT mixture. Across three warm-ups, its distinct mixtures fall in high-performing regions of the ratio sweeps. On LLaVA-1.5-7B, MNRS-Joint improves mean accepted length from 3.35 to 3.53 and mean speedup from $2.04\times$ to $2.16\times$ over composition-matched random training under greedy tree decoding. The gains also hold under stochastic chain decoding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In this paper, we introduce EiT by exploring the key design factors of pixel-space diffusion from three perspectives: model architecture, representation, and frequency domain. Inspired by Denoising Autoencoders (DAE) which perform denoising through a bottleneck, we construct a U-shaped pixel diffusion transformer that performs explicit compression along the channel dimension. In terms of representation, we find that both pixel and latent diffusion transformers exhibit a three-stage representation dynamics across layers and timesteps: denoising/local feature expansion, basis lift-up, and posterior projection. Moreover, earlier denoising and a longer basis lift-up stage are associated with better generation, which EiT naturally achieves through its channel bottleneck and increased network depth. From the frequency perspective, we establish the equivalence between pixel-space and frequency-space diffusion when keeping the network operating in pixel space. On ImageNet $256\times256$ and $512\times512$, EiT consistently outperforms JiT (Just image Transformers) in generation quality. Specifically, our EiT achieves better FID than JiT: 1.61 vs. 1.85 at $256\times256$, and 1.73 vs. 1.99 at $512\times512$. Remarkably, EiT can be seamlessly applied to video pixel diffusion by simply extending the patch size to temporal dimension and outperforms video latent diffusion in FVD evaluated on Kinetics-600 (resolution 256), under comparable total network parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/forever208/EiT

</details>

Abstract (英文): Cross-modal homography estimation requires reliable correspondences despite appearance differences between sensing modalities. Multi-scale refinement progressively reduces the search range, but its accuracy remains limited by the ability of matching features to distinguish nearby candidates. The vision foundation model (VFM) provides complementary representations, yet integrating these features through fixed interpolation leaves their spatial aggregation independent of image content and the correspondence objective. We propose the Foundation Model-Enhanced Matching Network (FoMNet), which combines learned spatial adaptation of VFM features with contextual correspondence estimation. Patch Feature Upsampling (PFU) progressively predicts location-specific kernels to aggregate neighboring features on upsampled grids, preparing VFM representations for fusion with CNN features at different matching scales. Contextual Correlation Refinement (CCR) then jointly processes source descriptors and local correlations with spatial context to refine candidate scores before each homography update. To reduce upsampling overhead, we develop FoMNet-D, which replaces PFU with bicubic interpolation and uses multi-scale feature-relation distillation to recover accuracy. Experiments on five cross-modal datasets show that FoMNet achieves the lowest mean average corner error (MACE) among the evaluated methods, reducing MACE by 10.6%-72.7% relative to MCNet. FoMNet-D retains competitive accuracy while reducing inference latency by 34.0% relative to FoMNet.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Overall part-discrimination accuracy can conceal failures when image position favors the wrong bilateral part. We examine whether dense descriptors distinguish the correct part under this conflict and whether their discrimination scores help select matching coordinates. We evaluate position agreement and conflict within the same target images and annotated part pairs, using a bounding-box-side score to define the conditions. We compare the original descriptors with their even and odd components under horizontal reflection, examine feature upsampling with the backbone and adapters fixed, and test candidate-selection rules based on odd scores. On SPair-71k, odd discrimination accuracy falls from 86.66% under agreement to 19.63% under conflict for frozen DINOv2, compared with 95.27% to 86.16% for MARCO. With MARCO's input geometry fixed, odd accuracy in box-side conflict was 34.12% on the native grid and 88.84% after learned upsampling and pooling back to that grid. Odd-score rules improve on the original hard-peak matchers overall for DINOv2 and DINOv3 and on conflict queries for MARCO. With MARCO's soft decoder fixed, reranking the cosine peaks changes mean per-pair PCK by +0.47 pp. For supervised GeoAware-SC, using the odd score to select the localization window center yields only 15 net additional correct matches, with 330 corrections and 315 introduced errors. Evaluating discrimination under position conflict reveals failures hidden by aggregate accuracy. Reporting corrections and introduced errors separately also shows whether a discriminative score produces useful matching decisions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Humans can recognize novel concepts from only a few observations by identifying defining visual attributes and eliminating alternative interpretations. However, a single observation is consistent with multiple possible concepts, and accurate visual perception alone is insufficient to determine the intended interpretation. Negative evidence can reduce this ambiguity by eliminating competing hypotheses. Inspired by Quine's gavagai problem, we introduce the Gavagai Benchmark to evaluate whether vision-language models can identify the attributes defining a novel concept from positive and negative examples. The benchmark consists of synthetic images depicting novel combinations of visual attributes and real images of newly described species. Using these datasets, we examine how models use negative examples to identify novel concepts and analyze the respective roles of visual perception and reasoning. Several models struggle even when correct attributes are provided, while others show large gaps when ground-truth attributes are replaced with model predictions. These diagnostic comparisons highlight challenges in both attribute perception and inference from limited evidence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The 3D points kept in a map decide whether a camera can be localized against it, and this choice matters more as the map becomes sparser. Existing 3D keypoint detectors usually define saliency by semantics, repeatability, reconstruction or descriptor matchability. None of these definitions asks whether a point will actually be detected in an image, and as a result most of the points they select are not detected again in images.
  In this paper, we define 3D saliency as image detectability: the response of a 2D interest-point detector at a surface point, aggregated over all views in which the point is visible. We propose DetField, a continuous field over 3D space that takes a point cloud as input and predicts detectability at any location around it. The supervision is the response of a 2D detector, back-projected from images onto the point cloud. Multi-scale curvature invariants are passed to the decoder at every query point, bypassing the voxel pooling of the encoder.
  In a localization benchmark that isolates the choice of 3D points, DetField outperforms Harris-3D and five learned 3D keypoint methods on objects and on real indoor scenes. On unseen rooms, it improves localization success by 7.4 points over Harris-3D, the strongest baseline. On objects, it also leads under a 2D detector not used in training. On sparse maps, it stays ahead of Harris-3D on both objects and real scenes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-world aerial object search methods often rely on persistent spatial maps, learned world models, or task-specific policy training. To examine how far a minimal, training-free design can go, we introduce SimpleUAV, an agent built around a frozen, off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM). SimpleUAV separates current-view target assessment from history-conditioned action selection, combining fresh candidate evidence with lightweight search context. Local geometry converts visual selections into bounded straight-line movements, depth-based checks halt blocked segments, and measured execution feedback guides subsequent decisions. This closed loop sustains search without persistent spatial maps or task-specific training. SimpleUAV achieves 41.61% Success Rate (SR) on UAV-ON and 11.98% on AerialDojo-200K, compared with 23.80% and 3.20% for the strongest prior baselines, respectively. With limited benchmark-specific adaptation, the same core agent transfers to aerial Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) on TravelUAV, improving Full SR by at least 19.66% across all three splits under L1 assistance. These results establish a strong baseline for evaluating when additional architectural complexity benefits aerial search.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reference banks used for industrial image inspection can be compressed to fit a deployment budget, but the resulting anomaly decisions depend on both the compressed scores and the operating threshold. We present a four-step protocol that compares compression methods at matched deployment sizes, establishes a reference distribution from 200 recalibrations, evaluates disagreement against two calibration benchmarks, and measures changes by distance from the full-bank threshold. With WideResNet-50, budget-matched product quantization (PQ) at the 10% budget has the lowest decision disagreement among five matched methods, including int8 scalar quantization, on VisA and MVTec AD at both operating levels. Int8 scalar quantization gives lower disagreement than PQ at the other four budgets. On MVTec AD at the lower operating level, PQ meets the calibration median in 34/39 cells and the upper quantile in 39/39 cells. Category-equally weighted image-level AUROC loss at this budget ranges from 0.0003 to 0.0037 across the evaluated configurations. On VisA at the 1% budget and a 1% operating level, disagreement reaches 28.12% (95% CI 23.25--33.58%) among near-threshold anomalies and 1.30% (0.79--2.14%) across the normal holdout. The 10% budget-matched PQ setting retains the lowest disagreement among four compared methods with ResNet-18, with clean reference banks, and when thresholds are estimated from normal-directory probes. The protocol connects deployment size to a calibration-referenced decision measure and identifies the evaluation populations most informative for inspection.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In 2D-3D localization, a camera estimates its pose by matching an image to a 3D map. A camera held in a hand or mounted on a robot can often choose its viewpoint before it takes the image. In this work, we ask whether an image detectability field is a useful viewpoint utility when the planner reads only the geometry of the map. The field can be queried at any 3D point near a surface. From the local geometry around the point, it predicts how likely a 2D image detector is to fire at the projection of the point. We use the trained field as it is, without fine-tuning it for view selection. A fixed SuperPoint and EPnP-RANSAC localizer then estimates the pose from the chosen view. We answer in two settings, each with a test registered before the runs. On synthetic ShapeNet objects with sparse point maps, summing the field over the visible landmarks beats counting them by +2.0 points of localization success rate (object-clustered 95% interval [+0.7, +3.2]). It also beats summing Harris-3D ranks by +2.2 [+0.8, +3.5]. On real indoor scenes from SceneFun3D, our adaptation of the learned view scorer LWL falls below counting on the test rooms. We therefore add the field to a learned per-landmark planner. On the test rooms with sparse maps whose landmarks the field also selects, the planner with the field beats the same planner without it by +1.8 points. It beats the planner with Harris-3D or curvature in its place by +1.0 and +0.8 points. All three room-clustered intervals lie above zero. In both settings, the field helps in sparse maps and is on par with Harris-3D in dense maps.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Equivariant neural networks promise improved representation learning through structural guarantees. However, mainstream vision encoders rarely incorporate equivariance for most geometric symmetries. Images are a particularly interesting modality with many such symmetries and extensive datasets from which to learn this structure. In this work, we investigate when vision encoders benefit from geometric inductive biases. We study the discrete rotation group of quarter-turns (C4) which acts losslessly without interpolation or boundary artifacts. Combined with image classification as a controlled invariant objective, this allows for a principled empirical investigation into the role of structural guarantees. We introduce ConvNeXt-C4 as a rotation equivariant ConvNeXt with dimension-equivalent yet orientation-structured representations. Training from scratch, our experiments show, that the C4 variant outperforms its base model across all tasks while operating with 6x fewer parameters and 1.6x fewer FLOPs. Its performance advantage increases log-linearly with decreasing training set size, with pre-training providing a similar improvement across both model variants. Analyzing the classifier head reveals that orientation information from the representations' structure is actively used, improving performance by 4.90% across all datasets. Our results highlight the sample-efficiency benefits of equivariance and the value of retained orientation information, motivating further investigation beyond image classification. Code is available at https://github.com/hlzl/equivariant-convnext.

</details>
