Abstract (英文): Physics-derived simulation can supplement scarce real-world LiDAR intensity measurements, but its effectiveness depends on the learning architecture and the amount of real training data available. We benchmark nine neural architectures for LiDAR intensity reconstruction from depth, semantic labels, incidence angle, and material reflectance, using real SemanticKITTI and synthetic VoxelScape range-image data. All models follow a fixed 20-epoch protocol; this controls the number of training passes, not the computational cost across architectures. We report mean absolute error (MAE), mean squared error (MSE), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), structural similarity (SSIM), inference timing, and spectral-energy agreement. We also evaluate four selected architectures with fixed-size training mixtures containing 0\%, 1\%, 5\%, 10\%, 20\%, 50\%, or 100\% real frames. With real-only training, UNet-V2 achieves the best reported reconstruction metrics (MAE 0.234, MSE 0.140, PSNR 8.64 dB, and SSIM 0.647), followed by a denoising diffusion model (DDPM). In several mixtures containing 1--10\% real data, DDPM achieves lower error and higher image-quality metrics than the evaluated deterministic alternatives, whereas UNet-V2 leads with 100\% real data. Spectral analysis additionally reveals architecture-dependent discrepancies across frequency bands. These findings characterize reconstruction trade-offs under the stated data mixtures and fixed-epoch protocol, without establishing compute-matched superiority or isolating the diffusion objective from backbone differences.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Video world models aim to create interactive physical worlds that capture how diverse materials move and interact. Yet material-dependent dynamics remain underexplored in controllable video generation, limiting reliable control in scenes with different materials. We present WorldInteract, a streaming video generation framework that decomposes cross-material interaction into material-dependent behavior generation and contact response computation. External pushes and contact impulses are expressed through a shared velocity-increment interface, allowing material behaviors learned from interaction-free videos to be reused in interacting scenes. A physics controller predicts contacts and computes velocity increments from estimated masses and restitution coefficients, while a causal generator executes these increments using learned material behaviors. This separation enables cross-material interaction without supervised contact videos spanning diverse material pairings and contact configurations. We train the generator on 30k simulated interaction-free videos spanning 22 material classes and introduce Material-Aware RL, using verifiable physical rewards to refine material behavior and control execution. WorldInteract substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods on Physics-IQ and delivers more reliable push control on real-world images.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World models are evaluated by predicting future observations from actions. These evaluations assume that actions are prepared the same way as during training, but an unchecked mismatch can make a model appear less accurate than it is. We audit this assumption in Navigation World Models (NWM) and find three interacting differences between its training and rollout code. Correcting the inputs, without retraining, reduces mean visual prediction error by 42%, measured with DreamSim on the RECON navigation dataset. The correction also changes the ranking of NWM checkpoints in an exploratory analysis and removes or reduces two alternative models' measured advantages. Correcting only the normalization offset instead increases error, showing why interacting mismatches must be checked together. To test whether sensitivity to input preparation extends beyond NWM, we deliberately introduce encoding errors into two alternative models; both become less accurate. We introduce ActRosetta, a tool that checks whether action encoding matches training, identifies supported causes of disagreement, and repairs inputs when sufficient information is available. Repair helps little where predictions barely respond to actions, the tool's diagnosis has limited coverage, and improved control remains unproven. For any action-conditioned world model, the lesson is the same: before reporting rollout error, verify that evaluation encodes actions exactly as training did.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate and early classification of brain tumours from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is essential for treatment planning, yet many published deep learning studies are trained and tested on small, single-source datasets, reporting accuracies that may not hold outside these datasets. This study compares six deep learning architectures for four-class classification (healthy, meningioma, glioma and pituitary tumour): a baseline convolutional neural network (CNN), an attention-based CNN, ShuffleNet, and EfficientNet-B0, ResNet-50 and Xception augmented with the Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM). The models were trained on a corpus assembled from four publicly available MRI datasets. Because these datasets carry no patient identifiers, a leakage-aware pipeline was developed that removes exact duplicates using MD5 hashing (550 images) and near-duplicates using perceptual hashing (8,024 images), and assigns visually similar images to the same fold during 5-fold group cross-validation. A leave-one-dataset-out (LODO) test on a fully held-out dataset was used to measure cross-dataset generalisation. Under a conventional random split, ShuffleNet achieved 97.1% accuracy with 1.2M parameters. Under leakage-aware group K-fold cross-validation, ShuffleNetV2 (98.41%) and EfficientNet-B0 (98.23%) were statistically indistinguishable (paired t-test, p = 0.19), and both significantly outperformed the custom CNNs. On the held-out dataset, accuracy dropped for every model: EfficientNet-B0 generalised best (78.4%), while ShuffleNetV2 fell to 66.2%. Glioma-meningioma confusion was the dominant error mode, and Grad-CAM maps showed that predictions were driven by tumour regions. These results indicate that in-distribution accuracy alone overstates real-world performance and that leakage-aware and cross-dataset evaluation should be reported alongside it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pretrained visual representations accelerate generative learning by abstracting away appearance and spatial detail. Yet the same abstraction makes the return to pixels far harder, shifting much of the burden onto a larger decoder. We ask why the representation-to-pixel path should be learned independently when the encoder already provides a learned pixel-to-semantics path (the semantic encoding path). We introduce RevFlow, based on the principle of generation as reverse encoding. RevFlow uses intermediate representations of a frozen encoder as successive generative targets, first generating deep semantic representations, then shallow representations that retain appearance and spatial detail, before decoding to pixels. To learn these transitions without training a separate generator for each stage, we develop HyC-DiT, a unified generative model with a standard DiT backbone shared across stages and stage-specific condition injection. We further show that native matching in semantic representation space enables few-step generation. RevFlow accelerates both learning and sampling. On ImageNet $256 \times 256$, RevFlow reaches 1.96 FID at 690M and 1.74 FID at 1.55B after only 5 training epochs. It also achieves 30.25 dB reconstruction PSNR with an 89M decoder. After a short native-matching adaptation phase, RevFlow-XL achieves 2.85 FID using a single sampling step in each stage. Our code repository is at https://github.com/ChengShiest/RevFlow

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scene rearrangement is an essential task for 3D content creation applications, requiring generation of diverse, aesthetic and functional scene rearrangements. We present SceneDesigner3D, an agentic evolutionary search method for 3D indoor scene rearrangement that systematically explores the design space of 3D scenes and generates multiple solutions according to common interior design principles. Given an input scene and a text prompt, SceneDesigner3D represents the rearrangement as an editable program and evolves a population of candidate layouts. Starting from distinct design ideas, a VLM agent mutates the programs by refining promising candidates, exploring new rearrangement strategies or redesigning from scratch, while verifying the layouts for physical validity. Proposals are scored by a VLM judge for adherence to the input prompt and satisfaction of design principles such as balance, zoning and emphasis, as well as numerically computed principles such as circulation and clutter. An archive of top-performing, structurally varied candidates further increases diversity of rearrangements. Experiments on the SAGE-10k and SceneSmith datasets show that SceneDesigner3D improves prompt adherence and computed design principle scores over baselines by up to 5.1% and 15.2%, respectively. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing Knowledge Distillation (KD) methods for semantic segmentation often guide the student to mimic the teacher's structured information generated from an individual image. However, they ignore the global semantic relations across various images that are valuable for KD. This paper proposes Cross-Image Relational KD V2 (CIRKDV2), which focuses on transferring structured pixel-to-pixel, pixel-to-region, and channel-to-channel relations modeled from global images in the dataset. A large teacher network often constructs a well-structured feature space in terms of global contextual dependencies. CIRKDV2 makes the student mimic the better structured semantic relations of the teacher, thus improving the feature representational capability. Beyond relational distillation, we further propose contextual feature distillation by using context aggregation to augment student pixel embeddings to match the semantic level with the teacher. This avoids the semantic gap problem between the teacher and student occurred in traditional feature distillation studies. Experimental results on Cityscapes, ADE20K, Pascal VOC, and COCO-Stuff-164K demonstrate that CIRKDV2 outperforms the state-of-the-art semantic segmentation distillation methods by large margins across various network architectures. Our code and pre-trained models are publicly available at https://github.com/winycg/CIRKD.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Guidance repels the prediction of a diffusion model away from a weaker reference prediction. It substantially improves sample quality, but its mechanism remains poorly understood. We first find that, at the scales used in practice, guidance does not improve the prediction of the posterior mean (the model's training target); it makes the prediction worse, while samples improve. Second, posterior-mean predictions have less variance than the data, and we find that guidance restores it. We introduce variance-matched guidance: a closed-form rule that picks the guidance scale by matching the variance of the guided predictions to that of the data. Without tuning, it recovers the hand-picked scales of eleven widely used models across image, video, audio, motion, and world generation; to our knowledge, no previous explanation of guidance predicts them. Applied per noise level, it yields a schedule without tuned hyperparameters that users prefer to hand-crafted ones on SD 3.5 Medium. Third, we offer an interpretation of the mechanism behind guidance: each model makes a random error in predicting the posterior mean, and guidance rescales the difference of these errors into a sample with the posterior's covariance, which improves sampling. This view also suggests two new hypotheses that our experiments support: guidance can work with negative scales, and guiding a model with its own checkpoint needs a larger scale than guiding it with an independently trained model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Constructing complete 3D worlds from a single panorama is challenging because large portions of the scene remain unobserved. Existing panoramic-video methods expand spatial coverage through dense sequential generation, but neighboring frames contain substantial redundancy, long trajectories accumulate errors, and independently generated directions may be inconsistent. We present WorldExpand, which formulates single-panorama world expansion as the generation of sparse, complementary, and high-resolution panoramic observations. This representation concentrates the generation budget on informative scene regions and preserves the fine-grained details required for 3D construction. WorldExpand addresses two key challenges: where to observe and how to synthesize. Our Coverage-Guided View Selection identifies informative target locations using scene-adaptive quadrants and warp-based coverage scores, while our Joint Panoramic Diffusion uses Gaussian-splatting-based geometric warps to jointly synthesize four pose-grounded and cross-view-consistent panoramas. A diagnostic-guided global--local attention schedule retains global attention only in sensitive layers, reducing the training and inference costs of high-resolution joint generation. The resulting posed panoramas can be directly fed into an existing reconstruction pipeline to build an explicit 3D representation. Experiments show that WorldExpand recovers content behind occluders and around distant corners, improves both novel-view synthesis and downstream 3D reconstruction metrics across various datasets, and generalizes robustly to diverse in-the-wild scenes. WorldExpand runs significantly faster than the state-of-the-art panoramic-video method while producing higher-resolution observations with stronger cross-view consistency.

Abstract (英文): Underexposure weakens object details, and overexposure obscures boundaries. Spike cameras encode light into high-frequency binary streams. Long-window spike counts give stable appearance cues, while short observations reveal changes in firing rates from sparse measurements. We propose SPIRE, a sensor-grounded semantic-spiking framework for illumination-robust object detection from a single spike stream. SPIRE builds an appearance view from the full packet through detection-oriented illumination adaptation and constructs a sequence of cumulative views from progressively longer prefixes. Each prefix supplies a rate map, an edge map, and a rate difference that tracks changes as spikes accumulate. At selected scales, ANN features and spike-derived initialization jointly set the initial membrane potentials and firing thresholds, allowing semantic cues to shape the responses during temporal encoding. Local spike-count contrasts supervise both branches during training. The residual between measured and ANN-predicted contrasts combines with the spiking features to produce a bounded correction to the fused feature pyramid. On simulated VOC and COCO, SPIRE-D outperforms the strongest baseline by 4.00-5.50 AP50 points across the four under- and overexposure settings. On real PKU-Vidar-DVS recordings, it achieves 67.07 AP50 and 40.10 AP75. SPIRE-D also uses 50.4% fewer MACs than SPFD.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text-to-image diffusion models can generate images that satisfy complex prompts, yet often produce images that omit requested objects, attributes, or relationships. We ask if models can more reliably generate text-aligned images by redistributing probability toward their more successful outcomes. We propose TrailMix, a training-free method that uses the base model's distribution of possible outcomes to adaptively guide generation toward better text alignment. During denoising, TrailMix generates inexpensive lookahead images from parallel trajectories (trails) and scores their alignment with the prompt using an external judge. The resulting distribution of reward scores allows us to estimate how strongly to steer generation toward a desired alignment level. We use these estimates to reweight and resample trajectories, favoring more promising outcomes. Across three text-to-image benchmarks, TrailMix outperforms existing baselines, with particularly strong gains on rare multi-object compositions. We further extend TrailMix to targeted image editing and introduce SEAM-Bench (Semantic Editing and Attribute Maintenance), a systematic benchmark evaluating whether semantic edits preserve other image content. Experiments on SEAM-Bench and existing benchmark PIE-Bench demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach for controlled image editing. Our results show how a model's own possible outcomes can inform reliable and controllable generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spike cameras capture high-speed motion through an integrate-and-fire mechanism, providing fine-grained temporal observations without exposure blur. However, their binary streams encode accumulated illumination rather than instantaneous intensity, making reliable motion estimation challenging. Existing spike-based methods exploit temporal aggregation to construct motion representations, but this process may weaken fine-grained firing patterns. Meanwhile, correlation-based estimators often rely on learned matching features without explicitly characterizing the spatial distribution and ambiguity of candidate displacements. To address these challenges, we propose TIDE-Flow, a spike-based optical flow estimation framework that jointly exploits Temporal Integration Evidence (TIE) and Displacement Evidence Encoding (DEE). TIE combines raw spike patterns, multi-scale firing statistics, temporal moments, and boundary-aware interval cues, preserving complementary temporal information before spatial feature compression. DEE further integrates structured candidate-grid features with explicit displacement statistics to characterize matching geometry and competing correspondences. Both components are incorporated into a difference-correlation recurrent refinement framework, enabling effective temporal motion modeling and correspondence estimation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of TIDE-Flow, with controlled ablations validating the contributions of temporal and displacement evidence modeling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Captured document images often suffer from diverse degradations caused by uncontrolled imaging conditions, which reduce readability and obscure fine text structures. Single-degradation restoration methods are usually trained and deployed for individual degradation types, and therefore require model selection before restoration. Unified restoration models alleviate the need to maintain multiple restoration networks by sharing one network across tasks, but representative unified document image restoration methods still depend on external task selection. This dependency limits automatic document image restoration. We propose DocDUR-Net, a degradation-aware unified restoration network that derives restoration guidance directly from the input image. It introduces a Degradation Perception Module (DPM) to extract degradation representations and predict degradation weights, and Degradation Prior-guided Adaptive Feature Fusion (DP-AFF) to combine learnable degradation priors with degradation features extracted from the input. The resulting guidance is injected into a shared restoration backbone, enabling adaptive restoration without external task selection. DocDUR-Net also supports a manual mode with explicit task selection. Experiments on document image restoration benchmarks show that DocDUR-Net achieves the best average PSNR of 29.405 dB and SSIM of 0.960 among unified methods while remaining competitive with single-degradation methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision transformers are strong but costly image encoders for video object segmentation. Training-free token merging cuts compute uniformly across the frame, including on tracked objects. We give a frozen DINOv3 ViT inside a trained Cutie head a training-free focus map: full resolution where the segmenter expects its objects and coarser merging elsewhere. Protecting only the previous frame's mask works on short clips but fails on long videos, where fast motion pushes targets outside the margin and into background merging, locking in loss. Our policy instead takes the union of velocity-shifted and block-matched masks, runs a camera-compensated surprise check, delegates uncertain regions to the compared merger, and falls back to it when all objects are lost. On long videos (LVOS V2 valid, pre-specified protocol, three ViT-S seeds), it beats ALGM 0.98 by +2.76 J&F (95% object CI [+1.22,+4.44]; video-cluster [+0.99,+4.52]) at 16% fewer counted encoder FLOPs (0.385 vs. 0.459), sitting 1.0 J&F point below the dense-encoder baseline; at ViT-B the gain over ALGM 0.95 is +3.89 [+2.03,+5.87]. On DAVIS 2017 val it stays within -0.19 J&F of the dense encoder at ViT-S (-0.12 at ViT-B). Controls qualify the result: protecting only the previous mask under ALGM matches or exceeds accuracy at higher FLOPs (0.486), so expectation and surprise mainly reduce counted encoder compute. Practically, lower counted encoder compute did not reduce startup-inclusive mean Cutie step time on a 12-video LVOS sample (3%-8% lower in steady state; an equally engineered ALGM is faster at ViT-S and ViT-B). Section 5.2 separately examines a Cutie permanent-memory schedule and its latency and memory trade-offs. Code: https://github.com/boletoms/focused-cv

Abstract (英文): Cross-modal place recognition (CMPR) matches natural language descriptions against databases of geo-tagged reference images, enabling localization without visual queries. Despite its potential for language-guided navigation, existing vision-language models lag far behind visual place recognition (VPR) baselines, even after task adaptation. Unlike general-purpose text-to-image retrieval, place recognition relies on subtle local cues to distinguish visually similar locations. We therefore hypothesize that a primary bottleneck in CMPR is capturing fine-grained correspondences between image regions and text details, which requires higher visual token granularity, strong text representation capacity, and local cross-modal matching. To address this challenge, we propose CrossPlace, a two-stage CMPR framework. Stage 1 performs global retrieval by pairing a jointly aligned DINOv2-B/14 visual backbone with a progressively fine-tuned BGE-Large text encoder, while Stage 2 refines top candidates using a lightweight token-level cross-attention reranker. Through systematic analysis, we isolate the drivers of retrieval accuracy and show that visual token density provides the largest performance improvement, while text encoder capacity and task fine-tuning offer complementary gains. To evaluate robustness under realistic conditions, we also extend CMPR evaluation to city-scale and severe weather and illumination settings. Across all existing and new benchmarks, CrossPlace achieves double-digit Recall@1 improvements over prior cross-modal methods, closes the gap to unimodal text retrieval, and narrows it to unimodal VPR. Code and benchmarks are available at https://github.com/oferidan1/CrossPlace

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual context is a powerful cue for object recognition: scenes inform what, where, and at what scale objects are likely to appear. Yet over-reliance on context becomes a failure mode when models predict objects based on their typical neighbors rather than the objects' own visual features, which we call typicality bias. While extensively studied in the pre-Transformer era, it has often been assumed that architectural and scale improvements would naturally solve it, and a systematic diagnostic across the modern vision stack remains absent. We provide such a diagnostic framework. We measure typicality bias via normalized pointwise mutual information (NPMI) over object pairs, and introduce STRANGE-Bench (Synthetic Typicality-RANked Grounded Evaluation Benchmark): a photorealistic, layout-controlled benchmark spanning 17 sub-tiers from typical to never-co-occurring object pairs. Across five model families (specialist detectors, open-vocabulary detectors, self-supervised, vision-language, and multimodal large language models) and three recognition tasks (supervised detection, open-vocabulary detection, and classification), the bias is prevalent: gaps of 5-11 p.p. persist when objects appear outside their typical company, and do not consistently shrink with scale. To mitigate it, we turn to data augmentation: existing augmentations are designed for visual diversity, not for breaking co-occurrence shortcuts, and sample from the very distribution that produces typicality bias. We propose Anchor-Atypicality Sampling (AAS), a simple sampling rule for paste-based augmentations that uses the NPMI signal to tilt the training-time co-occurrence distribution toward atypical partners. AAS reduces the typicality gap by up to 1.6 p.p. on natural-image COCO splits and 23.0 p.p. on synthetic STRANGE-Bench across three model families, without sacrificing overall performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): How an object deforms when it hits the ground reveals what it is made of. From a single real video of a drop, we estimate a deformable object's density-normalized stiffness $E/ρ$, and its yield stress $σ_Y/ρ$ when the impact causes plastic deformation, with a feed-forward predictor trained only in simulation. Real videos carry no material labels, so such predictors are developed and validated on synthetic data alone. We show that synthetic validation ranks their geometric inputs in the wrong order. Material acts through 3D deformation, and tracklets lifted to 3D with estimated depth are indeed the most accurate input in simulation. However, on real drops they are the least accurate, and better depth does not repair them: the failure persists with simulator depth in training, LiDAR depth at test time, and exact depth on rendered replays of the real drops. Short 2D optical-flow tracklets do the opposite: they improve nothing in simulation, yet they transfer best. Material is therefore better read from what perception measures consistently in both domains than from what is most informative in simulation. Our predictor, PhysReader, reads the video and 2D tracklets and learns the lift to 3D in simulation by matching a teacher that sees exact 3D deformation. To measure transfer, we introduce PhysReader-Real: 16 everyday objects dropped five times each, with reference materials fit jointly to each object's drops and checked against tensile tests. On PhysReader-Real, PhysReader is closer to the reference materials than every other input and baseline. The one exception is a per-scene optimizer started from PhysReader's prediction, which ends slightly closer but takes 49 minutes per video instead of 12 seconds.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Processing long videos with multimodal large language models (MLLMs) is prohibitively expensive, making informative frame selection essential for accurate video understanding. The default uniform sampling often misses task-relevant keyframes, while recent training-free selectors instead pick frames by how well each one matches the question under an image-text alignment (ITA) score. However, we show that ITA signals correspond poorly with the MLLM's true needs: their rankings correlate weakly with downstream accuracy and are even negatively correlated on over 40% of questions, whereas the MLLM's internal yes/no judgment yields a much stronger signal. We present ProbFaS, a training- and label-free probabilistic pipeline that fuses both signals for key frame selection. ProbFaS treats dense ITA scores as cheap, biased measurements and sparse MLLM judgments as accurate, costly ones, unifying them via co-kriging, a multi-fidelity Gaussian process, to calibrate relevance across all frames. A temporal determinantal point process then selects frames capturing relevance peaks while preserving temporal coverage. Across Video-MME, MLVU and LongVideoBench, ProbFaS is the most accurate among fourteen state-of-the-art training-free selectors and uniform sampling in 13 of 15 settings (8-128 frames), outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 3.1 percentage points. On MLVU and LongVideoBench, 32 ProbFaS frames match or surpass the top baseline at 128 frames and uniform sampling with up to 768 frames.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Real-time autoregressive video generators stream minutes of video and accept new prompts on the fly, but struggle to keep people, objects, and places consistent over a long interactive story. Agent systems provide such memory for offline multi-shot generation, yet they read the whole script in advance, plan each shot before generating it, and regenerate failed shots, which a live stream does not allow. We present Agentic LongLive, an asynchronous agentic memory for long video generation. A memory agent on separate GPUs reads only the prompts revealed so far, never the generated video, and keeps a text state and evolving images for every person, object, and scene. From this entity memory it composes a global image and a per-shot memory image, which an auxiliary GPU prefills into the generator's attention sink before the next shot starts, and writes entity cards for the prompts that name them, so the video never waits for memory and video errors are not written back. A small language model, trained with reinforcement learning and an image reward calibrated using downstream video outcomes, decides what to remember before the next prompt is known. On LongStory-Online, a new benchmark of 25 stories (857 shots) revealed one shot at a time, Agentic LongLive improves persistent-state accuracy of LongLive-2.0, its base generator, by 26.3 points, protagonist identity by 44.9, and revisited-place consistency by 40.4, at about 1% extra generation time on H100. The full system streams faster than real time on one 8xB200 node with the generator in BF16 (34.3 FPS), keeps image quality stable over 14-minute stories, and improves identity on two MiniMax-H3 generators, one of which, LongLive-H3, also runs in real time with it. To our knowledge, it is the first real-time video generator with text and image memory kept by an LLM agent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): While video diffusion models have grown faster and increasingly streamable, the video VAE remains a primary latency bottleneck across interactive generation and editing pipelines. Existing VAEs and their acceleration methods rely mainly on heavy convolutions or full frame attention, and most focus only on decoding, leaving encoding costly for streaming conditional generation and editing. This raises a natural question: Do video VAE encoding and decoding actually require such global spatiotemporal modeling? By analyzing the effective receptive fields of pretrained video VAEs, we find that both encoding and decoding a patch rely almost entirely on its spatial and temporal neighbors. To exploit this strong locality, we introduce LiveVAE, a streaming encoder and decoder built on causal window attention, which cuts per frame compute while keeping the temporal KV cache bounded for streaming. Decoupled encoder/decoder distillation followed by joint finetuning preserves the pretrained latent space, making LiveVAE a drop-in replacement for the original VAE. On MiniMax-H3, LiveVAE achieves 5.2-6.2x faster encoding and 3.0-3.3x faster decoding than the official VAE with higher PSNR. Paired with our dedicated inference infrastructure, LiveVAE pushes the speedup to 13.4x with almost no quality loss. Code and models will be released.

Abstract (英文): Diffusion models have shown strong potential for image matting by transferring generative priors learned from large-scale pretraining. However, existing approaches largely rely on latent diffusion models, whose compressed representations and VAE-based reconstruction introduce a mismatch with the high-frequency precision required for matting. We present PixelMatte, a framework that adapts pretrained pixel-space diffusion transformers for high-fidelity image matting. We show that strong image-conditioned matting capabilities can be acquired even from a pure text-to-image model without paired image-to-image pretraining. Our approach reuses the backbone's native patch embedding to jointly process the input image, trimap, and noisy alpha matte as spatially aligned tokens, enabling direct pixel-space prediction without additional condition encoders or adapters. With pixel-accurate synthetic supervision and real-image fine-tuning, PixelMatte predicts alpha mattes in a single forward pass. Two lightweight calibration modules, LayerShift and High-Pass Refiner, further improve regional opacity accuracy and boundary fidelity. Experiments across four benchmarks and two distinct pixel diffusion backbones demonstrate substantial improvements over existing methods, reducing SAD and gradient errors by 24-44% relative to RenderMatte on P3M-500-NP and AM-2K, while achieving competitive performance on AIM-500 and RenderMatte-2K. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of pixel-space generative priors for precision-sensitive image matting. Project page: https://white-yc.github.io/pixelmatte/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A single casual video leaves a moving scene under-determined: a point seen once from each place can slide toward or away from the camera and still match every frame. We constrain the reconstruction with the structure of the motion itself. Building on DynMF, we model a scene's motion as a handful of SE(3) trajectories, each rigid, and let every point follow one of them alone, never a blend. DynMF2 computes this factorization directly from 3D point trajectories, with no rendering, by Expectation-Maximization (EM). Its M-step fits, in closed form, every rigid motion to the points assigned to it, and its E-step reassigns every point to the motion that explains it with the least error. The background is simply one more motion, the camera's, so no masks are needed. Three tools then revise the factorization: requery adds trajectories where the rendering shows parts the tracker missed, merge joins motions that move together, and split separates those that do not. Thus the number of motions follows the scene. Without masks, DynMF2 clearly outperforms the mask-based baselines in view synthesis on iPhone and NVIDIA and in 3D tracking on iPhone. It recovers what masks leave out, from thin parts and details to small movers far in the background. Its motions segment the moving parts of MOVi-F and iTACO scenes competitively, although DynMF2 is never trained to segment. What moves in a scene is not decided in advance: it emerges from the rigid motions themselves.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present MEMO (a Mesh for Every MOment), a method that reconstructs the geometry of complete dynamic scenes, captured by multiple synchronized cameras, and extracts a mesh at every frame. Existing dynamic Gaussian splatting methods either model appearance alone or target isolated objects segmented by foreground masks, and all of them recover meshes by TSDF fusion or Poisson reconstruction, whose fixed grids limit the detail they can capture. At the core of MEMO is a per-Gaussian motion model inspired by Gaussian processes, which represents each Gaussian's position and rotation over time as a combination of temporal Gaussian kernels. The model keeps every Gaussian a valid surface element throughout the sequence and carries an implicit representation of the dynamic geometry, so a mesh can be derived at any time from a field defined by the Gaussians. Although common in real captures, large and fast motion poses a challenge that dynamic Gaussian splatting methods usually overlook. To account for it, we design a track-based initialization scheme that fits each Gaussian's initial trajectory to point tracks in closed form. We evaluate MEMO on Blender-Moments, a new benchmark of nine animated scenes with ground-truth meshes at every frame, and on two real-world datasets. MEMO reconstructs the most accurate geometry among state-of-the-art methods, and remains competitive in novel-view synthesis and training time. Project page: https://memo-4d.github.io

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present LuxAvatar, a feed-forward framework for real-time head avatar animation and relighting from a single portrait. To achieve both 3D controllability and complex facial light transport, we factorize avatar synthesis into an animatable 3D intrinsic Gaussian proxy and a causal autoregressive neural shader. This proxy disentangles canonical geometry and reflectance attributes, rapidly rasterizing deformed Gaussians into view- and motion-consistent PBR buffers. Conditioned on these buffers, our neural shader formulates forward rendering as a conditional video diffusion process, bypassing explicit PBR computation to synthesize realistic shading effects under target illumination. We achieve real-time streaming through heterogeneous distillation from a pretrained multi-step bidirectional relighting teacher to a two-step causal autoregressive neural shader, inheriting broad synthetic lighting priors while minimizing conditioning and decoding latency. To further specialize these priors to human heads, we construct a hybrid relighting dataset for domain-specific fine-tuning, capturing authentic facial dynamics and physically grounded light transport. Extensive experiments show that our framework enables 3D-controllable, real-time portrait animation and relighting, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in visual fidelity, motion control, and lighting accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image editing models can follow a wide range of instructions, but video editing models still lag behind, limited in part by the difficulty of collecting paired source and edited videos. Is it possible to transfer advanced image editing capabilities to video editors without new paired videos? We introduce Image-to-Video Transfer (I2VT), which improves pretrained video editors and expands them to new editing tasks using an image editor. It enables efficient post-training with no architectural changes and no new videos, using only individual images. Our key idea is to let the video editor role-play as an image editor: it edits an image as a one-frame video, with the image editor guiding the video editor's predictions at each denoising step. Although the video editor sees only single images during training, at test time it applies the improved and expanded capabilities to whole video clips while preserving temporal consistency. To push the boundaries of video editing, we introduce OpenVE++, an extension of OpenVE-Bench that adds four new editing tasks useful for video production, including action changes, expression changes, body movements, and scene relighting. Our experiments across three different video editors show that I2VT improves existing editing capabilities and expands them to new tasks, and our models achieve state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on both OpenVE-Bench and OpenVE++.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A general-purpose Vision-Language Model (VLM) has surpassed expert vision models in many domains such as object detection and video recognition. However, point correspondence is known to be challenging for VLMs since it goes beyond object-level recognition: it requires pixel-level localization of the same physical surface point across observations, even when appearance changes or the point disappears and later reappears. In this paper, we introduce AgenTAP, a VLM-based agent that directly predicts point coordinates and visibility and actively reasons over videos. Starting from its own estimate, the agent autonomously chooses what to inspect and uses the resulting evidence to verify point identity and refine its predictions. With GPT-6 Astra, it surpasses expert vision models in both point localization and visibility prediction, without correspondence-specific fine-tuning. We further use AgenTAP to re-identify physical points after disappearance and reconnect fragmented tracks, constructing EgoTAP-4D, a dataset of 3D point trajectories in long real-world egocentric videos. On sampled reappearing points, its predictions approach human agreement, and adding EgoTAP-4D to D4RT training improves average 3D tracking performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Camera-controlled video generation re-renders a video along a target camera trajectory, yet moving the camera around an object, the most natural way to change the viewpoint of an object-centric scene, remains difficult. Existing methods condition either on camera parameters, which are scale-ambiguous in monocular video, or on explicit geometry such as warped point clouds or depth, which breaks down under large viewpoint changes. We present AnyCam, a method that represents the camera with an anchor object in the scene: we reconstruct it in 3D and render it along the target trajectory, a camera signal that is scale-aware, view-complete and easy to specify relative to the object. A video diffusion model transfers the content and dynamics of the source video to the new viewpoint. Its H-Concat layout aligns source and target frames in time, so the model can be distilled into AnyCam-AR, a streaming few-step generator for on-the-fly camera control. Both models are trained on paired videos with pixel-aligned anchor renderings from multi-camera videos and static-scene captures. Experiments on diverse datasets and trajectories show that AnyCam performs favorably against camera-controlled video generation and 3D reconstruction methods in camera control and visual quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Gross primary productivity (GPP) stability is crucial for dryland carbon sinks, yet how climatic and anthropogenic proxy factors are associated with stability across spatiotemporal scales remains poorly understood. Using satellite-derived annual GPP from 1982 to 2018, we combined ensemble empirical mode decomposition with Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) and SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to quantify interannual, trend, and overall stability and assess variable importance, nonlinear associations, and local contributions. Results showed that: (1) Variable importance was strongly timescale-dependent. Interannual stability was most strongly associated with shortwave radiation trend (SRAD_SLOPE) and mean population density (POP_MEAN); trend stability with temperature variability (TEM_CV), SRAD_SLOPE, and POP_MEAN; and overall stability with SRAD_SLOPE and TEM_CV. Locally, SRAD_SLOPE (34.27%) and POP_MEAN (21.85%) were the main variables of interannual stability, TEM_CV (44.22%) of trend stability, and SRAD_SLOPE (50.68%) and TEM_CV (24.32%) of overall stability. (2) Associations varied across dimensions. Increasing precipitation was associated with higher trend stability but lower interannual and overall stability; SRAD_SLOPE was negatively associated with all three dimensions; and elevation, represented by the digital elevation model (DEM), showed opposite associations with trend stability and the other two dimensions. POP_MEAN showed a nonlinear association with trend stability, while forests and croplands were more closely associated with interannual and trend stability, respectively. Among land-cover trajectory types, no-significant-change trajectories in forests and grasslands were associated with higher interannual stability, whereas decrease-to-increase trajectories were associated with higher trend stability across all land-cover types. (3) Local SHAP patterns were spatially heterogeneous. SRAD_SLOPE and TEM_CV showed broadly similar patterns across dimensions, whereas PPT_SLOPE and DEM showed contrasting patterns between trend and interannual stability. These findings highlight the need to consider both temporal dimensions and spatial context when attributing dryland GPP stability and may inform future carbon-sink management.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many failures of deployed AI arise not from weak inference but from a mismatch between the state a task depends on and the context available when the system acts. Relevant user state, repository revisions, tool traces, sensor readings, or environment changes may be unavailable, distorted, or poorly selected. We call the effort to reduce this bottleneck context scaling. We frame a model as $P_θ(d\mid c)$ within a system governed by three budgets: training $B_θ$, decision-time $B_d$, and context-infrastructure $B_c$. Pretraining scaling invests in $θ$; decision-time scaling searches, refines, or verifies decisions under supplied context; context scaling improves the system's capacity to acquire, represent, deliver, evaluate, and persist deployment state for model use. The three programs interact, but only $B_c$ targets what the system can make available before a decision. This view makes the model-infrastructure pair the unit of analysis. We define effective-context value as a task- and intervention-dependent attribution, separate deployment state from model-visible context, and identify availability, fidelity, selection, and utilization failures. We then organize current mechanisms through a five-operation context lifecycle and propose controlled budget interventions and utility-cost curves for evaluation. The resulting agenda centers on grounded reporting, interaction-data and full-cost infrastructure, and safety and governance for persistent, adaptive systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When an LLM or VLM produces several candidate answers, picking the best one usually relies on the model's own confidence, a learned judge model, or a domain check. Each has a known weakness: an answer can be fluent but insecure, mathematically plausible but physically infeasible, or rated highly by a judge while quietly breaking a task requirement. We propose a scoring framework that combines a small set of hard checks every answer must pass with a wider set of softer checks whose scores are blended into one number per candidate. This adaptive scoring framework consists of three components: a drafting model that produces the candidates, a configuring model that selects and weights the checks for each task, and scoring models that rate properties no grounded tool can compute. We apply our proposed method to four tasks: writing code that is both correct and secure (CWEval), generating robot manipulation plans (LIBERO with added safety variants), passing competitive-programming hidden tests (LiveCodeBench), and answering questions from retrieved documents (HotpotQA). An ablation study isolates the contribution of each component and finds that the largest gains come from combining hard checks with candidate comparison: on tasks where the first sampled answer is often broken or unsafe, this pairing lifts joint correctness-and-safety by up to +0.21 (retrieval-augmented QA) and code-generation safety by up to +0.28 (CWEval), with the size of the gain tracking how weak the first sample is, falling to near zero or slightly negative where it is already strong. The per-task layer that selects and weights the softer checks adds no measurable value over simpler alternatives. This builds a unified scoring framework, together with an ablation that identifies the grounded hard checks, not the soft-factor configuration, as the source of the gain.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI systems are increasingly capable of producing machine-checked Lean proofs of advanced mathematical results. Real mathematical formalization, however, is not isolated theorem proving: it requires building the definitions and intermediate lemmas that connect a result to a formal library. Existing evaluations often provide isolated theorem statements in settings where much of this supporting theory is already available in Mathlib. We introduce AutoformBench, a benchmark of 341 Lean 4 formalization tasks drawn from 24 advanced mathematical sources. Lean-experienced mathematicians formalized and audited each target statement, leaving the corresponding Lean proof to the evaluated agent. Each problem is embedded in a broader mathematical development: agents receive the informal proof but must translate it into Lean while constructing the missing definitions, intermediate lemmas, and supporting infrastructure. We compare the performance of frontier models on these problems. We find a largely shared difficulty frontier: success depends less on mathematical domain than on models' ability to construct missing formal infrastructure. Data and code at https://github.com/facebookresearch/autoformbench

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI configuration tuning searches for settings that improve the performance of AI systems under a limited budget. Existing tuners use different sampling and search policies, making the choice of decision strategy central to both tuning quality and cost. We investigate whether general-purpose decision models can guide tuning, using Jev as a deployed representative that returns structured choices and numeric scores across diverse tasks. To assess where it helps, we compare its use as an optimizer, performance predictor, and region guider against conventional methods and a large language model (LLM) on eight AI tuning tasks. Conventional optimizers attain rank 1 in six of eight comparisons. Similarly, established predictors outperform Jev in seven. Through region guidance, however, it finds configurations with lower perplexity than those selected by the native tuner, offering a quality gain when additional decision time and API fees are acceptable. These findings yield actionable recommendations on selecting the decision role, retaining conventional predictors, and balancing tuning quality against cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support raises important safety concerns. Existing guard models primarily identify categories of harmful content, often overlooking the context needed to determine an appropriate response. We introduce a clinically grounded taxonomy that characterizes harm along three dimensions: what the harm is, who is involved, and when it occurs. It further separates harm characterization from intervention decisions, thus enabling safety policies to be specified outside the model weights through explicit, clinician-curated instructions that can be updated and audited. We validate the taxonomy with expert clinicians and validate a panel of LLM judges to scale annotation. Relabeling public benchmarks in this taxonomy shows that they rarely contain harm directed at the user, which accounts for nearly half of the harm in real conversations. We then train MindGuard2, a 35B-A3B reasoning-based guard model using supervised fine-tuning on short reasoning traces followed by reinforcement learning with a reasoning-length penalty, to minimize inference overhead while preserving the benefits of reasoning. On two public benchmarks and real-world conversations, MindGuard2 achieves balanced accuracy comparable to the substantially larger GLM-5.3 at approximately 10x lower median latency, advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier. Furthermore, it achieves low false-positive rates at high recall, helping balance the need to detect unsafe situations against the risk of unnecessary interventions that may disrupt support and undermine user trust. We release a safety guardrail model with SOTA performance trained on synthetic data, together with synthetic conversations and annotations of public benchmarks in our taxonomy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Procedurally generated logic problems are used to both evaluate and train reasoning in language models. Their difficulty is usually set by planting a proof, that is, by sampling a proof of the desired depth and stating its premises as the problem. However, a planted proof is only one possible proof. A problem can also have shorter proofs, or several unrelated ones, and its stated depth then overstates what solving it requires. We measure what a problem actually requires, relative to a stated set of inference rules. For each problem, we compute the steps every proof must make, the shortest proof, the number of minimal proofs and the indispensable premises, and export certificates that an independent checker verifies. Audits of eight datasets find depth that is forced (ProofWriter, Blocksworld), problems labelled depth 5 that require about two specific rule applications (FLDx2), and answers that need no particular step (ZebraLogic). We then construct problems whose every proof has a requested structure, using a catalog of shortcuts proved once per rule set. Every constructed problem is checked in full and comes with its own certificate. In training, the best data mixes depths and problem types. At equal size, such mixtures of certified problem types, including model-checking problems that forward generation does not produce, transfer better than FLDx2-style derivation data for Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 8B.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) posits that AI models converge toward a shared representation of the world, even when trained on different data modalities. Previous work has found evidence for the PRH in the activations of vision and language models. In this work, we ask whether vision and language models also converge in how they act on these representations: precisely, in how these internal representations affect the models' output. At a chosen layer of a transformer, the Jacobian lens gives every word in the vocabulary (or class in an image classifier) a vector in the hidden state: the vector that most increases the model's tendency to generate that word, at that position or later, or to predict that class. Collectively, these vectors make up what is referred to as the model's J-space. We find evidence of the PRH in J-space across vision and language models, and furthermore, we find that J-spaces mostly converge up to a rotation. That is, a single rotation, fitted on a subset of words, approximately maps one model's J-space onto another's, and so applies to words and classes it was not fitted on. A word's vector, rotated from a text-only language model's J-space into the visual patch tokens of a vision-language model, changes the object the vision-language model says is in the image (and only in the image region where it is added). Rotated into an image generator's text conditioning, a word's vector changes the object in the generated image. Rotated into an ImageNet classifier (trained without text), it raises that object's class score above almost all others, and vice versa. This suggests that models converge not only in how they represent the world, but also in how they act on it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A language model can name a brand without classifying it correctly. This study measures these outcomes separately for 1,000 B2B SaaS brands across 333 product categories. On Gemma 4 12B, with no external retrieval, category prompts elicit 510 brands (51.0%). A separate five-candidate category probe ranks the reference label first for 787 brands, a distractor first for 194, and yields little separation for 19. Among the 981 separated rankings, the error rate is 19.8%. It falls to 3.5% for the 547 brands with a winner-to-runner-up log-probability margin above 0.6. These are within-cohort measurements, not calibrated confidence estimates. Exploratory regressions associate technical coverage with recall and an owned-properties composite with category accuracy, but the composites use different adjustment sets and do not establish causal effects. Across four Gemma generations, measured recall is 39.8%, 44.3%, 51.1% and 51.0%. A separate representation-based sentiment measure has little linear association with customer ratings (r=0.03, n=921). The findings support reporting recall and category accuracy separately. Failure to elicit a brand does not establish its absence from model parameters or training data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents built on large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong capabilities in automated decision-making. However, LLMs may produce incorrect answers with high confidence, limiting their applicability in decisions that affect individuals and organizations. Reliable confidence scores are necessary to gate such decisions, and existing estimates are frequently unavailable or too coarse to support effective gating. This work uses supervised learning to train models to predict the correctness of LLM-generated answers, enabling automated confident responses and routing uncertain ones for human review. A linear confidence score head is fitted to the final-layer hidden state of the frozen LLM backbone, and text-only metamodels are trained as an alternative for closed models. All results come from a single 2B-parameter backbone, on graded answers that are about 98% SQuAD and the remainder TruthfulQA. Against multiple baselines, the confidence score head outperforms all baselines and metamodels, automating the largest fraction of the workload at high accuracy targets. Prompted confidence scores can appear well calibrated yet lack the resolution needed for thresholding. Although transferability to unseen datasets is modest, the confidence score head remains the most robust approach. The proposed confidence scoring framework enhances the safety of automating LLM-based agents and can be adapted to gate other properties, including safety or compliance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI models vary enormously in capability and price. Model routing exploits this by sending easier tasks to cheaper models. However, a single agentic task often combines easy and difficult subtasks, making one model choice for the entire task inefficient. We introduce partial routing, which guides a frontier lead model to delegate suitable subtasks to a cheaper worker model. From both models' past runs, we identify recurring subtasks and measure each model's quality and cost on them. These measurements produce delegation guidance, with a parameter, $δ$, controlling how much is delegated. The lead decides when and what to delegate during execution, without training either model. On held-out tasks from ViBench and Terminal-Bench 4.0, partial routing achieves higher mean task performance than the lead alone and task-level routing. With $δ= 0.75$, it raises the lead's ViBench score from 70.2 to 78.5 at 19% lower cost, and its Terminal-Bench pass rate from 54% to 61% at similar cost. Giving the lead only the subtask breakdown, without a worker, also reduces cost on both benchmarks and raises the Terminal-Bench pass rate. These results show that analysis of past runs can support more efficient planning and more effective delegation, with little cost and implementation overhead.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Internet of Things (IoT) systems generate low-level sensor observations that are not directly suitable for process analysis and must first be transformed into semantically meaningful events. This paper investigates collaborative language models as a zero-shot semantic abstraction layer for converting such observations into higher-level event and activity labels. We propose a five-stage workflow comprising semantic mapping, semantic aggregation, temporal and contextual aggregation, specialized reasoning, and final event abstraction. The resulting labels and contextual information can subsequently be represented as event records for downstream Process Mining (PM) tasks. The approach is evaluated through Human Activity Recognition on the CASAS Aruba smart home dataset using eleven workflow configurations. The experiments show that collaboration alone does not guarantee improved performance: general-purpose auxiliary SLMs can degrade recognition, whereas assigning complementary specialist roles leads to better results and allows a smaller final classifier to retain much of the performance of a larger model. The selected workflow achieves a macro F1 of 0.643 in the 1.5-day comparison and 0.567 (weighted F1 0.680) in a one-week evaluation. Although trained ML approaches remain more accurate on this dataset, the results demonstrate a different design point: modular semantic event abstraction using off-the-shelf models without task-specific parameter fine-tuning. The findings indicate that the structure of LM collaboration and task decomposition is more important than simply increasing the number of participating models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present an end-to-end empirical study of dynamic reasoning in next-generation detection and response pipelines, evaluating how static classifiers, graph-based signal fusion, and large language model (LLM) triage agents perform under strict human-analyst attention budgets (Recall at B alerts/day). Across 56 reproducible experiments spanning a multi-layer enterprise-and-cloud simulator and four public datasets (SOREL-20M, BODMAS, LANL, and DARPA Transparent Computing), we test twelve architectural claims governing modern fleet-scale defense. First, we show that fleet growth halves recall at a fixed analyst budget even at negligible compute cost (<$0.0003 per million events), and that rarity-weighted graph risk propagation outperforms GraphSAGE without neural training. Second, evaluating frontier reasoning models (Gemini) and fine-tuned open-weight models (Qwen3-4B) for alert triage reveals that unconstrained LLM auto-closure eliminates 90% of false positives while silently discarding 31-44% of true attack cases. Third, post-training with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) causes severe reward hacking on unseen attack classes, suppressing up to 86% of novel attacks compared to two-epoch supervised fine-tuning. Finally, against an adaptive adversary generating plausible cover stories in benign telemetry fields, LLM triage evasion rises sharply unless gated by a deterministic policy harness prior to model invocation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) typically optimises evidence selection for semantic relevance, whereas compliance-sensitive long-form generation also requires evidence supporting explicit structural obligations. Semantically relevant retrieval may therefore omit information needed for mandatory downstream clauses. We propose Constraint-Aligned RAG (CA-RAG), a retrieval-conditioning framework that augments section-specific queries with ontology-derived structural constraints and policy-grounded knowledge-graph relations before evidence selection. Retrieval-only evaluation with manually annotated evidence shows consistent improvements across retrieval budgets. At $k=6$, CA-RAG increases Precision@$k$ from $0.5268$ to $0.5692$, pooled Recall@$k$ from $0.5402$ to $0.5950$, and F1@$k$ from $0.5389$ to $0.5672$ over standard RAG. Generation experiments further show improvements in compliance and reliability in several model--domain settings, while generation-only constraints achieve higher clause coverage but lower overall compliance and reliability. These results indicate that structurally aligned retrieval can improve evidence sufficiency in compliance-sensitive long-form generation.

Abstract (英文): AI agents that operate in the real world must reason correctly and on-time, yet they currently do not account for how time should alter their reasoning. Modern agents learn to do tasks in environments that are entirely synchronous: where the world waits for the agent to act before changing. A real-time environment, by contrast, changes independent of the agent. Existing approaches try to bridge this gap by imposing a ratio of thinking to acting. However, for an agent to succeed in real-time it must learn to act quickly when delay is costly and to reason longer when the benefit outweighs the missed opportunities. To this end, we introduce real-time reasoning training (RRT) as the class of training techniques for real-time reasoning agents. We develop three standard RRT techniques: (1) using real-time data, (2) decoupling acting and observing, and (3) curriculum learning in time. Together, these simple techniques substantially outperform strong baselines. Across time pressures, we outperform the strongest non-RRT baseline by an average of 33% reward on in-distribution and 55% reward on out-of-distribution tasks in two real-time environments: Frogger and Snake. These results demonstrate that we can teach agents to balance reasoning and responsiveness under real-time constraints, and that RRT opens a broad, new set of capabilities for real-time agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Can we distinguish fully AI-generated reviews from those using AI only to rewrite human judgement? Furthermore, can we do so reliably when reviews are generated from diverse AI models and workflows across scientific domains? To this end, we introduce PeerReveal, a new detector that grounds its decisions in learned relationships between a review's claims and reference claims drawn from AI-generated reviews of the same paper, complemented by review-level textual signals. We evaluate PeerReveal on PeerShift, a new, challenging test set of 5,430 reviews spanning multiple domains, including machine learning, life sciences, and social sciences, combining variation in disciplinary review conventions with diverse AI review-production mechanisms. With regular training, PeerReveal achieves 70.52% pooled Macro-F1 on this benchmark, improving over the strongest baseline by 11.48 points. We develop an effective and practical adversarial training framework that further improves the detector's generalization using a small local generator optimized to produce challenging reviews, raising pooled Macro-F1 from 70.52% to 82.40%. These results show that robust AI-review detection benefits from richer review-level evidence and adversarial training on more challenging generation processes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automated Scientific Hypothesis Generation (ASHG) leveraging large language models (LLMs) aims to harness their strengths in knowledge integration, logical reasoning, and text generation for efficiently producing innovative, verifiable scientific hypotheses. However, existing approaches face three critical limitations: (i) insufficient fine-grained knowledge representation, as unstructured context impairs LLM comprehension and reasoning; (ii) lack of knowledge evolution traceability, constraining the generation of innovative cross-domain conjectures; (iii) absence of human-inspired and role-specialized multi-agent collaboration, failing to leverage the efficient innovation mechanisms arising from cognitive diversity and division of labor among expert roles to complete the full ASHG-evaluation loop. To address these limitations, we propose the Fine-grained Inspiration Graph-empowered Multi-Agent Collaboration framework (FIG-MAC) including: (i) Fine-grained Inspiration Graph module (FIG), to obtain fine-grained structured knowledge representations, where nodes represent academic semantic units and edges encode heuristic relationships among them; (ii) ontology-guided hybrid reasoning module, which follows the FIG ontology to construct structured reasoning paths and uses vector search to enrich them with semantic evidence, to obtain the traceable scientific conjecture paths; (iii) role-specialized Multi-Agent framework, inspired by real scientific teamwork, enabling a closed-loop process of scientific conjecture generation, screening, and validation. Experimental results demonstrate that FIG-MAC significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on source diversity, provenance-adjusted novelty, and raw novelty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Embedding models increasingly inform how people search, recommend, and retrieve information, yet we lack credible ways to test whether these similarity judgments reflect how humans understand meaning. Existing benchmarks typically rely on static, expert-labeled pairs with coarse Likert-style annotations, limiting scalability and making it difficult to obtain consistent, high-confidence annotations. We introduce Aligned Machine, an interactive, public-facing platform that transforms broad participation into a continuously growing Human-Aligned Similarity Benchmark (HASB) for evaluating semantic alignment in embedding-based systems. Participants complete a cognitively-natural, forced-choice task, comparing two pairs of items (text or images) and selecting which pair is more similar. This comparative design is accessible to non-experts, yields clean preference data, and supports robust aggregation. We report results from an initial deployment with 115 participants contributing 2,875 total labeled pairs and 872 unique pairs. Using HASB, we evaluated 12 widely used embedding models and found only partial alignment between embedding similarity and human judgments. Text-only models consistently achieve higher overall agreement than multimodal models, while multimodal performance varies systematically by task type: alignment is strongest for image-image comparisons, weaker for image-text comparisons, and lowest for text-text comparisons. Aligned Machine is designed to evolve over time as participation grows, supporting longitudinal analysis, domain-specific studies, bias detection, and measurement of concept drift across model versions. By combining accessible public participation with rigorous comparative evaluation, HASB provides a scalable and accountable framework for auditing semantic alignment in embedding models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Numerical cognition is a simple yet fundamental ability that remains challenging for modern vision-language models. Meanwhile, number sense is already present in numerically naïve animals, suggesting that it can emerge without explicit numerical learning. Deep neural networks provide a related observation, with numerosity-selective units appearing after training on unrelated natural-image tasks and even before any training. However, these findings leave unresolved whether visual learning contributes beyond the spontaneous emergence of numerical selectivity. Human development provides a clue, as children become better at making judgments in which numerosity and proportion conflict. Here, we introduce a geometric framework to trace how representations of these two quantities change across learning. Although untrained and pretrained networks exhibited similar unit-level numerosity selectivity and basic comparison performance, natural-image pretraining increased the angle between numerosity and proportion subspaces while reducing their overlap, supporting more robust readout under conflict. Direct manipulation of these geometric properties further demonstrated their functional contributions to decoding accuracy and functional independence. Importantly, conventional supervised pretraining was not essential for quantity separation, which also emerged through self-supervised learning without category labels and synthetic-image training without natural-image exposure. Together, our findings suggest that structured visual learning can refine spontaneously emerging number sense into robust and functionally independent quantity representations without explicit numerical training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Computer-aided design (CAD) provides the geometric foundation for rotors in fluid rotating machinery. However, creating models for different rotors requires different modeling processes, making it difficult to control geometry through design intent and reuse existing models. We introduce VaneCraft, an agent-driven framework for rotor generation, inverse recovery, and editing through a unified analytical representation. The agent interprets design requests and invokes geometric modeling operations. The representation defines geometric controls through meridional geometry and independent boundary angle laws. An anisotropic partial differential equation (PDE) maps these controls to the blade surfaces. Geometric parameter correction (GPC) uses a joint geometric encoder and a parameter decoder to refine controls initialized from rotor category and dimensions. Inverse recovery maps existing CAD to the same geometric controls for reconstruction and continued editing. Experiments demonstrate that VaneCraft supports anisotropy control and parameter editing and outperforms representative baselines in rotor CAD generation tasks. Project page is available on \href{https://github.com/shenchen333/vanecraft}{GitHub}.

Abstract (英文): AI coding agents write code faster than people can review it, so teams add AI reviewers. Reasoning models let such a reviewer trade cost for diligence through an effort setting. We asked how much effort a reviewer needs to catch plausible-but-wrong patches, which pass a benchmark's tests but fail stronger ones. Claude Opus 5.5 (preregistered), Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 (exploratory) each reviewed the same 90 agent-written Django patches from SWE-bench Verified at low, medium and high effort. By strengthened SWE-ABS tests, 55 patches were wrong and 35 correct. In every model, high effort accepted fewer wrong patches than low (false success .45 against .67 for Opus, .49 against .65 for Sonnet, .51 against .78 for Haiku), a supported difference for Opus and Haiku. For Opus, medium was non-inferior to high within 15 percentage points (difference .018, 95% CI [-.065, .108]) and cost 20% less, short of the preregistered 30%. For Sonnet and Haiku, medium cost 39% and 51% less than high but was not shown non-inferior. Opus at medium or high agreed best with the labels; Haiku at high cost about 8% as much per review as Opus at medium, accuracy difference unresolved. Exploratory coding showed that most rejections named the tested defect and that effort added such rejections. No setting is a strong gate: each accepted at least 45% of wrong patches. For 32 of the 55 wrong patches the issue may not require what the failing test checks; without them, neither accuracy hypothesis holds for any model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Adaptive network slicing in Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) demands reconfiguration decisions that respond to live traffic conditions while remaining safe with respect to Service Level Agreement (SLA) constraints, a combination that purely data-driven controllers cannot reliably guarantee without continuous retraining. We present SlicePlanner, a logic-guided closed-loop framework that treats each RAN reconfiguration as a controlled hypothesis: proposed from live and historical KeyPerformance Indicator (KPI) evidence, filtered through explicit SLA constraints and mined RAN control rules, and committed only when post-action measurements confirm improvement. SlicePlanner comprises two cooperating agents: SliceSense, which monitors live KPIs, retrieves context-conditioned evidence, and ranks candidate RAN actions through a surrogate-guided admissibility filter; and SliceGuard, which validates post-action KPI responses, rolls back harmful configurations, and refreshes rules under distribution drift. Validated over the air using OpenAirInterface, FlexRIC, and commercial Sierra Wireless 5G devices, SlicePlanner reduces SLA latency violations by up to 86% over static slicing and 50% over Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL)-based slicing, while maintaining comparable cell throughput and auditable decisions without online retraining.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in LLM-assisted theorem proving and program verification have attracted widespread attention. Beyond determining whether a proposition is true, LLM agents must produce evidence that can be independently checked by humans or formal verification tools. To evaluate the underlying, protocol-agnostic ability of LLM agents to construct verifiable proofs-rather than their proficiency with any particular proof language or verification tool, we introduce Merlin-Bench. Drawing on interactive proof systems from computational complexity theory, we cast an LLM agent as the prover: under a designated protocol, the agent interacts with a formal verifier to substantiate its claimed answer to a problem instance. We develop an automated framework for constructing test cases and use it to create 100 cases across 15 problem families, each formulated within this prover-verifier framework. Experiments with 10 frontier LLMs across multiple agent harnesses show that reliably producing verifiable proofs in accordance with specified protocols remains a substantial challenge. Our case studies further provide actionable insights into improving agents' ability to generate verifier-accepted proofs: tool use is essential for completing practical proof tasks; different forms of guidance, ranging from engineering instructions to problem-specific knowledge, substantially affect success rates on interactive-proof tasks; and proof-solving experience distilled from abstract tasks can transfer to concrete, real-world verification tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a large language model (LLM) evicts prompt keys and values at the end of prefill, it must decide what to keep before it sees the queries from subsequent decoding steps. Generating a draft continuation can approximate those queries at the cost of extra autoregressive steps. Can we use the embedding of the first output token, already available from the final prefill logits, to construct low-cost pseudo queries that approximate future decoding queries? We start with FTQ-8, which places eight copies of the first token ID at consecutive future positions and reads their attention to the prompt in one causal forward pass. Each copy sees a different prefix, but a generic opening such as the offers a poor literal guess for seven successive tokens. Faster and Better Query (FBQ) preserves the first embedding and learns an additive offset at each of the seven remaining positions. We train these offsets to predict the average prompt attention of a full-cache continuation. On LongBench, FBQ performs comparably to eight-step Lookahead Q-Cache at the same cache budget, using a single forward pass to compute all probe queries.

</details>
