Abstract (英文): Hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often arise from visual assumptions that go unchecked. Self-reflection, multi-agent debate, and counterfactual verification can reinforce erroneous conclusions when they rely on the same unverified visual assumptions. External visual tools can provide verifiable evidence, but models may select the wrong tools, supply incorrect parameters, or ignore or contradict the evidence they obtain. In this work, we propose EVA, an Evidence-seeking Visual Agent that reframes multimodal reasoning as an explicit process of evidence acquisition and verification. At the core of EVA is a unified evidence-grounded execution pipeline designed to address the above challenges through two tightly coupled mechanisms. First, we introduce Iterative Tool Replanning, which dynamically refines tool selection and parameters based on intermediate observations, improving the reliability of tool usage. Second, we develop Evidence Consistency Verification, an answer validation mechanism that improves alignment between the final prediction and the collected evidence, mitigating evidence--reasoning inconsistencies. In addition, to balance robustness and efficiency, EVA incorporates Selective Evidence Routing, an adaptive fast--slow reasoning strategy that determines when external evidence is necessary. Visually straightforward queries are handled via direct reasoning, while complex or high-risk cases trigger tool-based evidence acquisition. By jointly addressing how to reliably acquire evidence and how to use it consistently, EVA transforms multimodal reasoning from passive answer validation into explicit evidence-backed decision making. Experimental results demonstrate that EVA achieves significantly improved reasoning reliability over direct VLM inference and multi-agent methods, while maintaining efficiency through selective tool invocation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skill learning has become an important way to improve agent performance. However, agent trajectories often contain sensitive information and cannot be shared across clients, hindering collaborative skill learning. To tackle this issue, federated skill learning (FSL) has emerged as a solution by distilling trajectories into compact skill representations and uploading them to a central server for sharing. However, existing FSL methods typically share skills based on skill similarity without considering their contributions to heterogeneous tasks across clients, thereby degrading agent performance. To address this challenge, we propose P-FedSkill, a personalized FSL framework that comprises three key components: local skill extraction (LSE), personalized skill selection (PSS), and task-aware skill routing (TASR). Specifically, LSE first extracts skill patches (i.e., task applicability conditions and execution strategies) and task execution outcomes from trajectories. PSS then uses these outcomes to evaluate each skill patch's performance on client tasks and incorporates its task applicability condition to construct a personalized skill set for each client. Finally, TASR adaptively selects a task-relevant subset of skill patches based on their applicability conditions to guide agent execution. We further provide a theoretical guarantee for personalized skill selection in P-FedSkill, and extensive experiments also demonstrate its effectiveness.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly being explored for automated engineering design. However, repairing power-converter designs that fail engineering validation remains challenging: the system must identify physical causes, locate responsible design variables, and resolve violations without compromising other constraints. We introduce PowerFix, a physics-guided iterative repair framework that converts validation failures into structured diagnoses and executable patches while preserving topology and design requirements. PowerDiagGraph links failed checks to possible physical causes and constrains repair routes. An LLM prioritizes these routes, while a deterministic compiler translates selected repairs into authorized field-level edits. Candidates are validated on isolated design copies and committed only after all required engineering checks pass; failed validation can guide replanning. On our constructed 96-task flyback repair benchmark, comprising injected single faults, coupled faults, and held-out natural failures from our design generator, PowerFix achieves 78.7% verified repair success rate (VRSR), compared with 30.1% for the strongest of three LLM baselines. Three additional replanning rounds increase VRSR to 88.4%. These offline results provide pilot-stage evidence within a simulation-based design workflow, with hardware prototype validation and transfer to a second converter topology as the next steps toward deployment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-paper-to-slide generation (multi-PTSG) is the task of constructing a unified presentation from multiple research papers by selecting, organizing, and synthesizing information across sources. This task introduces two main challenges: (i) combining multiple sources into a coherent narrative requires identifying cross-paper relationships and synthesizing their content, and (ii) the desired organization and emphasis varies substantially across presenters, making personalization essential for user satisfaction. Motivated by these challenges, we propose KeyDeck, the first multi-PTSG framework that integrates per-paper and cross-paper analysis to identify relationships among sources and synthesize their contributions into a coherent narrative, and reference-guided personalization to infer presenters' narrative, content, textual, and visual preferences and adapt slide generation accordingly. To evaluate generation quality, personalization adherence, and conversational goal satisfaction, we introduce KeyBench, a benchmark of 100 expert-authored multi-paper presentations, together with a comprehensive evaluation framework. Our results demonstrate that KeyDeck outperforms existing approaches in multi-PTSG quality, personalization adherence, and conversational goal satisfaction.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and LLM based agents have expanded the capabilities of AI glasses, enabling egocentric visual question answering, memory retrieval, and proactive assistance. These applications involve diverse inputs, including speech, images, and video, and range from simple device control to complex visual reasoning, posing challenges for efficient computation and communication. While video centric capabilities have received considerable attention, simple text based and device executable tasks remain underexplored despite their prevalence in daily interactions. Efficiently supporting diverse workloads within a unified assistant remains an open challenge. We propose JOVE ICE, an intent guided cloud, edge, and device collaborative framework that routes requests among AI glasses, a paired smartphone, and cloud servers according to user intent. JOVE ICE integrates hierarchical intent classification with dynamic subagent orchestration to select efficient execution paths and flexibly incorporate new applications through specialized subagents. Analysis of real world AI glasses requests reveals that simple device control tasks account for over 30% of interactions, which JOVE ICE handles directly on device to avoid unnecessary cloud computation and costs. Evaluations on three public benchmarks demonstrate that JOVE ICE reduces time to first token by approximately 13% and token usage by over 11% compared with conventional device and cloud architectures, while maintaining task quality. These results demonstrate the potential of intent guided collaboration to improve the efficiency and scalability of AI glasses.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World models predict what happens under an action, but choosing between actions requires comparing their outcomes in the same physical situation. Factual transitions alone determine action-wise marginal futures and average effects, but not individual effects or the probability that switching actions causes harm. We introduce WhatIf, a counterfactual world model for manipulation that couples candidate futures through shared latent physics and transition noise inferred from factual history. Each candidate action induces a sparse object-factor causal graph describing the physical pathways it activates. Rolling out these graphs under the shared situation anchors predictions to observed outcomes and confines action contrasts to the objects each action reaches. Shared type-conditioned functions support varying numbers of objects, while a round-trip loss enforces action-invariant abduction without counterfactual labels. We show that replacing an individual effect with its conditional mean incurs mean-squared error equal to its conditional variance, motivating comparisons between coupled futures. Building on these comparisons, Counterfactual Advantage Planning (CAP) ranks plans by their advantage over an incumbent within shared situations and constrains the probability of harm. Paired advantages reduce estimator variance by a factor of $1-ρ$, where $ρ$ is the correlation between branch outcomes, and cancel model errors shared by both branches. Each executed transition provides hindsight advantages to update the physics posterior, the next plan, and an amortized policy without a learned critic. We further establish when observational transitions identify interventional dynamics. Paired simulator interventions evaluate counterfactual accuracy, graph recovery, compositional generalization, and decision quality.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can write and repair RTL, but successful compilation or testbench execution does not establish defect removal, behavioral preservation, security, or implementation feasibility. Existing LLM-based RTL repair methods often report a single repair rate without retaining separate evidence for these properties, limiting reproducible comparison and integration into hardware design flows. We present REFORGE, which, to our knowledge, is the first LLM-assisted RTL repair framework that maintains and accumulates structured repair evidence across iterations, allowing it to generate fixes that satisfy multiple objectives simultaneously, including functionality, behavioral equivalence, security, and cost efficiency. REFORGE profiles buggy RTL, identifies structural and security-relevant features, localizes failing behavior, and uses verification-guided diagnosis to generate complete RTL repair variants. Each variant undergoes syntax checking, simulation, formal analysis, off-trigger preservation, security re-scan, and synthesis-based power, performance, and area analysis. We implement REFORGE as a backend-independent repair-and-verification flow and evaluate it in 288 runs across three LLM backends and three benchmark suites. On CirFix, the proposed context and diagnosis increase runs delivering a checked repair from 32/63 to 51/63, while every configuration repairs the Hack@DAC OpenTitan defect. Evidence records also show that a testbench-only criterion reports 3.9 times more repairs than the full set of applicable checks, security-clean outputs may lack functional verification, and different RTL repairs can implement equivalent hardware. These results show that LLM-generated RTL repairs require explicit multi-stage verification evidence rather than a single pass/fail outcome.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Harness evolution offers a path to improving coding agents without updating model weights. However, existing methods can overfit their evolution benchmarks, producing gains that fail to carry over to new tasks. To address this challenge, we introduce Transferable Harness Evolution (TransHarness), which evolves harness intervention policies from same-task success-failure contrasts. These policies couple execution-context triggers with diagnostic guidance, enabling task-dependent checks and corrections beyond the source executions. Cross-task replay and independent semantic selection retain policies that intervene selectively and ground corrections in current-task evidence rather than source-specific solutions. We further curate an independent evolution set for cross-distribution transfer and conduct a cross-benchmark evaluation covering multiple strong models and a range of lightweight and mature harnesses. Experiments show that, with only 300 source trajectories from outside the test distribution, TransHarness further improves strong agents across distinct target benchmarks, demonstrating transferable harness evolution beyond benchmark overfitting. Specifically, TransHarness achieves state-of-the-art scores of 76.4% on DeepSWE v1.1 and 95.5% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, surpassing the strongest baseline model, GPT-6 Astra, by 2.4 and 5.6 percentage points, respectively.

Abstract (英文): Optimizing indoor climate systems plays a critical role in achieving the United Nations' Sustainable Development Goals 11 and 13. However, indoor delayed thermodynamic responses and partial observability severely hinder existing reinforcement learning (RL) methods, which are primarily limited by implicit thermal inertia, occupancy dynamic prediction, and cumulative prediction errors during long-horizon rollouts. Thus, we propose a novel occupant-centric indoor climate control framework. It develops indoor environmental world models to predict future physical thermal baselines and dynamic occupancy, explicitly capturing latent thermal inertia. By formulating adaptive regulation as counterfactual advantage learning against future environments via a Branching Dueling Q-Network, and integrating a Held-Action TD-λ target, it effectively mitigates delayed credit assignment. Additionally, a proposed physics-aligned smoothness regularization suppresses high-frequency actuator fluctuations, potentially extending equipment service life. Experiments on SemiBuildingSim and Sinergym demonstrate that our method achieves a superior multi-objective trade-off. Compared to the best-performing methods, it further reduces energy consumption by 11.2%, occupant discomfort by 44.2%, and action fluctuation by 47.3%, achieving better sample efficiency and cross-region effectiveness. This interdisciplinary study offers an energy-efficient, occupant-centric AI solution for control science, building science, and energy sustainability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diagnosis of congenital heart disease (CHD) requires understanding the spatial relationships between cardiac structures, which clinicians assess by scrolling through CT or cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) slices and rotating 3D reconstructions. Although automated methods have advanced cardiac segmentation, identifying congenital heart defects from volumetric scans remains an open challenge. The two existing approaches rely on handcrafted diagnostic rules limited to static anatomical abnormalities. We introduce CHD-Bench, the first benchmark for interactive CHD diagnosis, defining a multi-label task and evaluation protocol over 247 CT and MRI scans from three cohorts covering 38 CHD types. We also present CHD-Harness, open-source software that runs a VLM in a 3D Slicer environment through 22 tools, manages each examination, and records the resulting diagnosis and supporting views. We evaluate off-the-shelf medical, open weight, and closed weight VLMs and compare them with a 3D CNN classifier and published rule-based results. Gemini 4 Argon with CHD-Harness achieves the highest macro F1 across all three cohorts (0.63, 0.63, and 0.43). Allowing for interactive examination raises its macro F1 by up to 20 points over static views. These results establish CHD-Bench and CHD-Harness as a foundation for developing and evaluating VLMs for interactive CHD diagnosis.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Objective: To develop and validate InterGenEHR$^+$, an interactive framework for natural language-conditioned generation of synthetic tabular electronic health records (EHRs) and longitudinal trajectories for scenario-based evaluation of machine learning models.
  Methods: An optional large language model (LLM) parser converts clinical requests into structured constraints. Conditional variational autoencoders and diffusion models generate single-time-point records, while diffusion models generate longitudinal trajectories. We evaluate parsing, distributional fidelity, imputation, clinical-constraint adherence, mortality prediction, and clinical plausibility using a multi-institutional cohort of 874 patients receiving venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO).
  Results: Gemma 3 4B with few-shot prompting achieves the highest parsing performance with a low spurious slot rate. IGE-TabDDPM and IGE-PMVAE perform well in single-time-point generation; IGE-CSDI, IGE-FGTI, and IGE-MTSCI lead on different longitudinal fidelity metrics. Across five random seeds and three classifiers, synthetic cohorts retain predictive utility for mortality prediction on real test data. Clinician ratings support trajectory plausibility and alignment with requested scenarios.
  Conclusion: InterGenEHR$^+$ supports clinician-specified generation of EHR trajectories with statistical fidelity and downstream predictive utility in the evaluated VV-ECMO cohort. Extension to other clinical settings requires further validation.
  Significance: The framework supports scenario-based evaluation of machine learning models in data-sparse critical care settings.

Abstract (英文): We present ExtractBench Pro, a schema-guided document extraction benchmark that tests six challenging modes of extraction underrepresented in existing datasets. It is created following a framework for synthetic document generation, in which deterministic document generators assemble challenging components into documents that are designed to be difficult at a level calibrated for frontier systems. We synthesize 100 realistic documents spanning a diverse range of styles. On this data, we evaluate ten coding agents and eight extraction API configurations for accuracy, grounding, and their ability to perfectly extract documents. No extraction system extracts more than 61% of documents error-free. The strongest general-purpose coding agents, Claude Code Opus 5.5 and Codex GPT-6 Astra, outperform specialized extraction APIs on both perfect-document rate and Value F1, but at a higher cost per page. Among specialized extraction APIs, LlamaExtract spans the lower-cost frontier, with Agentic Plus Max Effort approaching the strongest coding agent on both perfect-document rate and Value F1 at lower cost per page. Our analysis of coding agent traces identifies intelligent image processing and programmatic reasoning, but also failures in applying document-specific definitions and recovering relationships from diagrams. We release ExtractBench Pro and these evaluation results as resources for the future development of improved frontier extraction systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-improvement is now a practical recipe for embodied policies: a policy collects experience, is retrained, and is kept when a judge rates it higher. However, the judge is usually trained or chosen inside the same loop, so its evidence is not independent of the policy. We introduce SealedLoop, an evaluation framework that adds a sealed judge: an evaluator outside the loop that the improver cannot see or optimize against. A new policy is kept only when a sequential test on the sealed judge confirms the gain, with a bounded probability of keeping a worse one. Running the same loop under each judge, we separate two sources of a false gain, which we call judge error (the loop's judge is wrong on its own data) and transfer gap (a real gain there does not carry over to unseen conditions). We evaluate on LIBERO manipulation, NAVSIM driving, and a full-scale real vehicle. In manipulation, a success classifier accepted 65 of 196 failures in one generation. In driving, policies that gain on the loop's own frames lose in an unseen city, so even an exact in-loop judge would keep 8/15 of them. When the sealed test decides, a driving loop built to improve ends with a better policy than under its own critic (0.674 against 0.649 PDMS, from 0.638), and on driving pairs with known sealed gain the test accepted 11 of 12 real gains and none of 59 losses. In a real-world field experiment on a published improvement step, the reinforced version drove further into a curve than the version it started from in all 6 blind pairs, though neither completed it. These results show that evidence from inside the loop, however accurate, is not enough to decide whether a policy has improved. SealedLoop offers a foundation for trustworthy self-improvement of embodied policies, in which progress is judged by evidence the improving system cannot influence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Barrier certificates provide formal safety guarantees for neural network controlled systems by ensuring that system trajectories avoid unsafe regions over time. However, after deployment, previously unseen obstacles or constraint changes may introduce new unsafe regions, invalidating the original certificate and potentially leading to unsafe behavior. We address this setting with adaptive piecewise barrier certificates, which preserve the original certificate and policy in unchanged regions while introducing localized updates around newly emerged unsafe regions. The central question is how to perform such updates efficiently during system execution without retraining from scratch. Our key idea is to cast deployment-time adaptation as a localized certificate repair problem and reduce it to an optimization task that leverages the learned structure of previous barrier certificates, enabling fast updates without global retraining. We further design a staged repair framework that progressively transitions from localized repair to joint policy-certificate updates and, when necessary, localized retraining. Empirical results on four benchmarks with five new obstacles show that our method succeeds in all cases and completes within tens of seconds on average, whereas retraining from scratch fails in most cases. On instances where retraining succeeds, our method is 17.3$\times$ faster on average. It incurs only 16.2% performance degradation relative to the original policy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Sign language generation (SLG) translates spoken-language text into pose sequences that convey its meaning. Recently, flow-based models with optimal transport (OT) have become a strong SLG baseline by handling the one-to-many nature of text-to-sign. While classifier-free guidance (CFG) could be applied to further strengthen the semantic alignment theoretically, its training involves conditional and unconditional training, which may contradict the pose-space OT, thus reducing the generation quality. Under the condition dropout, one noise sample can be paired with target motions exhibiting incompatible motion dynamics, interfering with the semantic guidance when the text condition is present. To address this issue, we propose Spectral Optimal Transport (SOT), which augments the pose-space cost with a spectral feature distance, reweighting the coupling toward the temporal-frequency structure of the target motions, thereby improving the performance when using CFG. The quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI dose-prediction models generate patient-specific 3D dose distributions from patient anatomy, providing an important intermediate representation for automated radiotherapy planning. However, an AI-predicted dose must undergo downstream optimization to produce a physically constrained treatment plan, a process that requires balancing competing planning objectives. We present RadOncGym, an agent-based framework that enables an LLM agent to iteratively tune 12 planning-objective weights for dose-mimicking optimization. To reduce iterative evaluation cost, RadOncGym incorporates a surrogate verifier that provides rapid feedback while allowing the agent to decide when to request a full planning solve within a fixed solve budget. We evaluate RadOncGym on 60 OpenKBP head-and-neck patients, each paired with 10 predictions from OpenKBP-Opt dose-prediction sets, yielding 600 patient-prediction contexts per experimental configuration. Under RadOncGym's 16-turn setting with a three-solve budget, a GPT-5.6 Sol agent reduces mean clinical-criterion violation by 0.6131 Gy and improves DVH and dose scores by 0.5986 and 0.2204 Gy relative to leaving all 12 weights fixed at 1, outperforming all evaluated non-agent weight-tuning methods under the same solve budget. It remains competitive with the 16-solve variants of these methods while requiring 75.0-77.6% less normalized runtime. RadOncGym demonstrates the potential of LLM agents as optimization planners in downstream radiotherapy plan optimization and provides a public testbed for their systematic evaluation. Code and configurations are available at https://github.com/miaot-usc/RadOncGym.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reusing past experience in long-horizon tasks requires more than storing successful procedures: Language agents must distinguish which procedures apply and what work remains as execution progresses. We introduce MetaDecomp, an in-context meta-learning framework for adaptive task decomposition that jointly learns a task-relative state space and reusable procedures. Its outer loop builds procedural memory from successful training executions, representing task progress as abstract states and procedures between states as directed acyclic graphs of operations and dependencies. As new progress distinctions are learned, MetaDecomp revisits historical execution evidence to reassess state assignments and reorganize the procedures they support. With memory fixed at test time, the inner loop refines an initial decomposition and revises it from environmental feedback, combining applicable procedure fragments with task-specific steps missing from memory. Evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld over three seeds, MetaDecomp achieves the highest mean performance among the compared methods in four of five settings, for example, an 86.3% success rate on ALFWorld with Qwen3-4B. Fixing the initial schema or omitting historical reprojection reduces performance in component ablations. MetaDecomp also uses fewer inference tokens than ReAct and AutoRefine in all five settings. Cross-benchmark analysis identifies a low-performance area where partial coverage degrades success relative to hidden memory; high coverage improves success, while low coverage changes little. Code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are becoming general readers of medical images, yet they remain weak at locating small abnormalities. Brain metastases expose this gap: at 384 px a 0.1 mL lesion covers less than a quarter of one visual token, and at one false positive per scan frontier MLLMs find fewer than 30% of lesions of 0.1-1 mL. Fine-tuning on one dataset helps but transfers poorly, and improving it further is slow trial and error steered by a single score. We introduce LANTERN (Lesion-finding AgeNt Through Error-driven Recursive learNing), a framework in which an MLLM improves recursively from its own misses. LANTERN recasts model development as an error-driven loop: a failure census attributes every missed lesion to one of four causes, an LLM mentor revises one of four levels (evidence acquisition, visual representation, learning policy, decision aggregation) per round, and a rule frozen before the first round decides what is kept, without test data and with every tool call audited. Over 15 unattended rounds, LANTERN raises a fine-tuned 4B MLLM from finding 75% to 94% of lesions at one false positive per scan on BraTS-MET, and 91% of those of 0.1-1 mL, compared with at most 51% and 29% for five frontier MLLMs read under the starting protocol; the gains hold on an external cohort (89%) and on unseen meningiomas (77%). These results show that an MLLM's own misses, attributed to their causes, can drive its reliable recursive self-improvement.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a guardrail rejects an LLM agent's action, its message reaches any attacker observing the trajectory: a fixed refusal leaves benign users unable to recover, whereas an explanation can tell the attacker what to try next. We study this as safe disclosure: explaining rejections so that authorized tasks recover without increasing adaptive attack success. Safe disclosure depends on both the decision and the message: what the guardrail blocks and what it reveals to the agent and the attacker. Because hand-written rules alone cannot anticipate how adaptive attackers probe decisions or use messages, we introduce FORK, which co-evolves a guardrail's natural-language decision and disclosure skills with an attacker that reads every public message. Each exploited message and permissive decision gives FORK evidence for a gradient-free revision of its natural-language skill library. We evaluate FORK on four benchmarks. Averaged over three agents on FORK-World, FORK cuts attack success rate (ASR) under adaptive attack from 78.4% to 14.1% and keeps utility above 85%, outperforming baselines on both ASR and utility. Notably, on MT-AgentRisk, whose environments and tasks are unseen in training, FORK lowers ASR from 70.6% to 9.1% with 93.1% benign utility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical reasoning agents combine large language model (LLM) reasoning with clinical workflows and specialized neural tools. However, general-purpose LLMs receive limited training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty and distributional shifts of neural tool outputs make selecting tools from simple descriptions significantly more challenging than in deterministic settings such as calculators or dictionary lookups. In this paper, we propose EATS, an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that leverages accumulated tool-use trajectories to improve tool selection in medical reasoning agents. EATS constructs a memory of past interactions, including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback, and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries. As memory construction does not require ground-truth supervision, EATS scales naturally with test-time experience. We evaluate EATS on tool-augmented medical reasoning tasks, including closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation. Experiments on four public datasets, ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, and SLAKE-VQA, across three multimodal LLMs, Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, and GPT-5.5, show consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. Code is available at https://github.com/Limingyuan001/EATS.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical reasoning agents combine foundation models with specialized tools to interpret medical images, yet reusing their historical experience remains challenging. A correct final answer can conceal flawed reasoning or tool use, synthesized advice may lack source support, and superficially similar cases may require different diagnostic procedures. In this paper, we propose EGSE, an evidence-grounded skill evolution framework that distills historical dialogues from similar cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through supported and validated updates to improve medical agents. EGSE evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use and contrasts repeated successful and failed rollouts to extract useful practices and evidence-supported avoidance guidance. These experiences are synthesized into workflows with applicability conditions and checks traceable to source dialogues. Library additions and merges require source-grounded review and execution validation. At inference time, EGSE routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then retrieves and selects applicable workflows based on their conditions, the current images, and the question to guide evidence acquisition and interpretation. Experiments across four chest X-ray benchmarks show that EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. It also delivers consistent accuracy gains across seven foundation models and supports skill transfer across datasets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent skills are directories of instructions, reference materials, and scripts loaded on demand by coding agents. According to SkillsBench, although human-curated skills can raise the passing rate by about 16.6 percentage points (pp) on average, they have actually reduced the results for some tasks. Later research has started to gate or eliminate skills according to the above per-task gains. We have audited the above benchmark at the source-code level and analysed 7,554 public trials to form 1,421 paired "(configuration, task) cells". Source-code audit identified two controlled-verified cases of grader dependence and one operational definition gap; the average lift on these three tasks was 74.6 pp, and the overall average lift for the full set was 18.7 pp. The audit also found that four tasks contained placeholder skill directories, and a certain execution harness additionally added 48 built-in skills in most runs. No task-level reduction achieved statistical significance in the pooled reanalysis with multiple-testing correction. As there were at most three runs per condition for each cell, the observed negative differences are very susceptible to run-sampling noise, and arithmetically, 58% of the cells could not have shown a decrease. Therefore, the raw numbers of negative cells cannot show that skills cause harm. A gating strategy based on the skill-free score seems to improve by 2-3 pp in the logs; however, even after shuffling condition labels to remove any skill effect, it still achieves an increase of 4-7 pp. On the held-out runs not used for fitting and decision-making, there was no statistically significant improvement. The audit ledger and analysis scripts have been published by us.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automated decision systems, from learned policies to model-based optimizers, often minimize costs such as energy or resource use while meeting a service requirement, for example the quality of service (QoS) specified in a service level agreement (SLA), a comfort band, or a safety margin. The requirement can be scalarized with the costs into a single objective, with a fixed or an adaptively tuned (Lagrangian) weight, or it can be imposed as a constraint. We study this choice in the Smart Buildings Challenge, in which a single policy controls the heating, ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) of previously unseen buildings and is evaluated by a weighted sum of comfort, energy cost and carbon emissions. With all other components fixed, we compare the two treatments in model-based optimization, represented by model predictive control (MPC) with the requirement as a hard or a soft (l1-penalized) constraint, and in learned policies, represented by proximal policy optimization (PPO) with a fixed or a Lagrangian penalty in the reward or a projection layer on the action. In MPC, the soft constraint reproduces the hard one once its weight exceeds the requirement's shadow price, which the challenge's comfort weight exceeds approximately fourfold; below this weight, comfort is sacrificed, and under a metric that values comfort below its shadow price the soft constraint outperforms the hard one by up to 28%. A weight tuned on the training buildings is 3.9% worse than the hard constraint on 48 unseen buildings, because the shadow price varies across buildings. In PPO, the projected policy satisfies the requirement from the outset and leads early in training; with longer training, the Lagrangian policy overtakes it while the projected policy deteriorates, and the fixed penalty does not learn to satisfy the requirement.

</details>

Abstract (英文): There are several approaches to AI-driven mathematical discovery. One of these involves the use of large language models (LLMs) to resolve well-specified conjectures via text-based reasoning, while another uses narrow models and interpretability for more open-ended, human-in-the-loop exploration using object-based mathematical data. The latter is limited in its ability to move beyond simple empirical pattern detection while the former is not well-designed for direct, open-ended exploration of large mathematical datasets. In this paper we introduce PatternMiner, a system that combines these two paradigms. In PatternMiner, an LLM agent generates mathematical datasets based on an initial broad question, trains narrow machine learning models on them, uses interpretability techniques to extract patterns from these trained models, and uses these patterns to formulate conjectures. Once an initial conjecture has been formulated, PatternMiner instructs the agent to search for counterexamples using calls to mathematical software. If a set of counterexamples can be found, these are used as feedback for conjecture refinement or another round of training and interpretability. The conjecture progressively evolves through this iterative sequence with the agent allowed to try to prove it at any point. We describe the results of applying PatternMiner to five well-known positivity problems in algebraic combinatorics. PatternMiner proves several new theorems related to Schubert positivity and projection, a character-row evaluation formula, a restricted version of the Kazhdan--Lusztig combinatorial invariance conjecture, and Schur positivity for a family of immanant coefficients. We end by analyzing the most common routes to conjecture generation that are used by PatternMiner in our case studies as well as documenting the most common failure modes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A speaker who asserts honestly may later discover that its assertion was false. Such erroneous truths are not lies, yet they raise the question of whether, and on which moral grounds, the speaker must retract them. Using the FIPA-ACL performative disconfirm, we extend a belief-based framework that evaluates speech acts under deontologism and consequentialism to the case of retraction. We prove that the honesty duty of deontologism, which bears on utterances when they are made, never requires retracting an erroneous truth, and add a persistent one, which forbids letting the addressee believe what the speaker no longer believes. On a legal-advice case implemented in Answer Set Programming, we show that deontologism and consequentialism both require the retraction, but that consequentialism no longer does once it comes too late to prevent harm.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Few-shot adaptation transfers pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to new domains using a small set of labeled images. Most methods fit prompts, representations, or classifiers within an adaptation procedure specified in advance. Yet the visual distinctions needed for recognition can differ substantially across domains, motivating the question: Can an LLM agent use few-shot examples and model observations to design the adaptation procedure itself? We propose Agentic Model Editing (AME), an agentic framework that jointly designs input transformations, encoder edits, feature computation, and classifier fitting as an executable, domain-customized adaptation program. The agent inspects images, class semantics, model parameters, intermediate representations, and activations to formulate hypotheses about the target task. It implements and tests candidate programs on the support set, with aggregate validation accuracy guiding refinement and selection. Each program fits a complete domain model that is fixed before testing. The agent and its tools are shared across domains, while each domain receives its own adaptation program. Across eleven datasets with CLIP ViT-B/16, AME achieves the highest average accuracies of 81.2% without back-propagation and 81.9% with back-propagation among the compared methods. Further experiments demonstrate applicability across VLM backbones and LLM agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Ad platforms and marketplaces show bidders a bid landscape, the estimated chance of winning at each candidate bid, and increasingly let agents built on reasoning language models choose the bid. We give six open reasoning models of 4B to 14B parameters such landscapes, with win curves from eBay closing prices and from a simulated advertising market (AuctionNet), and score their bids on held-out auctions against an exact calculator. Because the agent is handed the estimates, the lost value can be split by cause. Two failures belong to the models. They pass over the best option even when the values written in their own trace already rank it first, and their wrong answers lean toward the highest bid on the menu. The lean is about the bid's size, not its place in the list, and it survives sampling. At 768 forced tokens on eBay, under pay-your-bid pricing, the wrong answers cost bidders 7-17% of the surplus the calculator's bids earn. Arithmetic errors are rare. Two further losses come from operator choices. A token cap cuts traces short: allowed to stop on their own within 4,096 tokens, five models recover 91-100% of the calculator's lead over a model that does no reasoning. Asking for the answer while the reasoning block is still open misreads it: closing the block first lifts the weakest model from 42% to 84% on eBay and from 24% to 81% on AuctionNet. Finally, an audit that flags bids disagreeing with the trace's own numbers, and overrides them with the calculator's bid, raises the share of the calculator's lead recovered at 768 forced tokens from 42-85% to 84-100% on eBay under nested cross-validation, and the same reader settings transfer from eBay to AuctionNet.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Executing monolithic neural weights, frontier foundation models, or entire multi-agent workflows uniformly on every input incurs substantial computational, latency, and monetary overhead. Conditional computation mitigates this cost by deciding dynamically which internal experts, model endpoints, or specialized agents to invoke per request. Yet token-level gating in sparse neural architectures, query-level model cascades, and task-level multi-agent coordination have largely progressed in disconnected research silos, obscuring recurring system failures and transferable fixes. In this systematic review, we synthesize 2,289 studies published between 2017 and September 2026 (selected from 13,884 unique records) across 20 methodological families under a unified two-step inspect-then-allocate framework. Discrete hard selection accounts for 70%, 83%, and 91% of token-, query-, and task-scale literature respectively, and computational overhead appears as a top operational bottleneck across every layer. Engineering safeguards remain sharply asymmetric: intra-layer gating prioritizes load balancing while rarely incorporating uncertainty estimation, whereas model selection and agent orchestration rely on confidence thresholds and budget rules while confronting miscalibration and cascading errors. At the same time, publication volume is accelerating fastest in agentic task delegation, where routing mechanisms are least often isolated experimentally and methodological bias risk is highest.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent harnesses today manage context on the model's behalf: when the window fills, the harness triggers a compaction that summarizes the history. We study the alternative, \emph{model-driven} context management, in which the model itself decides when and what to compact through a context API. We gave models control over their own context in \nooa, NVIDIA's open source agent harness, and compared the two strategies on document research and machine learning engineering across context budgets from 8K to 512K tokens. Models that use the context API effectively reach higher accuracy, often with fewer input tokens, especially at tight budgets: \textbf{GPT 6.1 nearly triples its score at a 32K budget} on OfficeQA Pro v2 (21\%~$\rightarrow$~56\%), and \textbf{Kimi gains with up to 41\% fewer input tokens}. Models differ widely, however, in how often they use the API, how aggressively they compact, and when they start compacting, and prompt changes shift that timing. Finally, we show that \textbf{inference engines can make model-driven edits cheaper}: by reusing the KV cache of the unchanged suffix after an edit, \textbf{we cut prefill compute by up to 15\% per request with no measurable change in accuracy}. Together, these results point to a new co-design frontier: models trained to manage their own context, served by inference engines that support KV cache editing.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Providers watermark the outputs of large language models, while applications sample many responses per prompt for self-consistency, best-of-n selection, and uncertainty estimation. We show that deployed configurations of distortion-free watermarks, which deduplicate keyed contexts only within a response, couple the responses of a request: answer templates make all responses query the watermark with the same context before the answer, so collisions across responses, usually assumed rare, become the norm. Key pools reduce or avoid the coupling at the cost of multiple testing, and the exact remedy of a context history shared across the request leaves many positions unwatermarked. We propose SOLO, which keeps a request-scoped context history but anchors its keyed contexts on content selected by min-hash, so that siblings rarely compete for a context and random edits stay local. Its responses are jointly distributed as independent unwatermarked responses, each detectable with a single key. On five language models from three families and three benchmarks, SOLO preserves self-consistency and uncertainty estimation and, at a 1% false positive rate, detects more responses than four-token key pools on most tasks, also after randomly editing 10% of the tokens, and more than the context history on most clean tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/ZhongliangGuo/solo-watermark.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When LLM agents verify each other's work over a long interaction, they drift into collusion: they come to accept work without the evidence that the verification protocol requires. Collusion is absent at the start and builds up episode by episode, which we trace to accurate feedback accumulating in memory. An agent that follows the rule is told that its verdict was wrong whenever its peer was right, so breaking the rule is the reward-maximizing choice, and agents talk themselves into it in their private reflections. Existing mitigations leave the agents unchanged; we train the behavior out. PROVE (Protocol Reinforcement from Verified Evidence) is a multi-agent reinforcement learning method that rewards verdicts by the evidence behind them rather than by their outcome, and credits every call an agent makes, including the calls that solve its own task, with the outcome that call controls. On Qwen3.6-27B, a LoRA adapter trained on six-episode interactions takes the collusion rate on held-out task sequences at the original ten-episode horizon from 65.0% to 1.0%, with no detectable change in task success. Collusion that converged in every interaction now converges in none, and the gain holds four episodes beyond the longest interaction seen in training. The trained 27B model colludes less than every model evaluated in the original study, closed-weight frontier models included. Code is available at https://github.com/Haochen-Luo/PROVE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. We rebuild a dated biomedical knowledge graph as it stood at the end of each year from 2020 up to its newest version. We then ask six open language models, with and without this graph, which targets will enter a first trial for a disease the next year. Without retrieval, the models rank about as well as trial history, a count of each target's past trials in the same therapeutic area, also after their training cutoffs. Evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, before or after the models' training cutoffs. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. Evaluations of target prioritisation should date both features and labels, with evidence from before the decision and outcomes after it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Shapley values in reinforcement learning are used to explain which observation features drive an agent's behavior and expected return, but not its certainty. Explaining certainty requires understanding how observation features influence different sources of return uncertainty, and Shapley values need the agent's prediction for every subset of visible features. We propose EXPOSURE, which uses one mask-aware belief network for both action selection and Shapley explanations. It explains the variance of the return, split into an observational part due to missing features and an inherent part due to the environment's own randomness, and the entropy of its belief over the masked features. In tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper, where exact Shapley values are computable, EXPOSURE gives more accurate explanations of expected return and its uncertainty, while uniquely explaining belief uncertainty.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) with general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offers a flexible alternative to task-specific navigation policies, while reliable long-horizon execution remains challenging. Agents must track navigation progress, detect discrepancies between commanded and actual motion, and recognize when movement has passed relevant landmarks or entrances. We present Navigent, an agentic VLN framework built around a frozen MLLM and a Geometry-Grounded Execution Critic (GEC). GEC performs two complementary checks: action-to-motion consistency, which evaluates measured displacement against commanded movement, and motion-to-landmark consistency, which tracks agent-selected visual anchors and entrance candidates in world coordinates. Detected discrepancies interrupt the remaining action sequence and trigger reobservation, local adjustment, or node-based recovery. Historical visual memory supports progress tracking across extended routes. An offline self-improvement procedure further analyzes successful and failed training trajectories to refine navigation prompts and GEC logic while keeping model weights fixed. On the R2R-CE validation-unseen split (1,839 episodes) and RxR-CE validation-unseen split(3,669 episodes), Navigent achieves 84.0%/86.0% SR and 72.9%/69.1% SPL, respectively, demonstrating the value of geometry-grounded execution verification for reliable agentic navigation. Project Page: https://marslab-navigent.github.io/navigent.github.io/ Code: https://github.com/MarsLab-Navigent/Navigent

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) perform well on chemistry knowledge benchmarks, which mainly assess question answering and isolated tasks. Chemical research, however, requires using specialized tools and interpreting their outputs to obtain chemically valid results. To address this gap, we introduce Crucible Bench to evaluate this ability across all four stages of the design-make-test-analyze (DMTA) cycle. The benchmark contains 1,078 tasks in 12 task families curated from 18 published datasets, covering molecular design, synthesis planning, experimental execution, closed-loop optimization, simulation, and analytical characterization. Agents access 75 domain-specific tools through the Model Context Protocol in seven isolated environments. Sandboxed graders verify the resulting synthesis routes, experimental protocols, and other outputs. Laboratory tasks use robotic simulators or previously measured experimental data. We evaluate 14 LLMs as agents and find that the best achieves an overall score of 0.545, with synthesis planning and closed-loop optimization emerging as the hardest domains. Overall performance does not predict optimization performance well: the model ranked twelfth overall achieves the highest score in closed-loop optimization. Error analysis also shows that agents often submit chemically invalid results despite running their tools successfully. Stronger models benefit more from tool access, but reliable use of chemical software remains a challenge that Crucible Bench can track as models improve.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) outperform single-agent systems, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. While assigning a large LLM to every agent is common, it results in inefficient resource utilization, leaving the system far from the Pareto frontier. The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. Most existing routers treat cost only implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint. A fixed accuracy-cost preference also cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. We introduce MeterRouter, a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally synthesizes a collaboration graph by selecting each agent's role, LLM, incoming connections, and termination conditioned on the query and a per-query monetary budget. We initialize the router by imitating successful execution graphs, then jointly refine its decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) whose reward favors correct answers and penalizes spending and budget overruns. Across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, MeterRouter is (1) accurate, achieving performance comparable to the strongest MAS; (2) economical, reaching that accuracy at 82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router and extending the accuracy-cost frontier beyond the ceiling of single-agent systems; and (3) controllable, adapting its spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from passive chatbots into autonomous systems that operate continuously in dynamic environments. Such agents need not only solve current tasks but also learn from past interactions to improve future performance. However, existing streaming benchmarks often rely on short task sequences or largely independent tasks, providing limited evidence of whether agents can effectively reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{EvoLink-Bench}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolving agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams. It covers daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows, with persistent workspaces and dependencies across tasks. The benchmark includes two complementary levels: \textbf{L1}, which evaluates the acquisition and revision of language-expressible knowledge, and \textbf{L2}, which assesses the development of task-level capabilities through execution and feedback. Its evaluation further distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks. We evaluate five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, covering 19,635 scored task slots from selected evaluation cohorts. Results show that no framework consistently performs best across both levels, retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks, and persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Furthermore, the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting that these two forms of evolution require different learning mechanisms. Performance gains also vary across domains and task streams, with improvements on some tasks not necessarily extending to others.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We formalize Agentic Waterfall Feed, a new problem of personalized information discovery in which users actively direct recommendations and the system adapts to evolving needs inferred from both explicit requests and browsing behavior. We address this problem with the Agentic Waterfall framework, whose language-model controller maintains persistent user state and coordinates content retrieval, ranking, and state updates. To evaluate systems on this task, we contribute the Agentic Waterfall Dataset and a replay-based evaluation protocol. The dataset combines real content metadata with simulated interaction histories. The protocol compares systems under shared histories using relevance scores and blind judgments of how well recommendations satisfy current user goals and constraints. Agentic Waterfall remains competitive with strong baselines on conventional recommendation tasks across five public benchmarks. On the Agentic Waterfall Dataset, it outperforms the strongest baseline in both evaluations, with relative improvements of up to 16.1%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aviation accident narratives contain safety knowledge that is difficult to capture using structured occurrence fields alone. This study evaluates prompting-based knowledge extraction across four dimensions: concept accuracy, verbatim evidence grounding, structural compliance, and directed relation extraction. SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct was evaluated under zero-shot, one-shot, fixed few-shot, and retrieval-grounded prompting using 300 National Transportation Safety Board narratives from 2000-2020, containing 3,763 reference concepts and 467 directed relations. Evaluation combined structural checks, verbatim evidence matching, expert-reviewed semantic matching, and paired bootstrap confidence intervals. No prompting strategy performed best across all dimensions. Retrieval-grounded prompting achieved the highest concept micro-F1 of 32.27%, although its advantage over fixed few-shot prompting was uncertain (95% CI: -0.92 to 2.40 percentage points). One-shot prompting achieved the highest relation F1 of 6.95%, identifying directed relation extraction as the principal SmolLM2 bottleneck. Compared with fixed few-shot prompting, retrieval improved verbatim grounding (59.45% vs. 54.02%) but reduced complete structural compliance (33.3% vs. 42.7%). Verbatim matching establishes source presence rather than evidential relevance. An exploratory comparison using 35 benchmark narratives showed that Qwen3-8B achieved higher concept micro-F1 across all prompting conditions and higher relation F1 under demonstration-based prompting, reaching 51.37% and 34.62%, respectively, under one-shot prompting. The small subset limits generalization. These findings support evaluating semantic correctness, source traceability, structural reliability, and directed relation accuracy separately.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model agents are increasingly improved via agentic reinforcement learning, which can be on-policy but suffers from expensive rollouts and sparse rewards, limiting its application at test time. In this paper, we present a verbally Supervised Fine-Tuning (vSFT) framework that enables the conjunction of dense feedback in natural language and weight updates at test time. Specifically, our vSFT forms a closed loop between an agent and a supervisor via a hypernetwork, in which the supervisor generates verbal supervision from the agent's failed trajectories, and the hypernetwork converts that supervision into low-rank weight updates to tune the agent. We validate that this test-time in-parameter evolving process outperforms both its in-context counterparts and prevalent reinforcement learning methods. In particular, our vSFT using Qwen3-4B outperforms GRPO and Reflexion by 8.7% and 13.9% in success rate on ALFWorld, and by 4.8% and 18.2% on WebShop, respectively. We believe that learning how to adapt a model, rather than an adapted model, offers a promising path towards more capable agents that improve themselves.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI wearables, such as smart glasses, extend the context available to agents beyond digital environments to users' physical surroundings. With these devices, computer tasks can originate from users' ongoing activities rather than solely from explicit instructions. Through first-person observations, agents can identify objects and activities relevant to user requests and ground digital tasks in the surrounding context. We call this interaction paradigm Ego Use. However, first-person observations are limited by the wearer's viewpoint and may provide incomplete or ambiguous evidence of ongoing activities. As new observations arrive, agents may need to revise their understanding of the physical context and user intent, and reassess whether prior digital actions remain valid. Whether current agents can reliably handle these changes remains insufficiently evaluated. We introduce EgoUseBench, a benchmark for evaluating Ego Use across three interaction settings with different levels of first-person context. Computer-Use relies on explicit instructions, Offline Ego Use provides prerecorded first-person context before execution, and Online Ego Use provides continuous observations as user intent evolves. Based on over 74 hours of real-world first-person recordings, we construct 693 tasks across the three interaction settings. To capture task evolution, we track first-person observations, user requirements, and website state separately. We further group tasks by how information changes during execution, resulting in three types: Graft (revised user requirements), Refresh (new first-person observations), and Clarify (agent-initiated information seeking). We evaluate advanced models and find that average success rates in Online Ego Use are 26.3% lower than in Computer-Use. This result reveals the difficulty of keeping task execution aligned with evolving first-person context and user intent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-session hiring assistants must infer evolving recruiter preferences and decision patterns across projects and time. We introduce Open Knowledge Format (OKF)-indexed episodic memory to organize interactions and retrieve related events through their lineage. We also introduce Procedure Memory, which learns reusable decision procedures from these episodes through prompted reflection or reinforcement learning. We compare these approaches with no memory, full-history context, and conversation search, and evaluate Jev-class models for index navigation and GPT-5.6 Luna as a lower-cost alternative to GPT-5.4.
  In our evaluation, OKF-indexed retrieval achieves low-priority preference F1 scores of 0.93-0.95 at medium and high preference overlap between hiring projects. It significantly outperforms full-history context and conversation search in both settings, and no memory at high overlap, while using 69% fewer context tokens than full history. Procedure Memory reduces conversational turns by up to 39% relative to no memory. Qualitative and long-horizon evaluations provide evidence of temporal reasoning, with RL-learned procedures achieving 60% on both correct abstention and exact-field identification. Jev-class models improve conversational efficiency but more frequently miss temporally relevant evidence, highlighting the importance of evidence selection.
  On PersonaMem-v2, Procedure Memory with OKF retrieval achieves 50.2% accuracy versus 48.6% for full-history context and 29.0% for no memory, using 90.5% fewer reader input tokens than full history. GPT-5.6 Luna performs comparably to GPT-5.4 at lower cost. These findings highlight the value of structured episodic memory and learned decision procedures for efficient personalization and temporal reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent work on Weight-Space Programming uses LLMs to edit neural weights with forward passes only, conflating LLM scripting with the underlying algebra of forward-only editing. We show that for core structured editing regimes, an LLM optimizer is unnecessary, and introduce Forward Weight-Space Programming (FWSP): a deterministic pipeline combining probing, localization, stratified operators (O1-O4), automatic blind routing (83.3% vs. 33.3% for a 27B LLM with the same tool library), and paired verification with zero additional per-token computation. For reasoning LLMs, we propose Protected FisherGate, a closed-form covariance-whitened readout edit orthogonal to normal-reasoning states: on DeepSeek-R1-0528-8B, a single frozen math-calibrated patch achieves 84.2% HumanEval pass@1 (1K budget; 75.4 +/- 9.4% across 3/5 Stage-4-passing subsets), beating 512-token-reserved budget forcing by +14.6 pp (-23.4% tokens) while transferring across HF-to-vLLM backends on full GSM8K and MATH-500. Across five modalities and thirteen models (50K to 27B dense and FP8 MoE), we further prove and verify two operator boundaries, subspace entanglement and frozen representation span, where FWSP-localized LoRA bridges forward-only and gradient adaptation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The behavior of memory systems for long-running AI assistants is governed by a harness, which consists of the code, prompts, and configurations for memory construction, maintenance, retrieval, and answer generation. However, optimizing the harness of a memory system remains largely manual, and unrestricted editing alone does not ensure structural improvements. In this paper, we introduce HoMe, an evaluation-driven framework that iteratively evaluates candidate harnesses and derives each new candidate from any previously evaluated harness by editing any part of it. HoMe combines session-level memory versioning with execution traces to identify shared failure mechanisms across cases and localize them to the responsible sessions and harness components. It encourages early structural exploration through candidate gating and reduces evaluation cost through smoke tests, adaptive sampling, and the reuse of constructed memory. We validate HoMe with the open-source memory system ReMe on two large-scale long-horizon memory benchmarks, LongMemEval-S and BEAM. With six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark, after a full initial evaluation, HoMe discovers harnesses that improve end-task performance over the originally strong harness while consuming far fewer query-time tokens. The code is available at https://github.com/agentscope-ai/ReMe.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large pretrained language models provide capable policies for interactive tasks, but how much model capacity is necessary for a specialized language agent? We introduce ZeroHero, a framework for training small language agents (SLAs) from scratch. In each environment, a large language model acts as the expert, and we collect its successful trajectories. We pretrain each SLA, which has fewer than 50 million parameters, to predict the expert's responses given the interaction history. We then post-train the agent on its own interactions using proximal policy optimization (PPO). During post-training, reasoning alignment supplements environment rewards with expert supervision on the language the agent generates alongside its actions. We evaluate the agents in three multistep environments where acting requires both understanding and generating language. In Crafter, agents interpret textual observations and generate reasoning before acting. In WebShop, they translate shopping instructions into search queries. In Alfworld-Dialogue, they ask clarifying questions and use the answers to guide household actions. The agents approach expert performance on WebShop and surpass their respective demonstration experts on Crafter training rollouts and held-out Alfworld-Dialogue tasks. An interactive demo is available at https://zoyav.github.io/-ZoyaV-zerohero-demo.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autonomous research agents are emerging as a promising approach to scientific problem-solving across disciplines, owing to their growing ability to improve a solution through repeated experiments. Evaluating this ability requires benchmarks that pose open-ended research problems and measure improvements over established scientific methods. We introduce REE (Research Entrance Exam), a benchmark that tests AI agents on scientific research. It consists of 100 open-ended research problems spanning five broad scientific areas and 23 domains. To ensure high quality, every problem is drawn from active research in its field and individually reviewed by domain experts. Each problem is posed as a task of iterative improvement: an agent has up to 12 hours to improve a working starter solution, and REE scores the result on a common scale against an established expert solution evaluated under comparable conditions. By this measure, two of the four frontier agents we evaluate, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol, outperform the expert solutions on 86% and 81% of problems, across all five scientific areas. Their runs also show how they do so: both agents surpass the expert solutions within the first two hours on average, and their best solutions mostly tailor or combine established methods of the field. REE thus provides a unified framework for evaluating autonomous scientific research, capturing both improvements over established methods and the research processes behind them.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models rarely satisfy all behavioral requirements of a new platform, and teams without a large deployed fleet lack the data to address the resulting failures. We propose a failure-driven data flywheel for post-training such models. It constructs target scenarios absent from the available data by inserting task-relevant entities into reconstructed real-world scenes, and evaluates the model against each behavioral requirement of the target task. Failure rates form a failure map across task conditions and difficulty levels, which is re-evaluated after each round and assigns larger data generation quotas to conditions where the model still fails. We instantiate the framework in autonomous driving for occluded-pedestrian emergence and yielding at stop-sign-controlled intersections, and evaluate it on two VLA models and one non-VLA planner in simulation and on a real vehicle. For occluded-pedestrian emergence, five rounds of post-training improve the critical-phase collision-free rate on the sealed test set from 0.261 to 0.579 for AutoVLA and from 0.390 to 0.765 for ReCogDrive, while the unnecessary braking rate in paired control scenarios never exceeds 0.013. On a full-scale vehicle without real-world training, post-trained ReCogDrive achieves 4 of 5 safe passages, versus 1 of 5 for the base model. Fine-grained evaluation reveals a pattern obscured by the collision-free rate alone: both VLA models learn anticipatory deceleration before reaching the occluding vehicle, while braking after pedestrian emergence remains limited. For stop-sign yielding, the stop-before-line rate rises from 0.100 to 0.897 for AutoVLA and from 0.000 to 0.667 for ReCogDrive. With sequential training from the pedestrian-capability models, it reaches 0.599 and 0.358, respectively, while the pedestrian collision-free rate decreases by at most 0.032.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) continually introduce emerging entities whose interaction histories evolve from zero-shot debuts into sparse, bursty few-shot trajectories. However, current paradigms are fragmented: pattern-transfer methods lack catalog-wide event memory, whereas online memory models suffer from endpoint-role asymmetry that degrades unseen-answer ranking. Furthermore, sparse early interactions exhibit both long-range dispersion and local redundancy under skewed distribution. To address these challenges, we propose INSPIRE (Integrating Shared Patterns and Individual Evidence for Reasoning), a two-stage forecasting framework over a known catalog. In Stage I, INSPIRE learns an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor that fuses event-updated memory across the full candidate catalog while applying coordinated query- and catalog-side masking. Subsequently, Stage II freezes this predictor and optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter (EA) combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision with prior-odds debiasing and soft residual alignment. In addition, we decompose evaluation by endpoint roles and track longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts via query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics. Across four benchmarks based on the ICEWS and GDELT event datasets, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Relative to paired Stage I predictors, EA further increases Emerging MRR by 8.28%-32.63%, Answer-new MRR by 7.81%-41.24%, and cohort macro MRR by 9.64%-39.94%, gains attributed to semantic priors and candidate-level score competition suppressing mismatched established competitors without zero-support prior shortcuts. The official code is publicly available at https://github.com/KcAcoZhang/INSPIRE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mathematicians solve problems faster when they know the right theory. We ask how much a stated theory is worth to a large language model (LLM) solver, and whether a model can write that theory itself. In a length-matched protocol, adding the right theory to the context of Qwen3-14B raises pass@1 on pattern waiting-time problems from 0.37 to 0.83 and cuts the median tokens of correct solutions from 10.2k to 6.3k, while worked examples of the same length do not help. The gain shrinks as the solver and its token budget grow (+0.46, +0.26, +0.07), and at a long budget it comes almost entirely from finishing within budget: solutions that finish are correct 98--100\% of the time in every condition. To separate induction from recall, we plant unpublished theorems in custom probability families and measure how much of the oracle theory's gain an induced theory recovers. A strong open model, required to explore the training problems with code before writing, recovers 60\% of the gain on one planted family, 48\% on a second, and almost none on a third, where it finds the right form but not the condition that makes the form computable. No single induced note is complete, but a strong model that merges several notes recovers 61\% of the gain on the classic family, above the best single note (47\%). Theories are worth the reasoning they save, and models can find their pieces before they can assemble them.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents increasingly rely on external memory, yet they still hallucinate about what they remember. Often the correct memory is retrieved and placed in the prompt, but the answer contradicts it or the memory is applied to the wrong question. We call these use-time memory hallucinations. Across five open models, we find that they arise from the conflict between parametric knowledge and external memory and take three forms: memory-ignoring, where the model returns to a stale fact in its weights (up to 46% of queries); memory-misapplying, where it attributes the memory to unrelated entities (up to 24%, and 49% when retrieval is combined with weight editing); and version confusion, where 53% of answers about revised facts assert the superseded value when both versions remain stored. We propose TASK memory, which goes beyond text-only memory by combining a Text note with Activation Steering and Knowledge editing that share one in-model address. The text holds the content. A removable knowledge edit supplies the address through its subject key and supports multi-hop composition. A pair of steering vectors decides, query by query, whether the model adopts the memory or keeps its own answer; the vectors store no content and control how the text memory is used. On frozen models under a pre-registered, test-once protocol, TASK reduces memory-ignoring hallucinations to at most 1.5% in all ten model-dataset cells and cuts neighbour misuse from 39.2% to 7.5% (+10.8 pp composite on CounterFact). It improves a 300-memory library over BM25 RAG by 16.4 pp, an advantage that holds at 3,000 memories, resolves fact revisions without fact IDs, and adds 7.5 pp of multi-hop accuracy on MQuAKE. 21 of 23 cross-model comparisons (8B-27B, four families) are significant, and TASK beats three recent context-steering methods in 9 of 10 model-dataset cells.

</details>
